It’s just how they are, and you can rely on them to stay that way—and you don’t have to give up on your idea.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Fortunately, emotional stability exists on a continuum. Most people are adequately stable, while others have abundant emotional stability, characterized by empathy, rationality, emotional control, consistency, communication skills, and acumen for social interaction.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">These people are usually easy to predict and partner with, if your interests align with theirs. But not always. So, final strategy: Deepen your <i>own</i> emotional stability—and you might be surprised at how stable others become.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Those are the bare-bones basics of the <b>Six Signs for Behavior Prediction</b>—absolutely none of which I knew back in the dark days after 9/11, when I thought I knew a great deal about trust, but knew very little.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Luckily, I had some good teachers.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_40" title="40"/>January 5, 2002<br/>The End of History</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I headed toward my second meeting with Leo, driving slowly through the benign gauntlet of grieving people, with my car window rolled down, smelling the residue of burned wreckage that seemed as if it would forever foul the air of New York.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Emily, a volunteer from New Jersey, reached out from the sidewalk and handed me a bottle of Gatorade along with a note that said, “Thank you for saving America.” In my exhaustion and despair, Emily’s kindness made me sick with guilt. Every day, my work felt like a matter of life and death, and every night I knew I hadn’t achieved a <i>single damn thing</i> that could help <i>anybody.</i> I remember sitting in traffic, thinking: <i>My God!</i> <i>I’m totally useless!</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse’s pep talk had helped, but I hadn’t yet had time to test out his ideas.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was the low point in my career, and to a large extent at this time, my career was my life. After four years in the Bureau, preceded by five years in the Marine Corps and four years at the Naval Academy, I could no more save America than I could fly.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But the destruction of my dreams left me with nothing to lose, and my mindset shifted, as if on its own, and settled in a place that said it was acceptable to <i>help one person at a time.</i> It felt like neither capitulation nor epiphany. It just felt like a promise I’d made to myself deep in the past but had long forgotten in my dreams of grandeur.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I would start with Leo. If he was sober enough to accept it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He was, but he looked like he had a hangover—and was anxious to start working on his next one. The winter sun was plummeting, soot lay on the snow like a black blanket, and the dark, sour-smelling bar where we met felt like one more embodiment of my failure and America’s gloom. When I sat down, Leo’s face was so dark and creased with the history of his pain that I forgot about my rehearsed questions and blurted, “Is there something I can do for you?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_41" title="41"/>He didn’t say anything, and I didn’t either, because I just didn’t have the energy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Finally, he said slowly, “Do you know anything about visa extensions?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I nodded, and then I learned about the life of “The Lion,” Leo’s nickname in the Soviet secret police—due to his physical bulk and personal power. It was the only employment option he was offered, as a way to leave the postwar displacement gulag. He’d been forced to do things for the KGB that would fill any sane person with lifelong shame, and he vowed at the time to someday redeem his soul by risking everything for the cause of freedom, which he had so seldom experienced.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Trying to comprehend, I asked some dumb question about the mystery of survivor’s guilt, and with a look of terrible amusement at my naivete, he said, “It’s not because you survive, young man, but what you <i>do</i> to survive. A little manipulation for a better job here, a piece of stolen bread there, a secret hiding place that was not big enough to share. The strong do not survive, my young friend. Only the wily.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo had lost his only son in the Soviet war with Afghanistan, his first wife to breast cancer, and most of his money to bureaucrats who’d managed to survive the Soviet collapse by allying themselves with gangsters. His only family now was his second wife and his grandson, Viktor, a bicycle messenger who had been hit by a car in the melee on 9/11 and inhaled enough toxins while lying on a sidewalk for twelve hours to make his lungs fill with water and blood. I had a slight frame of reference for that, because my lungs were also damaged that day.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo’s insurance company was inundated with 9/11 claims, and he’d spent the last dollar he had on out-of-pocket medical expenses—with no assurance of reimbursement—and his grandson’s visa was expiring.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Was it Viktor who I heard calling out to you?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He nodded. “He’s very afraid,” Leo said, his voice cracking with sorrow. “If he’s sent back to Russia, his medical care will probably end.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I bought Leo a drink. Then I said, “I have a few friends. Let me see what I can do.” Somehow he knew that I really meant it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_42" title="42"/>“That would be greatly appreciated,” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">My offer didn’t feel like an obligation to me. It felt like freedom—in the most American of ways. It was a personal connection offered freely, which could not be dissolved by any decree of government, business, or religion.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo gulped his drink and told me that he would introduce me to “another member of the club”—meaning an asset in counterintelligence—who supposedly had information about secret al-Qaeda sanctuaries in a former Soviet state.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It felt like we were finally building a predictable, trust-based relationship. I didn’t feel at all manipulated, and he didn’t, either.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I lifted my drink and said, <i>“Doveryai no Proveryai.”</i> He repeated it and clinked his glass with mine.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">His al-Qaeda claim was astonishing to me, as my offer to help probably was to him. But we both knew that we’d be working together to help his grandson, so we shared that mission. We also knew it would take at least a year for his grandson to heal, so I would have plenty of time to see if Leo was reliable—and vice versa. I never doubted his competence, and his diligence would be supercharged by his concern for his grandson. I was certain he’d soon feel the same way about my reliability, after I solved his grandson’s visa problem and helped him navigate some federal programs that provided 9/11 medical care.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As I saw a different side of him, I realized that beneath his wounded exterior he was a sensitive man with true wisdom, hiding a heart that had been badly hurt, but still had hope.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I knew from his past actions that he was nothing if not a survivor, and that his gift for survival—despite the guilt it caused him—could be transferred to others whose wounds were still raw and bloody.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In the fall and winter of 2001, which still seems near, a multitude of Americans tasted for the first time the bitter vulnerability that so much of the world suffers every day. Millions of us were still hiding our battered psyches in holes, trying to find a way to get past what had happened, and to crawl out—begin again—and learn to find the best in others, rather than fear the worst.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_43" title="43"/>For these reasons and others, I trusted Leo to guide me on the most important work of my life.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Our friendship had begun.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The al-Qaeda operation was just the beginning—and would almost immediately lead to an event that threatened to do far more damage than the World Trade Center attacks.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When that happened, I was grateful to have such a dependable, predictable man by my side. I’m certain Leo felt the same. I didn’t even have to ask.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">A New Beginning</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">In the dark days after 9/11—gradually to grow brighter—the “<i>end</i> of history” was nevertheless <i>over</i>, and we were back into historic times, starting with what felt like a modern version of the Dark Ages.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Now 9/11 feels forever frozen in the American consciousness. It exists almost as a part of the present, in a surreal compartment in which time is measured not so much by when, but by who and what<i>—</i>and why.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It’s said that if we don’t remember history, we will be doomed to repeat it. So having a part of 9/11 frozen into so many people’s memories should help keep us safe.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But humankind will <i>always</i> repeat history, no matter how well we remember it. We are, after all, only human. That is the lesson we learned during the short and precious moments when history seemed to cease.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So now we can say with sad certainty that history can never end, because people will keep making the same mistakes, again and again.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Can a prediction system such as mine remedy that? No. Not globally, nor eternally. Not a chance.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But it will help some people from making the same mistakes they’ve made before.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That’s a start—and maybe all there is—but it’s enough, at least for now.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_44" title="44"/>For you, there is—with luck and work—a chance that your best days yet will soon begin.</p>
			<aside>
				<h3 class="x10-Sidebar-Title">DEBRIEFING</h3>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-A">CHAPTER 2: <br/>“THINK LIKE AN FBI BEHAVIORAL ANALYST”</p>
				<div class="border"/>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Quote:</b> “Agents don’t assume anything until they collect objective, observable, rational data, free from emotions—including the most destructive emotion: fear.”</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Message:</b> Sizing people up—with the behavioral science protocols that <i>predict how they will act—</i>has become so denatured by myths and matters of morality that its practical purposes have been obscured. <i>But any person can learn the fundamental skills of sizing people up in a matter of days, leading to insights of great value, both professionally and personally.</i></p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-Head">The Hard Truths About Predicting Behavior</p>
				<ul>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-First"><b>Variability:</b> The behavior of almost all people changes significantly throughout life, depending upon their situations, and their degree of control over their own lives.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Immunity:</b> People with notable power—which decreases their accountability—are sometimes tempted to behave badly, and often succumb to the temptation.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Vulnerability:</b> The actions of vulnerable people are the easiest to predict, because they are the most accountable.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Incompetence:</b> People often mislead others by making the honest mistake of thinking they can do things they can’t.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_45" title="45"/>The 50 Percent Rule:</b> The most common forms of predicting behavior—lacking the data points of behavioral science—are wrong approximately half the time.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Intuition:</b> Intuition is notably ineffective for predicting what someone will do.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Appearances:</b> People who share many situational similarities with you are often no easier to predict than anyone else.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Longevity:</b> Long-term relationships, by choice or by circumstance, make both parties more accountable, and therefore predictable.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Perceptions:</b> Predicting what people will do depends primarily on <i>their</i> perceptions, not yours.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted"><b>Persistence:</b> People who are the most manipulative often masquerade as the most virtuous and trustworthy.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-Last"><b>Trust:</b> Trust—based on objective, observable actions such as honesty and transparency—is the most important value in most human transactions.</p>
					</li>
				</ul>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">The Six Signs for Behavior Prediction</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First"><b>Vesting:</b> People’s belief that they will benefit from your success.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Longevity:</b> People’s belief that they will have a long relationship with you.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Reliability:</b> People’s observable, quantifiable degrees of competence and diligence.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Actions:</b> People’s present behaviors, especially when they are consistent with their past behaviors.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Language:</b> People’s ability to communicate in a way that is nonjudgmental, validating, selfless, deep, and focused on you.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last"><b>Stability:</b> People’s degree of emotional maturity, dependability, and consistency is free from manipulative, self-destructive <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_46" title="46"/>traits.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
			</aside>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 9: Part II: The Six Signs for Behavior Prediction: (How to Size People Up) ===

<div class="part">
			<h1 class="x02-Part-Number" id="_idParaDest-11"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_47" title="47"/>PART II</h1>
			<h1 class="x02-Part-Title" id="_idParaDest-12">THE SIX SIGNS FOR BEHAVIOR PREDICTION</h1>
			<h1 class="x02-Part-Subtitle" id="_idParaDest-13">(How to Size People <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_48" title="48"/>Up)</h1>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 10: Chapter 3: Build Bulletproof Alliances: Sign #1: Vesting ===

<div>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Number" id="_idParaDest-14"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_49" title="49"/>3</h2>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Title" id="_idParaDest-15">BUILD BULLETPROOF ALLIANCES</h2>
			<div class="box">
				<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle" id="_idParaDest-16">Sign #1: VESTING</h2>
				<p class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle-Para">Does this person believe they will benefit from your success?</p>
			</div>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A-CO">March 18, 2002, 9:00 a.m.<br/>March Madness</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">“Yeah?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That’s how Leo’s grandson answered the phone. His voice was flat with disinterest, so I knew he had seen it was me on the caller ID.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Hi, Viktor. Is your grandfather around?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yeah.” Silence.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The kid—still a stalwart Russian—hated me, even though I’d helped him with his visa and his medical treatment.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Viktor knew enough about my operations to halt my fledgling career in its tracks, but I wasn’t at all worried, because that wouldn’t be in his best interests. Viktor’s grandfather was fully vested in me and my success—as I was in him—and that put Viktor in a very transparent position: If he messed with me, he’d be messing with his grandfather, whom he not only feared but loved far more.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I was optimistic that Viktor would eventually warm up to me, as <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_50" title="50"/>strangers often do when they feel like they’re in a long-term relationship.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some people might see that type of advantage as leverage, manipulation, or even emotional blackmail, but to me it was trust. The three of us, like it or not, were jammed into this horrible moment of history together, so we all knew what the others would do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“How’s your treatment going?” I asked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I dunno.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Mine’s going okay,” I volunteered. We were both being treated for pulmonary toxicity and chronic coughs, caused by inhaling the toxic fumes of Ground Zero.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Looking back, I think the treatment might have saved my life. In the months since 9/11, approximately a thousand more people had already died, many of them due to serious injuries, organ failure, pneumonia, and suicide—including a colleague of mine. Far more were sick or suffering, and soon would die.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Helping Viktor had cemented my relationship with Leo, but Viktor—who hated being an emigrant, and wasn’t sold on the American Dream—still saw me as an ambitious, ruthless punk who had turned his grandfather into a traitor. From Viktor’s perspective, that made sense to me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even back then, I’d learned how to strategically shut down my own point of view when I needed to, and to see myself with the same contempt as the people who didn’t like me. It’s a painful exercise in self-awareness, but if you can summon enough ego suspension and inner security to view yourself through the eyes of others, it gives you virtual X-ray vision into them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You can understand why they feel the way they do, and it tells you what they need from you. Often as not, though, it’s something you thought you were already giving them. But it’s not <i>your</i> perspective that matters. It’s theirs.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When you get into their heads, you can also see what they consider to be in their own best interests—and when you know that, you can usually predict what they’ll do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_51" title="51"/>“My <i>friend</i>!” Leo was on the line. “How can I help you?” His voice sounded much different than it had at our first painful meeting. The Queens influence on his accent seemed to be gone, replaced by a warm, deep tone that was softened even further by his pleasant Russian accent.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Why the difference? Maybe it was because he was prone to compartmentalization, a subconscious psychological defense mechanism many people use to protect themselves from addressing painful emotions. It is often revealed by language patterns, word choices, and tone.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">More simply, maybe he just presented himself as a badass Russian New Yorker until he got to know people.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In any case, he sounded like he was smiling—always a great tell, even if I couldn’t visually confirm it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When you’re sizing people up, your <i>greatest sources of information will usually be through conversation.</i> Conversation provides vocabulary tells, speech tells, nonverbal tells from the rest of the body, and factual information. It often generates a wealth of data points, even on a landline phone call with no visuals. It’s even more productive if you get some face time with them, because people are social animals, and because most of us know at least enough about nonverbal communication to have a good idea about how someone feels.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Alternately, though, you can sometimes inspire people to be more straightforward and candid when they know they’re not being observed. It’s all a matter of collecting data points and going with what seems to work best.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo talked for a few more minutes, and then I had to get down to “busy-ness.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Leo, we’re in a world of crap,” I said. “State just expelled sixty Russian diplomats, and some of them are <i>our</i> guys.” Meaning: people he and I relied on as unwitting sources of information—his targets, which he’d passed on to me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Because of Hanssen?” He was talking about Robert Hanssen, an FBI agent who was awaiting sentencing for selling secrets to Russia.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yes, Hanssen—so now we’ve got to rebuild our network. At the worst possible time.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_52" title="52"/>Within the Bureau, most of us were pissed about the State Department’s mass expulsions of Russian diplomats. We called it March Madness. It had taken Leo decades, in some cases, to build the rapport with these sources. The only reason I didn’t feel totally overwhelmed was because agents are trained—in somewhat the same way that I’m training you now—to refrain from solving problems until they’re certain that the problems actually exist. But this had certainly knocked me off my stride.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Meet me at the club?” Leo said. He meant his social club for Russian men, which he operated partly for profit and partly to find new contacts.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Can we do it after five thirty? I’m on a damage control deadline with my Jedi Master.” He knew who I meant: Jesse. “We just got tasked with supporting the Hanssen investigation, going back to his early days in New York.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I knew that Leo hated to desecrate the Happy Hour with business, but he was always great about fulfilling my agenda, on my schedule, as if he had none of his own.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Five thirty is perfect!” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“It’s not gonna be easy to rebuild our network,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“No,” he said, with an edgy tone of desperation that was common after 9/11. “But we’ve <i>got</i> to. You and me.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At this point, I didn’t know that behavioral analysts considered vesting in other people’s interests to be a cornerstone of trust. I just knew how good it felt when he reiterated his commitment to our relationship.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I know a guy who may be willing to help,” Leo said. “We’ll start with him and go friend to friend.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That was beyond risky, because it required a tremendous degree of trust for an agent to place their career—and maybe even life—in the hands of someone who starts as a stranger. It’s also risky for U.S. national security.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But I eventually ended up taking Leo’s audacious advice—mostly out of my own desperation—and its brilliance gave me an education equal to a doctoral dissertation.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_53" title="53"/>Methods in the Madness</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I secured my office and bounded up five flights of stairs, just to burn off stress, to the twenty-fifth floor, where Jesse was already half buried in documents about Hanssen. Jesse had worked with Hanssen on a Russian squad for several years, so he and I were expected to come up with something scintillating—in the next nine hours—that would help establish Hanssen’s possible earlier ties to the Russians.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Check it out,” Jesse said when I came in. He took a quarter out of his pocket, stood it on the floor, and watched it roll rapidly to the center of the room, which was sagging dangerously from an extraordinary tonnage of paper-heavy files, and was scheduled for a rebuild. The mountain of files was finally being digitized, as the FBI entered the era of big data, with its promise of providing <i>much better security</i> for confidential information. Sound ironic?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse watched his quarter gain speed, hit a hill on the other side of the room, come to a halt, and fall. “Know what that shows me?” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was expecting another life lesson.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Too damn much paperwork,” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Although Jesse was one of the most decorated people in the agency, with sophisticated counterintelligence—or CI—operations on three continents, he relied far more on his relationships than on written records. I didn’t have a seasoned network, so I was trying to codify his basic methods for turning personal data points into a system. I love systems, because they can be taught in dirt-simple manuals—along the lines of <i>Counterintelligence for Dummies</i> (for people like me). At this point in my life, I’ve seen systems change the cultures of companies, and even countries.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I also value the power of systems for translating emotion into logic, and eliminating emotion for emotion’s sake. Everybody has emotions, but in serious matters, such as counterintelligence, feelings that stray from logic can be deadly. It’s too easy to get emotionally hijacked, fly off the handle, and create a mess you can’t clean up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_54" title="54"/>“Look at this one,” Jesse said, handing me an article about Hanssen. Notes had been scribbled onto it after his arrest a few weeks earlier, when he’d received a dead drop of $50,000 as part of the $1.4 million in cash and diamonds that Russia had paid him.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For that sum, Hanssen had exposed nine U.S. double agents to Russia’s version of the FBI. Three of the agents had been executed, after interrogations that were probably painful. He had also sold Russia information about U.S. nuclear war preparations, as well as surveillance detection technologies that a Russian agent had passed on to Osama bin Laden.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I scanned the dossier. It showed Hanssen’s numerous marital indiscretions and his dire need for his father’s approval.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I asked Jesse if he’d written anything for our memo about Hanssen’s possible earlier work with the Russians.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“No, but I got it up here,” he said, tapping his head.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That was good enough for me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“All I know is what I see,” Jesse said, “but that’s plenty, if you keep your eyes open. Bottom line on Hanssen: self-involved, paranoid prick. The entire time I worked with him, he never partnered with anybody. Hell, he even ate his lunch alone. No ties. No loyalty. Not even with people who tried to be his friend. He <i>acted</i> like he didn’t fit in, so of course he didn’t. Bob didn’t give a rat’s ass about anybody but Bob—and he wasn’t afraid to say it, because he thought it made him look like an up-and-comer. He was always pissed off about something, always felt persecuted, and acted like he was smarter than the other people on our squad. So we never knew what he was up to. When somebody is paranoid like that, you get paranoid about <i>them.</i>”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“He turned traitor over hurt feelings?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“When people are too lazy—or too damaged—to think, they take everything personally, and it messes with their heads.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse handed me an old case file that had been written by Hanssen. It had his supervisor’s comments on it, in big red letters: “THIS IS BULLSHIT!!” and “HOW DO YOU KNOW <i>THIS??</i>”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_55" title="55"/>“That’s typical Bob,” Jesse said. “Even his supervisor didn’t trust him. But nobody said anything. Ever.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Why? Or more to the point, why didn’t <i>you</i>?” Some people would have taken that as an insult. Jesse took it as a question.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“The usual reasons. Benefit of the doubt. Don’t rock the boat. Groupthink. ‘Everybody else tolerates him, so he must be okay.’ And when he finally got to be a supervisor, it got ten times easier for him to be an asshole, as it always does. But Hanssen wasn’t some kind of superspy. He was just good at covering his ass.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">His face clouded, and he said, “When I was new here—like you are—I wish somebody had taken me aside and said, ‘An FBI agent doesn’t give people the benefit of the doubt.’”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d never heard anyone say that. And it went against the grain of my sense of goodness. Jesse read the doubt in my face.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“An agent makes people <i>earn</i> it,” he continued. “By collecting information about them—no matter who they are—and by making informed decisions.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’ll be honest—it seemed cynical. He looked at me in a quizzical way that made me feel exposed—he was probably reading my nonverbals—and said, “You don’t want to hear that, do you? You like to trust people.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Doesn’t everybody?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Not anymore. At least not in this country.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">With that sad reference to our post-9/11 new world disorder, we wrote up what we’d discovered in the archives.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was flipping from being a wide-eyed innocent—just one step removed from the flower-power philosophies of the 1970s—to being somebody who, hopefully, would soon deserve the trust of his colleagues and country.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was also learning some of that perspective from the people in our Washington field office who were leading the Hanssen investigation. From those lessons, and others that followed, I now know this: It’s wise to trust everybody<i>—</i>until they start talking, which is usually right away. At that point, if you’re interested in doing something important <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_56" title="56"/>with them, you’ve got to start thinking like an FBI behavioral analyst: rationally, carefully, and objectively. You have to see people for who they <i>are,</i> with your vision unclouded by fear, affection, or just plain laziness.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If they say the right things, do the right the things, and show the right intentions, keep trusting them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some of them will become people that you trust with your life.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Most won’t. But that’s okay, because it’s not their purpose on earth to be the trustee of your life. Their life is about them, just as yours is about you, and they apparently don’t think that having you in their life will make much difference. You just need to figure out—with <i>facts—</i>where they’re headed. And if they’re not headed the same place you are, so be it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At that point, you can still trust them to do what’s in their own best interests, and they can easily remain an ally, even if they become a competitor. That happens all the time, and with enough humility and rationality, you can build a relationship with a competitor that lasts far longer than any temporary competition you might have had with them. So stay flexible. I learned that in the marines. One of our mottoes was <i>Semper Gumby.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The reality is, very few people are looking to hurt you. They’re just trying to take care of themselves and their families—as you are—so it’s against your best interests to judge them for that. If you do, you’ll ruin any future chance for mutual vesting, because people can smell a judgmental attitude a mile away.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Over time, people and situations change—and someone who pushed you away in the past may want to vest in you. That’s always exciting, because vesting is an excellent creator and barometer of affiliation. When someone decides that your success is their success, they’re powerfully motivated to do whatever they can for you. In your professional life, vesting is the ultimate animator of teamwork, and at home, it’s what keeps a family near and dear.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I usually experience vesting in only about one-fifth of the people who seem to share some or all of my goals. Many of the other 80 percent are reluctant to offer trust because they’ve been burned. You can <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_57" title="57"/>look at that reticence to vest as a negative, but you can also see it as a sign of someone’s intelligence and sincerity. Trust is sacred. It shouldn’t be squandered, and it carries obligations for those who take it seriously.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Many people tiptoe into trust, which is usually smart. But occasionally the tells of willingness to vest—expressed in words, deeds, and intentions—are so obvious that it results in a phenomenon I call “trust at first sight.”</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">The Negative Tells for Vesting</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Each of the tells of trust—also referred to here as “negative tells”—and distrust will be conveyed to you in the following four ways:</p>
			<div role="list">
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered-First" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">Verbal communication:</b> What they say, and how they say it.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">Nonverbal communication:</b> Body language that either confirms their message, or causes you to doubt it.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">Actions:</b> Current and past actions that are recorded in public and private sources of information.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">Intentions:</b> Stated and/or observed, that indicate someone’s relative degree of alignment with your own goals and dreams.</p>
			</div>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The tells of distrust are often less significant than the tells of trust, mostly because they are more ambiguous and open to interpretation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">A tell of distrust may stem from simple lack of interest in you, or from a benign misunderstanding.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL">To understand the tells of vesting, we’ll start with the ones that indicate that someone is <i>not</i> willing to vest in you. These tells are usually hard to miss, simply because they hurt.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_58" title="58"/>The tells of distrust described here are very common, but there are countless variations. These will give you an excellent idea, though, of the content and tone of many other tells, some of which may apply to you alone, since we’re all different people, in different situations.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><b>Warning:</b> When these very common tells of distrust occur, don’t assume the worst. The tells of distrust can be tricky, and it’s easy to get emotionally hijacked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Stay rational and don’t take things personally until you get the facts.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even if the facts show that someone does not want to vest in you, you should still try to make the best of the situation. Try to solve the problem, and if you can’t, move on—and embrace the change.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Learning how to embrace every situation in a positive way can be life-changing. In the science of behavioral analysis—unlike the hard sciences of math or physics—a positive attitude, applied wisely and vigorously, can transform almost anything into a positive.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN NEGATIVE TELLS FOR VESTING IN YOUR SUCCESS</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. Supervisors pass you over for a promotion and give it to someone with whom you work.</span> This is often the strongest negative tell, because it clearly shows that you’re not perceived as the person who will best enhance the life of the supervisor who awarded the promotion.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The first time can mean nothing. Several times sends a clear message: Start being more helpful to others—especially your bosses—or look for a new job.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Maybe, though, they’re just looking for something better to give you.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. Your bosses point out ways that you’re different from them. </span>Ouch! Opposites don’t attract—they repel.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Your supervisors are saying: You’ll never be part of our tribe until you start making our lives better. If you don’t, you’re dispensable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_59" title="59"/>It’s a clear warning. Ask, in a sincere way, how you can make your supervisors’ lives better.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If it’s something you can live with, give it a try. If it’s not, stay classy and try to depart in your own time and your own style.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. Your bosses leave you out of meetings that your peers attend. This is also one of the worst tells. </span>It’s obvious that they think you’re not needed, probably because you’re not doing your best to help other people do <i>their</i> best.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But don’t assume the obvious. When I was young, I often made an issue out of this, but that just made things worse. Maybe you don’t belong in the meeting—so figure out where you <i>do</i> belong, and work your way up from there with actions that include helping your supervisors and peers to succeed.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People usually won’t leave you out of a meeting if they think you’ll help them push their own agenda.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People with power over you exaggerate the importance of your trivial errors. </span>They’re probably trying to find reasons to get rid of you, most likely because they don’t think their own careers are benefiting from your presence.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In many companies, people’s careers are often linked to the performance of their division, or the company as a whole, so their sense of their own best interests can extend to the company itself.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When you are helping others and helping your company, people find ways to downplay errors.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">So get the facts—straight from the person who criticizes you—and let the facts, rather than your fear, guide your response.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. Your immediate supervisor mentions other departments where you might excel. </span>If you hear it from a supervisor who shows no <i>other</i> signs of being vested in your success, it usually means you’re not giving them what they want. You’d better start—right away. They seem to think they’d be better off without you.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_60" title="60"/>But if it’s from someone who does show signs of being vested in you, jump at it. Tell them your dreams.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Either way, stay positive. People who look for the worst usually find it, often creating disaster out of thin air.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. Executives engage in negative nonverbals while you’re offering your ideas. </span>This tell is critically important, because someone’s nonverbals are often more revealing than what they say.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The easiest negative nonverbals to notice include eyelid flutters, lip compression, eyebrow furrowing, or a failure to look you in the eye, shake your hand, or smile at you. As a rule, I look at people’s facial expressions more than any other form of nonverbal behavior, because faces are the most revealing.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Most of these nonverbal tells say: I don’t want to hear another word, because I don’t think it’s going to help me get what I need right now.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. Your boss asks you about rumors that you’ve done something wrong. </span>This is a bad tell, without much wiggle room. Regardless of the veracity of the rumor, it’s bad management to listen to rumors and then hit somebody over the head with them. It’s very passive-aggressive.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But if it comes from someone who considers you very valuable to them, thank them for the warning.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. Your organization’s decision makers text or email while they’re talking to you, or walk away while you’re still talking. </span>Unless this occurs in the middle of a crisis, both are tells that your career doesn’t matter to them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Someone walking away while you’re talking is the worst. It’s blatant—and if they do it in front of others, it’s the kiss of death. They clearly don’t think you’re valuable to them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If this happens repeatedly and pointedly, accept the <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_61" title="61"/>information, deal with it rationally, and get ready to leave—or make yourself <i>much</i> more valuable while you still have time.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. Leaders never say, “I really want you to succeed here,” or words to that effect. </span>What doesn’t get said is often as important as what does. How much clearer can it be? They don’t really care.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Suck it up and initiate the issue: “I want to contribute to your success and the company’s. What suggestions do you have on how I can be a better resource?”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If you do validate their criticism, you’ll feel vulnerable, right? Don’t worry about it. You were already vulnerable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. People challenge your thoughts and opinions. </span>Most people are sure that this is a bad sign—because it often is. But good leaders and valuable peers typically challenge the ideas of the people they value most, because they take their ideas seriously.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The worst-case scenario is that they feel critical of you, even more than any individual thought or opinion you offer. They’re just looking for a peg where they can hang their hater-hat.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">So assess the criticism for validity, importance, and its mode of presentation. Some leaders <i>like</i> a dialogue that’s based on differences of opinion.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">If they’ve got a good point, thank them for their input—sincerely, not passive-aggressively—and jump on it. Working for people means working on their behalf.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>Now ask yourself:</b> How many of these following tells apply to you? Are you doing enough research before you decide something? Have you ever gotten burned by someone who went quickly in and out of your life? Have other people misled you when you didn’t have time to check on what they said?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">These are the types of questions that you need to ask yourself after <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_62" title="62"/>each of the tells—positive and negative—for the rest of the book. But I won’t waste your time by mentioning it again.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">March 18, 2002, 5:30 p.m.<br/>The Outsiders</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I arrived a few minutes early for my meeting with Leo at a place called the Outsiders Club, the social hall he owned. It was part of his outdoor adventure company—for guided events such as hunting, fishing, or hiking—that was used by Russian tourists or businesspeople, as well as for Leo’s own clique of Russian emigrants.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The name had a double meaning that the guests from abroad were not privy to. “Outsiders” did not refer solely to outdoorsmen—as the visitors usually presumed, for obvious reasons. It also referred to the antipathy of the local emigrants, almost all of whom were alienated from Russia. Some of them worked as confidential human sources, under Leo’s wing. Almost all of them came from the former satellite nations controlled by the Soviet Union—including Ukraine, Georgia, and the -<i>stan</i> states like Uzbekistan or Kazakhstan. Those were the hot spots that were already locked in a bloody state of perma-rebellion against Russia and its new president, the former failed KGB agent Vladimir Putin, who somehow managed to land on his feet after his poor performance as an agent.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The importance of knowing details applied even more stringently to Leo’s visitors from Russia, who were often intelligence officers (aka spies) and were obviously unaware that Leo was a double agent. They had an ugly streak of entitlement that stemmed from being upper-crust residents of the Moscow area, or Muscovites, who were also known as White Russians. The “white” delineated them from the “ethnic” Russians of the breakaway states. Ethnic Russians had long been second-class citizens in their own society, even during the “classless” Soviet era. No ethnic Russian, here or in the motherland, had ever been an agent for the KGB or its new incarnation, the SVR. Nor were there any <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_63" title="63"/>female SVR agents at this time, because they were not considered—believe it or not—intelligent enough for the position.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If these bizarre differentiations that govern Russian trust seem like arcane and archaic leftovers from a long-ago tribal past, remember that tribal warfare has been the most enduring of all human conflicts and still dominates the world’s modern military engagements. It’s been going on for centuries, and involves people with whom I have worked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some of these people were very vulnerable to severe retribution then—and some may still be. In the world of counterintelligence, people’s lives and reputations are always on the line, and discretion and confidentiality are always at play. That’s why I don’t use real names in these anecdotes, and change details that would expose people to harm.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">America is vastly less punitive to innocent people, but it also has many divisions. In America, tribal trust is currently dominated by the smoldering, divisive differences of race, money, religion, and politics, and try as we might to have a reasonable rationale, many of us hand out trust like candy to anybody in our own tribe. But that’s very unwise.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The irrationality of trust even infects America in its most supposedly rational institutions, including national security. For example, a flirty and attractive female cyberthreat analyst recently made connections with hundreds of people—82 percent males—from the U.S. military, intelligence agencies, and information security companies. Google and Lockheed Martin expressed interest in hiring her, and she was offered speaking engagements at security conferences. But the “woman” was just an avatar created by a national security researcher to see how gullible people—especially men—can be when they don’t look beyond a pretty face.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was on a team that had a somewhat similar experience with a genuine Russian spy, Anna Chapman, who used her charm and beauty to hack American security.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I had no illusions, as I waited for Leo to arrive, about the vulnerability to manipulation in assessing people that we <i>all</i> share—especially in a crisis, when we are most in need of the ability to accurately size people up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_64" title="64"/>At this moment, though, I was indeed in a crisis. I needed to quickly rebuild a team of informants—both witting and unwitting.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When Leo, the club president, burst through the door with his husky Lion King build and bighearted smile, the differences in the room seemed to diminish. But not among everyone. Many of the men were still very Russian, and the cultural norm in Russia is that people who smile effusively are less trustworthy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Hello, men!” Leo boomed.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He gave me a big hug and waved for two glasses of what had become our traditional drink: Gzhelka White Gold vodka. “I love this,” Leo said, lowering his voice to a conspiratorial decibel, which felt appropriate, considering my misgivings about what he had in mind. “Starting new operations! Rebuilding the team! Learning from you! I dreamed of days like this in the camps, just to stay alive—and it’s happening!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He touched his glass to mine, with shiny eyes. “Thank you for this dream, my good friend.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I touched his glass. His appreciation was contagious—as that trait always is—and his pride in our work was inspiring.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But after fourteen prior handlers, why did Leo trust me? I had to assume it started when I offered to help him. Nothing else made sense.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“How is Valentina?” I asked. He’d recently gotten remarried, and three days later his wife had been in a car wreck.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“My Valentine is not well. Medicare issues.” His eyes went dry, a sign that he was back into survival mode.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“What can I do for you?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“A word to the right person in the Bronx division of Social Security?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I will do everything I can.” That was—thank God—something I could do. My neighbor worked for the Social Security Administration. Jesse had long ago advised me to form great relationships with <i>everyone</i> in my life. You never know . . .</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo leaned close. “Your priority now is terrorism,” he said, his voice now a husky whisper. “I have worked with you only on Russian issues. <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_65" title="65"/>I have another idea, if you will indulge me.” His eyes were wide and searching.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Only if it can be good for <i>you</i>, too,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He bent even closer. “I grew up with a man who lives in Manhattan now named Sergei. And my father—he was a country doctor, you know?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I do know.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“My father saved the life of Sergei’s sister, and on that day Sergei pledged to me that I was his”—he had to take a breath—“brother for life. For <i>life</i>.” He said the word with reverence, having seen so much life casually destroyed. “Sergei works at the Russian consulate now, but he is not a White Russian. So for him diplomacy is a difficult business.” As earlier in our relationship, he pronounced it <i>busy-ness</i>—probably on purpose. He always became more Russian when he was near Russians.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Is he a member of the club?” I asked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“The Outsiders?” Leo asked softly. “Or the other?” He meant the clandestine services.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“The Outsiders. This place.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yes, the Outsiders only,” he said. “He is a clean diplomat.” That meant he was not with Special Services—the spy network—and would not be on anybody’s radar, ours or theirs.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“He loves Russia. In the way some men love wives who do not deserve it. But he loves me more. As one should.” Leo cherished his adopted country of America enough to do things that could get him quietly assassinated, but he held the love for people as the most sacred of all.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Then why would he talk to me?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Because he loves me. And I love you.” He said it in a matter-of-fact way, but it still put a lump in my throat.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’d be happy to meet him.” I didn’t have the heart to say it, but this did <i>not</i> sound wise.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo reached over, shook my hand, and let it linger. Two more drinks suddenly appeared, and I handed one to him.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i>“Nostrovia!”</i> I said, clinking his glass.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_66" title="66"/>I thought about how distant and despairing he’d been only seven months earlier—when I still had the acrid stink of Ground Zero on my clothing—and I felt a sense of wonder for the finest feature of the human spirit: the generosity that lies deep within it, anxious to emerge.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I also knew—despite this moment, which touched me to my core—that America, Leo, his wife, Viktor, myself, and the huge grieving city of New York were still in a world of pain and chaos. We were all refugees, in our own way, from a new kind of war that threatened never to end—and never has.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I just hoped that Leo’s friend Sergei wouldn’t report me to a security officer, who would probably make a protest to the U.S. State Department and create a big diplomatic mess—with me at the center.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It could easily turn into an international incident at a time of serious national vulnerability. It was also possible that the Russians would punish me personally. I’d be largely immune if I stayed in the States, but if I traveled abroad, it might be quite different.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Leo and some of the other Outsiders I had met here were familiar with a far darker and more dangerous life than the one I had led, and I could see how something as dicey as approaching a known Russian loyalist would seem reasonable to them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I knew I needed to find a dependable, systematic method that would enable me to succeed in this bizarre business—or at least to survive. But I didn’t know where to start, other than with what I later learned was the most important aspect of sizing somebody up: vesting in Leo’s success, by continuing to help his grandson.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even though the kid was a loose cannon, I knew I was doing the right thing.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">March 21, 2002<br/>Staying Positive</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I spent the next few days picking Jesse’s brain about how to deal with the Sergei situation, and he was even more optimistic than usual. He’d <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_67" title="67"/>seen the deadly side of war and espionage, and was hard to rattle. Even by 2002, though, I’d learned enough from Jesse and Leo to consider having a <i>very frank</i> discussion with Sergei, if I saw the tells of vesting that I needed. Why not? If I think that your success is my success, why wouldn’t I trust you?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Over the years, in almost all of the cases that included vesting, the relationships and objectives succeeded—especially in the business world, since it revolves around money, which is easy to measure.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">A company is more likely to succeed over the long term if:</p>
			<ul>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted-First">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted-First"><b>Owners and C-suite executives</b> believe that, for them to succeed, they need to vest in the success of their direct reports.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b>Managers</b> believe that, to succeed, they need to vest in their employees.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b>Investors</b> believe that management is primarily looking out for them.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted-Last">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted-Last"><b>Customers</b> believe that the company is driven by customer satisfaction.</p>
				</li>
			</ul>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In personal relationships, though, vesting is probably even more powerful. Among the happy couples I know, each person not only loves the other, but is convinced that anything that happens to their partner—good or bad—affects them equally.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The following ten tells are very common and carry a lot of clout. But use them partly to notice tells of your own.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN POSITIVE TELLS FOR VESTING IN YOUR SUCCESS</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People are happy to work at your tempo, and in your work style instead of theirs.</span> There are people who just won’t allow others to disrupt their way of doing things, and they tend not to vest in others simply because we all have somewhat different ways of getting things done.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People who are comfortable vesting in others, though, are <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_68" title="68"/>proud of bending over backward to accommodate the needs of their close associates.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. People typically talk in terms of your own priorities, interests, and prosperity. </span>They make you the center of attention because they see you as their surrogate and don’t need to recapture the spotlight for themselves. The wise response is to grant them the same courtesy and commitment.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. People look for ways to expand their relationship with you. </span>They seek further common interests, and include you in many of the important things they do, even if it’s not something you can do on a level equal to theirs. They’ll find a way to employ your skills.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They will also bridge the gap between personal and professional relationships—by bringing you into their personal life if you work together, or by sharing their professional life with you if they’re a social friend.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People join you in a difficult project even when they would rather not. </span>On several occasions, colleagues of mine have been assigned to something that felt like a death march to me, but I’ve volunteered to join them out of personal consideration. There’s no better foundation for vesting than to suck it up and dive in.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">A less dramatic but still meaningful way to show you’re vested in someone’s success is to work unpaid overtime with them, especially if you can contribute something they can’t.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">As always, actions speak loudest. That’s partly why I volunteer as a pilot for a medical airlift organization. It’s time and resource intensive, and is the most action-oriented contribution I can make.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People call in their own favors on your behalf. </span>Cashing in the currency held in someone’s own “favor bank” is a huge tell of <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_69" title="69"/>vesting. It not only signals the proactive willingness of their help, but also often creates a new relationship for you, if you meet the person who owed the favor.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It can also create what’s called the Benjamin Franklin Effect: When someone does a favor for you, it makes them like you more, because they’ve invested in you and would suffer from cognitive dissonance if they didn’t like you. It’s almost pure elixir of vesting. Franklin discovered it when he borrowed a book from an enemy and found that the guy no longer disliked him.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. Virtually all of someone’s positive actions are unsolicited. </span>I call this the “initiative test.” If someone helps you on their own initiative, it indicates much more vesting than if you have to ask them for help.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It shows even more vesting if they create new forums, tasks, or venues that are intended to help you, such as forming a committee and putting you at its head.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. People create a positive image of you inside and outside of your company, and give you credit for success that they were involved with, at the expense of their own credit. </span>This form of self-branding not only connotes faith in their connection with you, but also reveals the generosity and emotional stability of their own personality.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If this extremely valuable tell comes from an immediate supervisor, it shows that they care enough about you to risk the chance of losing you to another company or division—or even to run the risk of you taking their own job.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People who consistently create a positive brand for you are pure gold. Treat them well and the mutual trust will run deep.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. People show genuine excitement about your achievements. </span>That’s usually because they see your success as theirs, too, but it can also indicate pure generosity. If they don’t show excitement, <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_70" title="70"/>but seem to be guardedly resentful or jealous, you may want to find another ally.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It’s even better if they show unabashed pleasure at the prospect of benefiting from your achievement. In that case, there’s nothing shameful about somebody benefiting from you and voicing it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. People extend their professional relationship with you into a social one. </span>This is huge and also very common. The bilevel connection creates two separate, mutually reinforcing forms of commitment. It also indicates that they enjoy you as a person, and not just someone who can help them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. People tell you something they’ve never told anyone else. </span>This is huge!</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They trust your discretion and advice, and see you as nonjudgmental.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Of those values, the one I admire most is the refusal to judge, which strongly encourages the people around you to be honest. It also creates a relaxed, congenial atmosphere.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">When someone opens their heart to you, you can’t help but see yourself in a better light. It quiets your self-criticism, and helps you rise above being your own best enemy.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">March 30, 2002<br/>Trust at First Sight</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">“It’s a go,” my supervisor said. “HQ authorized the contact.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d had to go through channels on the Sergei operation and was glad that my meeting with him had been approved. But it was not unanimous: Some people were fiercely opposed.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I didn’t like my odds and I hated the timing. Gaining the cooperation of a diplomat is as likely as winning the lottery—which is why we often referred to this scenario as “hitting lotto.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_71" title="71"/>The biggest risk was that Sergei would rat me out to his embassy, because diplomats can get an instant promotion for reporting such contact to their security officers. And the time crunch mandated a one-meeting agreement instead of, say, two years—which is about average and is safer.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So lacking anything tangible to offer, I’d have to extend what I call trust at first sight: genuine, respectful consideration for the other person’s best interests.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">These days, I can confidently offer my own trust to almost anyone—until they abuse it, which happens—but back then I didn’t realize how flexible I could be with trust. I was still hooked on the notion—which seems quaint now—that trust was a spiritual and moral absolute that could never be retracted, or even modified. The very idea that you could love someone and still not trust them remained foreign to me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was concerned, though, that Sergei would feel constrained by the power of his government. For well over a hundred years, Russians have generally been dominated by strong governments: starting with the czars, then the Soviet commissars, and then the drug czars in Putin’s billionaire boys club.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I called Sergei at a covert number provided by Leo, and he agreed to meet me at a Wall Street restaurant called Reserve Cut. My dining choice was an unspoken message to him that nobody in his consulate would see him in lower Manhattan, miles from its location on the Upper East Side.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He agreed immediately. Most Russian diplomats would have insisted I visit them at their office, indicating very politely that they didn’t even want to consider the possibility of an “unofficial relationship.” Maybe Sergei just wanted to get away from his grim, gray consulate and eat at a spendy, trendy in-spot. Leo had told me that Sergei didn’t get out much and always appreciated a good meal with friendly conversation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">On D-Day, as the maître d’ led Sergei to my table, I made a baseline read of his body language: eyebrows tensed with compression, lips tight, head tilted back in reticence, arms folded in front of him. He <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_72" title="72"/>eyeballed me, too, as he read my body for the same information. A quick, soft handshake. Shields up. He glanced at his watch. Bad sign.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Sergei, thank you so much for taking the time to have a conversation with me. Although I wish our mutual good friend had been able to make it.” No reason to name names.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d talked to Leo about having him join us, as a way to help put Sergei at ease, but we decided it might actually inhibit Sergei, if he and I actually did get down to a deal.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The mention of Leo softened Sergei’s face slightly as he scanned the room of power brokers approvingly—as Leo thought he would—without seeming self-conscious. His suit and shirt didn’t meet the implicit dress code, but he wore them well. He had broad shoulders and a trim physique, and looked better than most of his Russian counterparts, who tended to be pale and pudgy from their numbing number of office hours.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Our friend thought we had a few things in common and might be able to find other common interests, with commensurate resources,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Sergei looked relieved that I’d immediately acknowledged the reality of the situation—in the bonding, ambiguous international language of bureaucracy—instead of insulting his intelligence with head games. In his relief, a few more lines left his face.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">His response encouraged me enough to offer him another taste of reality, although I would again need to shade it in vague language, to acknowledge the gravity of my statement, and to give him room for denial if things went south.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I know you’re a diplomat,” I said, “but let’s say, hypothetically, that somebody in your consulate was in Special Services.” His brows raised slightly, because that was code for intel. It was also a tacit admission that I, too, was in “special services”—the term used by real-life spies, who almost never say “spy.” The corners of his mouth canted slightly upward: a subtle signal of assent. We already had a grain of mutual vesting!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But as he sipped a drink and fussed with his napkin, he glanced at his watch again—which might have been a brush-off, so I asked him <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_73" title="73"/>point-blank if he needed to be somewhere. At the very least, it would show my sensitivity to his needs. But if he said he had to leave soon, it would mean: <i>No dice. Game over</i>. Let’s order!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Not at all,” he said. “I have as long as you’d like.” Good sign! He wasn’t actually withdrawing, but simply had some time constraints, and was subordinating his schedule to mine. (That’s Positive Tell #1. I’ll keep pointing out the positive tells. I’m sure you’ve already noticed some of the negative tells, such as his unmistakably negative nonverbals.)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I asked him how he’d met Leo, as an open-ended “inkblot” test to see where he’d go. If he gave me an abrupt “That was long ago,” he’d be saying that his relationship with Leo was none of my business—and that I had no hope of piggybacking on Leo’s relationship with him.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">This might seem like nothing but small talk, but I’ve found that all talk can be important and significant, if it enables you to build a healthy, mutually supportive relationship.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’ve also found that, as a rule, the further people go back in their lives, the more they lower their shields, presumably because of some primal instinct.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Here are other tells of vesting I was looking for: Is he appropriately receptive to neutral subjects? Is he proactive about revealing himself? Is he willing to share something that’s important to him? Will he say something he hasn’t told anyone else? Is he going to hand me a couple of nuggets that promised more?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As I’d requested before Sergei arrived, the waiter brought two glasses of top-shelf Gzhelka White Gold vodka, which was not only Leo’s favorite, but also Sergei’s. That was another way to show Sergei that Leo had told me several things about him (hopefully without it seeming creepy), and it increased the level of intimacy between Sergei and me—whether he liked it or not. It was a complicated encounter, but probably no more complex than the hard meetings in your profession. Manipulation, deceit, and temptation are everywhere.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So Sergei gradually surrendered the false presumption of enjoying full privacy and told me about the day Leo’s father had saved his sister’s <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_74" title="74"/>life. His memories triggered emotion, but he controlled it well, and again we crossed a bridge of connection.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then I asked him another open-ended question, making it sound as spontaneous as possible. “What was your favorite childhood holiday or tradition?” It’s my all-time favorite, and has evoked many fascinating and enriching reactions, because it’s a nonthreatening way to break the ice with people and help shift their mood to one of receptivity. It brings them back to a time and place where they felt happy and safe, and ratchets up the contentment neurotransmitters of dopamine and serotonin—though not quite as effectively as good vodka, which they served generously here, as I’d arranged. He told me what a Russian Orthodox Christmas was like, as he settled into an amazing tuna and white truffle appetizer.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Robin,” he said. “What are you looking for? Why are we chatting?” Great! It was the first tell indicating that he wanted to share more than a two-hundred-dollar lunch with me. And it was unsolicited (Positive Tell #6)!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’d like to get an understanding of your priorities and interests,” I said, “to see if I could be a resource for any of them.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He nodded amiably. “What are <i>your</i> priorities?” he asked. Nice! He was shifting the focus to me (Positive Tell #2). I was warming up to him!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Right now I’m rebuilding, in the aftermath of the Robert Hanssen case.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“That sounds important!” He pumped me for more details—taking pains to show me he wasn’t looking for anything classified—and seemed genuinely impressed that I was working at that level so early in my career (Positive Tell #8).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I brought the conversation back to our very different childhoods—not for business purposes, but because it’s my nature to be curious. To connect, you’ve got to be yourself, win or lose, because people are extremely well attuned to manipulation these days. Any whiff of it can make them go cold in a heartbeat.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">After another vodka, I asked, “So what do you think we might be <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_75" title="75"/>able to do together? Something both our countries would be happy about?” I liked the guy. He was a straight shooter and smart—everything Leo claimed. “What are <i>your</i> interests?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“To make the world better for my children. <i>Safer,</i>” he said pointedly. That kind of goody-goody response can be canned and insincere, but he continued: “I sometimes take them . . . took them . . . to lower Manhattan, around the World Trade Center.” He stopped abruptly. Russian spies don’t show sentiment, and merely mentioning his kids—in the same sentence as the WTC—had obviously touched a chord.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“When the airplanes hit,” he said, “I wasn’t sure where my children were, or what would happen next.” His face was stoic, but he again stopped abruptly.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">To fill the silence, I said, “I understand that. All too well.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Fathers are fathers everywhere,” he said (Positive Tell #9).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Of course. Our first priority is always their safety.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Please. . . .” He was hesitant. “I am taking a risk chatting with you, and did it only as a favor to our mutual friend. If you value your relationship with him and wish to continue to chat, this must remain between us. He would understand your discretion, I am sure” (Positive Tell #10).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The tells of trust were coming more quickly. That’s usually the norm: Trust invites trust. “I wasn’t sure what to expect when I came here,” I said, “but I was hopeful, based on the warm feelings our friend holds toward you. And this has exceeded what I had hoped for.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">His entrée of salmon and black caviar came, with some tiny, crunchy tempura vegetables. “We should meet again,” Sergei said (Positive Tell #3), as he spread some caviar on a toast-point coated with cream and lemon.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Do you have friends in Special Services back home?” I asked. I didn’t think he would answer, and he didn’t. But he gave me a half-smile to show that he wasn’t offended by my effort—and that, hell yes, of course he did.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That wasn’t a nosy spy question, even though it came out like one. Just more curiosity—I like getting to know people. But I was glad he <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_76" title="76"/>didn’t question why I wanted to know. That raised <i>my</i> comfort level. So I asked him to name one of his favorite memories as a kid. That’s another pacifying, evocative question.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He told me about building a country home, or dacha, with his father. In return, I told him about my family’s far more frugal camping trips to a lake. I enjoyed being the only one at the table with a working-class background, and he appreciated the irony.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he said, “I cannot help you on many of the things that you may wish I would.” I was only mildly disappointed. Even in those days, I knew that building a relationship with an important person is usually more valuable than solving a momentary problem. “But if you are curious about some of my thoughts on terrorism . . . ?” His mind reading reminded me of Jesse.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Absolutely.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I can facilitate that interest of yours by calling a person in my home state who is indebted to me. And he can tell you things that will help you, and may be of some service to my own country, and to me as well” (Positive Tell #5). “If a cooperative agreement is reached,” he said, “I will stay involved, even though that will be difficult, for several reasons. But that is my problem, not yours. Of course, if you succeed, it will be of great benefit to me” (Positive Tell #4).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he dropped an amazing gift onto my lap—even now it is classified information. I can tell you, though, that it involved terrorists who had infiltrated various insurgent militias in his home state, and in other republics that were rebelling against Russia.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">An operation against them would be great for American national security. It would limit the geographic spread of al-Qaeda, forcing them to stay centralized and to remain an identifiable, actionable target.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I will not, of course, tell my people that you and I have had a cordial midday meal,” he said. “But perhaps in the future we both will have higher ranks and titles, and perhaps the ties between our countries will strengthen by then, and we can cooperate more openly” (Positive Tell #7).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As you have undoubtedly noticed, this meeting—which produced <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_77" title="77"/>notable results that probably saved many lives—was not a cloak-and-dagger exercise in mind manipulation, nor was it a lovefest between two people who are dying to please each other.
