						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They position you as someone who’d be hard to replace.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They periodically offer rewards, including promotions.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They give you perks that signify a long-term relationship.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They encourage you to expand your training.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They welcome you to be part of social activities.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They typically say “we” instead of “I” or “you.”</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They never seem to take you for granted.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-Head">Ten Negative Tells for the Perception of Longevity in a Relationship</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They forget your name but don’t seem to care.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t mention renewing your temporary contract.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t try to connect with you on a personal level.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">You’re the last to know about certain important issues.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t ask about your long-term goals.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">You aren’t included in work-related social events.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">A peer employee is rude to you.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Your offer to expand your role is rejected.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">It seems as if there are cliques that don’t welcome your presence.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">When people talk about the future, they never mention <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_114" title="114"/>you.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
			</aside>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 12: Chapter 5: Know Who to Go To: Sign #3: Reliability ===

<div>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Number" id="_idParaDest-20"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_115" title="115"/>5</h2>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Title" id="_idParaDest-21">KNOW WHO TO GO TO</h2>
			<div class="box">
				<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle" id="_idParaDest-22">Sign #3: RELIABILITY</h2>
				<p class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle-Para">Can this person do what they say they will? And will they?</p>
			</div>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A-CO">August 2018<br/>Southeastern United States</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">America was in the dawning days of threat from a new weapon of mass destruction, and a task force I’d joined was among the first in the FBI to confront it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As our team leader called the initial meeting to order, I pulled out my tablet to take notes, hungry for what he knew about this developing danger. He had a reputation for brilliance, and he seemed cordial and receptive, so I had no glaring reason not to trust him with this difficult assignment. Besides, his supervisors obviously relied on him, so I assumed I could, too, in a rational and objective transfer of trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">My readiness to trust the guy—whom I’ll call George—was also bolstered by the apparent presence of three of the Six Signs for Behavior Prediction. His past patterns of competence and diligence were stellar (Sign #3: Reliability), and he was known for caring about the <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_116" title="116"/>success of the people with whom he worked (Sign #1: Vesting). Also, because of the severity of this situation, it looked as if he and I would be linked for a long time (Sign #2: Longevity).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I had enough secondhand data points on George to make me like and respect him, but not enough yet to make me fully trust him with this part of the national security of the United States. A person at the helm of a project like this needed to be absolutely <i>reliable:</i> a quality, as I’ve mentioned, that consists primarily of <i>competence</i> and <i>diligence.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Despite my residue of doubt about George, I felt optimistic and receptive—an attitude that has sparked most of the best relationships in my life. Many people, of course, start at the other end of the spectrum of trust, but if you’re pessimistic and wary, people usually sense it, react to it, and return it—as golden opportunities die on the vine. Even feeling neutral about someone can, so to speak, leave you stuck in neutral.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The task force was composed of people from several offices, so we started by introducing ourselves in five-minute snippets, talking mostly about the training that had brought us here. When George’s turn came, he rose with a look of quiet confidence and squared off into the rectilinear body language of a powerful man facing a momentous challenge. His first words, reflecting the mission statement he’d crafted, grabbed the attention of everybody at the table.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“We’re facing a mass destruction weapon of the future,” he intoned, “and our mission is to understand it, confine it, control it, and eliminate it. If we do, history may remember this moment. If we don’t, God help us all.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We all sat a little straighter, and George seemed to like the effect his words had on us.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Glancing at the three-by-five cards in his hand, he told the story of how he’d been assigned to this position. Then he followed up with a recitation of his greatest hits, so I put down my tablet and waited for him to introduce our agenda of action. But he went off on a tangent about identifying funds, creating processes, and fulfilling the paperwork requirements.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That gave me pause. He’d opened with a galvanizing selling point, <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_117" title="117"/>but now he was <i>un</i>selling it with a sea of digressions that were all delivered with equal verve. Some of the agents were drifting into thousand-yard stares, but I wrote off the jibber-jabber as just a bad case of first-meeting jitters.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was able to keep my focus, but needed coffee—badly. The problem was, we weren’t allowed to put any food or drink on our sleek redwood conference table, which was built along the lines of an aircraft carrier. That’s government for you, right? Splurge on a table, and then trust agents enough to stop mass destruction, but not enough to keep their table clean?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I peeked at my watch. <i>Oh shit.</i> Forty minutes had passed, and he was still on the story of his life. The day was already dreary with low-hanging clouds, and the shades were drawn—as they are in all of our buildings, all the time—but even so, I couldn’t help but marvel at how something as sexy as preventing mass destruction could be reduced to clerical work so dull that it made me rate the day by its weather.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was a bad tell, because in group situations people don’t believe you’re competent at something if you can’t communicate it. It’s part of the package, and I’ve seen it kill important projects.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As George droned on, it became clear that he was far more adept with technology than with people, which was understandable, since he was in the investigative sector of the FBI, which tends to be tech driven. In my branch—operations—people are the driving force. We find confidential human sources and enlist them in projects, which are, in effect, the products that we brand, market, and sell to supervisors.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">George’s specialty, which I rarely dealt with, was an area called Flaps and Seals: a surveillance sector that includes covertly opening the envelopes and packages of suspects and foreign threats, using a range of technologies. It’s associated with a section known as Locks, Clicks, and Picks, a branch of the Bureau’s tradecraft that performs missions requiring clandestine entry into buildings and rooms—black-bag jobs—to find evidence, disable cryptography devices, and deposit surveillance equipment—some of it really cool, like miniature audio-video drones that looked like bugs, called insectothopters. None of this, though, had much to do with people.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_118" title="118"/>But I’ll say this for the tech guys: They always had a hell of a lot better toys than we did.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As the clock ticked and my stomach growled, I grew less receptive to George, because he was still stuck in the weeds and was adopting a pontifical tone. Irrelevant information by itself is bad enough, but irrelevant <i>pontifical</i> information is unforgivable—and as common as dirt.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I wasn’t happy. But so what? If the point of my work was to make me happy, I wouldn’t get paid.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Besides, I was at the epicenter of America’s response to a game-changing weapon of the future—which was, somewhat ironically, drones. Like many people, I hadn’t given much thought to drones as an emerging threat, but one of my field supervisors, Doug Wellborne, had put an end to that.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Doug, who knew that I was a licensed pilot, had called me into his office and told me that the use of drones in criminal and terrorist activities was growing exponentially, and that a task force was being assembled to deal with it, starting with organizing a conference that would include the best minds in the field of unmanned aerial vehicles, or UAVs.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I listened hard to what Doug had to say, because he was a lotto legend: a great boss, totally mission-oriented. He was reliable at whatever he chose to do, because his personality was pure elixir of competence and diligence. When people have as much of both qualities as Doug did, it’s almost impossible <i>not</i> to rely on them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Full-bore reliability is a veritable superpower. Reliable, dependable people are so consistently successful at whatever they do that their bosses automatically give them the most important jobs—and look elsewhere when they’re searching for weak links, someone to blame, or a victim to downsize.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Like almost all other competent, diligent people, Doug’s reliability in the field—free from oversight and scrutiny—invariably tipped the rules of engagement in his favor. He was so accustomed to accomplishment that he believed that virtually everything within the realm of <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_119" title="119"/>practicality could be achieved, if it was met with enough skill and sweat. Every time I saw him he reminded me of an amazing colonel I had in the marines whose motto was: <i>Never tell me no. Just tell me what it will cost me</i>.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Doug was a specialist in weapons of mass destruction, and like other WMD guys in the postmodern, less-is-more era that started after America failed to pacify the Middle East with its vast fighting forces, the specter of drones scared the hell out of him. In the wrong hands, they held the potential to terrorize and control entire populations—with a very limited investment, and terrifying drama.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Drones of up to twenty-seven feet had already been used extensively by the U.S. in the war on terror, and our UAVs had killed an estimated 2,500 people.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But China is the world’s largest drone manufacturer, and use of their UAVs was proliferating in the Middle East. ISIS and al-Qaeda didn’t have the massive drones that America deploys, but the terrorists were terminally effective with their off-the-shelf models, particularly in swarm formations that were loaded with grenade-sized munitions. These drones were as effective as their handcrafted roadside bombs: the improvised explosive devices called IEDs that had killed and crippled so many soldiers in the Iraq and Afghanistan wars. For the terrorists, drones had become IEDs with wings. Even more distressing, there was growing speculation in the press that ISIS and al-Qaeda were engaged in active measures to unleash UAV strikes on the continental U.S., potentially delivering large payloads of chemical, biological, or radiological material.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In a scenario known as a “balloon drop,” a swarm of drones can swoop down on an outdoor stadium—or any crowd—loaded with firearms, hand grenades, or ultratoxic agents, including the deadly poison ricin. Larger drones can be used as vehicles for bombs, including the “dirty bombs” that spread radioactive material.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Drones had also made punk criminals as dangerous as terrorists. Organized crime was not only thriving with the hobby-store models, but also had access to UAVs that cost $30,000 to $50,000. For a cartel, that’s chump change. But after the first meeting with George, I could <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_120" title="120"/>tell that I was going to have to give him a lot of my resources. He was out of his comfort zone and desperately needed a dose of the empowering potion of trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I had approximately the same seniority as George and could have requested a shuffle in the leadership of the team—or tried to take it often over myself. But one of the most ironic ingredients of power is that it grows faster when you let go of it, so I had no problem with letting George look like a rock star—if he was up to it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">On the brighter side, I now had a great reason to get a drone pilot’s certificate and to spend $1,500 of my own money on a DJI Phantom 4 drone: one that was typical of what some of the bad guys were already using.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was going to hit the park with my son and get some flying done. In that rush of anticipation—and the satisfaction I get from mastering a new technology—I felt like I’d taken the first step in figuring out George. He was just a hard-core tech guy with a good heart.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">The Power of Reliability</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">In many situations, reliability is virtually synonymous with trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You can love someone with all your heart and trust them with your deepest secrets, but there are countless areas in which relying upon that same person would be not just impossible, but insane. You’d never trust an airline pilot to perform your brain surgery, or trust a surgeon to fly your jet. You’ll always distrust people in more areas than you trust them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So be real. You owe it to yourself and to the people you spare from jobs they can’t do. And don’t overlook the need for both competence <i>and</i> diligence.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We all wish that we could rely upon people who are competent but not diligent or diligent but not competent. But we can’t, in the real world—and that’s the only one we have.</p>
			<h4 class="x05-Head-B"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_121" title="121"/>Competence</h4>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">During the centuries when farming and manufacturing dominated the demands for human survival, reliability was generally so easy to assess that it became a side issue in the American economy. It was simply expected<i>.</i> But in the age of information, managers consistently get fooled—often to disastrous degrees—because the internet provides numerous mechanisms for deceit and manipulation, in matters of information.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Because of this, competence management was recently isolated as a specialty and is now generally addressed by human resource analytics. Unfortunately, though, when new forms of reality checks are devised, people can find ways to subvert them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That problem places extra importance on knowing the tells of competence and diligence. People will still lie to you, but you’ll usually know it when they do.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>Bottom line:</b> Expect competence, but dive deep enough to ferret out the phonies. If you find incompetence, make sure it’s remedied, or that people are confined to tasks they are capable of completing. If you don’t have a competence management team—or even person—find one.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">There is also a classic, unchanged factor at play: the so-called “curse of competence.” Some people are actually punished for their competence, because less competent coworkers sabotage their success or dump their own work on them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Competence should be determined before someone is hired, but the conventional hiring process—consisting of a review of résumés, multiple interviews, trial tasks, and reference checks—has long been overrated as an accurate process.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Almost all applicants present their level of competence in the best possible light, and many of them inadvertently overstate it, due to lack of self-awareness, or the belief that they can learn on the job.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Résumés also frequently misrepresent competence, because past <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_122" title="122"/>employers tend to offer generous assessments, sometimes as a way to soften the blow of dismissing someone, or for their own convenience.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It is also common for highly skilled managers who are involved in the hiring process to overrate candidates, due to the false and overly optimistic assumption that their own level of competence is common, or at least attainable.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>The net effect:</b> Competence—a quality that should be easy to determine in a rational, objective way—has become a subjective, irrational popularity contest when assessed only by the conventional and antiquated methods.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Competence isn’t just a business problem. In the initial glow of friendship, people often forget that their relationships require a sufficient degree of mutual competence simply to survive in a busy, complex society in which free time exists as a zero-sum factor.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Marriages are even more dependent upon competence than friendships, because they typically include a significant degree of partnership in the practical matters of finances, household work, childcare issues, time management, social activities, legalities and ethics, and goals. Most of the behavioral literature about the causes of marital problems includes as many that are related to competence as to love.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As a rule, if people are competent in their marriages and social relationships, they also tend to be competent professionally. Partly because of this, one of the best tells for competence in both personal and professional relationships is to ask people about multiple areas of their lives—their kids, their spouses, their professions, achievements, and their friendships. This can involve relatively vague “inkblot-test” questions, along the lines of: “Tell me about some of the <i>personal</i> challenges you’ve faced.” If people start sharing their thoughts with no overt self-consciousness and with complete transparency, they are far more likely to be honest and accurate about their professional competence.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_123" title="123"/>If they’re vague or defensive, they may well be exaggerating their ability to be competent in their work.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When I’m exploring people for tells of competence, I look for <i>consistency</i> and <i>congruence:</i> consistency of statements when I ask the same question in a couple of different ways, and congruence among all answers, including those that seem to have nothing to do with work.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even though lack of competence is a dealbreaker in most issues of trust, competence without diligence can be even more insidious, because it can catch you off guard. Being able to do something is not at all the same as actually doing it. So one of the bugs in the equation of reliability is that very competent people often ride the wave of their raw skills, considering diligence to be the sad purview of the worker bees who come in at the end of a project to tie up loose ends and clean up messes.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In that situation, nobody wins, and strife can run rampant. It’s the dark side of competence.</p>
			<h4 class="x05-Head-B">Diligence</h4>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Human beings are like the <i>Titanic</i>: We’re very large ships with very small rudders. We all try to steer a straight course, but we typically have more forward motion than sure, steady guidance.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Diligence is one of our primary rudders. It guides us during days of doubt, it course-corrects when we go off track, and it contributes tremendously to the predictability that reliability creates and reflects.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Diligence consists primarily of specific, core qualities of character:</p>
			<ul>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted-First">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted-First"><b>Persistence.</b> There’s no easy way to do a hard job.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b>Motivation.</b> Your work, if your heart is in it, can live long after you die, and travel the world, and with it, a part of your heart.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b>Thoroughness.</b> If you can’t finish, don’t start.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b>Attention to detail.</b> Nobody wants to clean up after you. Not even your mom.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_124" title="124"/>An impeccable work ethic.</b> Work is the great equalizer. It exalts the humble and humbles the exalted.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted"><b>A sense of self-responsibility in all tasks, no matter how minor, or seemingly unrelated.</b> They call it due diligence because it’s <i>due—</i>not optional.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted-Last">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted-Last"><b>Consideration for others.</b> The only measure of love that is true is not how you feel but what you <i>do.</i></p>
				</li>
			</ul>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Diligence is often even more important than competence, because when diligent people aren’t competent at the task you give them, you can generally reassign them to something else. Also, when people are usually competent, but not always—which is very common—their diligence can enable them to achieve full competence.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For the most part, it’s easier for diligent people to overcome a lack of competence than it is for competent people to overcome a lack of diligence.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I love working with diligent people, because their problems don’t become my problems. They work without drama, don’t waste time, don’t allow last-minute glitches to occur, don’t play office politics, and don’t shirk responsibility.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When someone shows diligence, it’s important to mention your appreciation for that trait. The relative enthusiasm of their response will help you measure how important that quality is to them. When the work of an extremely diligent person is rewarded, their response will usually be effusive.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The reward doesn’t need to be financial, because the primary motivator of character-driven people is sincere, consistent <i>respect—</i>even more than money. The best way to reward them is to voice the importance of their value, and seek their thoughts and opinions. Talk to them in terms of their own priorities, validate them nonjudgmentally, empower them with choice, and vest in their goals.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Another way to determine someone’s relative diligence is to objectively calculate the <i>lack</i> of it in people who are performing poorly. Pollsters say that at least half of all American employees just go through <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_125" title="125"/>the motions at work. Their lack of diligence shows up in absenteeism, low energy, lack of enthusiasm, and goofing off at work. Many workers who are <i>not</i> diligent tend to be quiet and reserved, don’t form bonds with coworkers, and work robotically and dispassionately.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Interestingly, most disengaged workers tend to apply the same uninspired mentality to their leisure activities on weekends and after work.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It’s estimated that about 50 percent of workers are consistently engaged, 30 percent are only partially engaged, and 20 percent are not at all engaged in their work. Instead of working, many of these people surf the internet, shop online, socialize, play video games, look at social media, take naps, talk on the phone, job hunt, and get snacks and drinks.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That behavior costs American employers approximately 2 percent of our gross domestic product, and companies spend about 20 percent of an unreliable person’s salary to replace them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Many of the companies for whom I consult have told me that they can’t recruit an ideal workforce, so they try to make the best of the one they have, in accord with the advice of motivation guru Frank Pacetta, who created the phrase “Don’t fire people—fire them up!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Firing people up starts with figuring them out. Determine their core competencies and do what you can to revive their diligence. One of the most powerful methods for both tasks is to take a leap of trust and vest in their success. Then—<i>Doveryai no Proveryai!</i>—give them a task they should be able to do, and see if they do it. Minimize your risks, but let uninspired workers experience the intoxicating atmosphere of a trust-based environment. If they can’t make it there, they can’t make it anywhere.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That was what I did with George at our second meeting.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">George the Second</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">“Good news!” I told George. “I’ve got the sharpest guy in the UAV field for our conference.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I described the person I had in mind, who’d come to me through <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_126" title="126"/>someone I’d known for more than twenty years: a former marine, now CEO of a cryptography lab, who was one of the recruits I had long ago supervised when he was going through the boot camp hell week known as “The Crucible.” I’d witnessed the guy’s selfless acts of helping fellow recruits, and his diligence, competence, and generosity were still clear in my mind. So I was happy to accept the person he recommended, as a transfer of trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Reach out to him and see if he’ll be our keynote speaker,” George said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Roger that. But I think it would be better if <i>you</i> did. You’re the leader. You’ve got the gravitas.” That wasn’t true yet, but I was trying to make it true.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“On it!” George said. His eyes were bright with the self-respect that arises organically in people when you treat them with trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“And ask him about his colleagues,” I said, “because he’s surrounded by people who work with drones. You could probably fill your whole roster with his people.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Good find!” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The rest of the second meeting went much better than the first. George started connecting with the task force agents, listening more and talking less. When he did talk, he asked about our thoughts and opinions. I was seeing more flashes of the tells of trust than I had at the first meeting.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some people would probably think that handing off my contact would undercut my own authority in this important task force, but not me. Leadership is all about sharing power—not consolidating it—and about being a resource for the success of others on your team. People who don’t get that are sailing on the same <i>Titanic</i> as the people who think the best way to impress others is to tell everybody how great they are.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Much of the rest of the meeting was spent talking about the mechanics of funding and the technicalities of unmanned aerial guidance systems. I was able to follow it much better than before, because I’d attended a series of classes on drones, which was mostly populated by broadcast journalists, who are now using drones extensively.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_127" title="127"/>I was impressed with George’s affinity for technology. He had the mind of a scientist, which was probably part of the reason his people skills weren’t as strong as his tech skills. I believe that people with extreme gifts in one major area of the intellect, such as math and science, often have corresponding deficits in the areas at the opposite end of the spectrum, which in this case would be language and social skills.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">My own proclivity is for the soft sciences of language and social skills—and to be honest, I think that they’re actually easier than the hard sciences. That’s part of the reason I shifted my major in college from aerospace engineering to political science. But the soft sciences are not less <i>important</i>. If anything, to survive and thrive, people skills are probably somewhat more important.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">People say that life isn’t fair, but it seems pretty fair to me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The problem is people. <i>We’re</i> not fair.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN NEGATIVE TELLS FOR RELIABILITY (COMPETENCE PLUS DILIGENCE)</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. Unreliable people micromanage without providing real help. </span>Like most of the tells of trust and distrust, this can occur among people below your job status or above it, even though that’s not always apparent.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Micromanaging is usually thought of as a problem among managers, but the people they manage often turn the tables on them by invoking the expertise they’ve achieved from focusing on very specific tasks.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In either circumstance, micromanagement numbs the brains and kills the spirits of the people whose autonomy is being usurped.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. Unreliable people disappear and play hard-to-find. </span>This is very common and very destructive. The usual excuse for playing hard-to-get is that someone is just too busy to get back to you. But when good managers don’t have time to respond, they tell you right away. It takes seconds, and nobody feels slighted.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_128" title="128"/>It usually doesn’t help to confront the person, because they probably won’t give you a genuine, transparent response. We’re all busy—that’s why they call it bus-i-ness—but anybody who hides behind that excuse is looking for ways to stay out of situations they don’t understand.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. Unreliable people have chronic punctuality problems. </span>They miss planes, are late for everything from lunch to a project completion, and act as if it makes them very important—rather than a very big pain in the ass.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This is a tell of both incompetence <i>and</i> lack of diligence, a kiss of death for reliability. Competent people manage their time. Diligent people manage it even when it’s almost unmanageable. Either way, you can rely on them to get something done.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. Unreliable people grab the credit of others. </span>These people don’t have much of a work ethic, or they wouldn’t need to pretend they did the work of others. For example, about a hundred agents had a hand in the famous femme fatale spy case of Anna Chapman, and I’ve met dozens of them, and most of them took almost full credit for it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This happens most among relatively small teams—when the rest of the team isn’t around—because there are fewer people to confront.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Some people do this even when they’ve achieved their <i>own</i> assignment, and have been <i>rewarded</i> for it. These are the scary people, because they have a compulsive need to steal. They are, in effect, the kleptomaniacs of intellectual property.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. Unreliable people are frequently careless about appearance and physical presentation.</span></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This doesn’t apply to people who sincerely believe they are dressing appropriately—such as a techie in a hoodie.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It applies to people who don’t even try. It’s a sign of disrespect: for you and, more important, for themselves and their work. They <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_129" title="129"/>don’t think they’re doing well, so they act it out in an indirect, sublimated way. Their sloppy presentation is self-destructive, and you don’t want to become collateral damage.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This is a subtle tell, because the people who do it usually <i>say</i> they’re reliable, and are unaware that they’re actually revealing their disappointment in themselves.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Instead of dealing with that painful issue in a forthright way—which would open them to direct criticism—they come to work in sloppy or dirty clothes, their hygiene isn’t great, they litter, their office is a mess, they lose things, they have poor health habits, or they drive a dirty car.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">You don’t need to be Freud to see that these shortcomings subconsciously mirror their self-perception.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">What they <i>think</i> they’re saying: “I’m like Einstein—a scatterbrained genius!”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. Unreliable people depend on others to fix their problems. </span>This is another good example of fear disguised as arrogance.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People are supposed to feel reliable <i>themselves,</i> and act accordingly. That doesn’t mean that everybody can fix everything, but some people just don’t feel confident or energetic enough to finish something on their own.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When people lack a bedrock sense of self-sufficiency, they’re prone to handing off problems without even <i>trying</i> to solve them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If someone appears to overuse your various support systems, it may be the tip of an iceberg of unreliability.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. Unreliable people have a hard time picking up new concepts. </span>That doesn’t mean they’re dumb. It means their range of competence is narrow, and they haven’t done much to broaden it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The FBI, like most large organizations, offers training in many areas, so when someone lacks multiple certifications, it’s an indication that they don’t believe in their own competence, or that they lack the diligence to care.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_130" title="130"/>Most people who have a multitude of limitations have limited themselves—and if you vest your success in them, you may be limiting yourself, too.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. Unreliable people don’t keep adequate records. </span>This is one of those traits that escapes the attention of many managers, because in our fast-forward world, records are often perceived as ancient history.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Bad record keeping also creates the possibility that someone is hiding things.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">One of the most damaging aspects of this tell is bad note-taking by people in meetings, conferences, and teleconferences. By the time the damage from this surfaces, it can usually be blamed on many different issues, or on other people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Good notes are a great tell of someone’s ability and desire to excel, because they most often reflect the thoughts of <i>other</i> people—not just oneself.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Bad note-taking connotes indifference to the thoughts and opinions of those talking, and when it’s done in person, it can signify a sense of superiority.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The monstrous dictator Joseph Stalin was known for taking copious notes—and only much later was it revealed that most of them were just doodles.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. Unreliable people are more involved in planning than action. </span>Planning is great, but it’s just the beginning of a project. That should be obvious, but it isn’t, especially among people with limited attention spans.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">These people often lose interest in a project when its initial aura of excitement fades. They want somebody else to come in and do the grunt work of execution and maintenance.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People who avoid the routine chores of a project sometimes act as if they’re simply adept at delegating responsibility, but they’re more likely to just be scattered and impulsive.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">A tell behind this tell is that the people who shirk <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_131" title="131"/>responsibility typically don’t document their work. That gives them the opportunity to bail on failures.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When I first joined the Bureau, I was encouraged to write down my actions in regular reports as my operations unfolded, because that was the best way to document success or failure. My supervisor’s mantra was, “If you didn’t write it, you didn’t do it.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">I’ve stuck with that system ever since, and I insist on it in the teams I run.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. Unreliable people don’t take their own mistakes seriously. </span>They try to escape blame through various strategies—blaming others, claiming they were not warned about the cause of the mistake, or doctoring details to make the mistake look inevitable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Another very common reaction is to just minimize the mistake. If they put a decimal point in the wrong place and it costs the company a thousand dollars, they see it as nothing more than a typo, or a small loss for a big company. It’s common for people to be too critical of themselves, but also common to not be critical enough.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">Irresponsible people also often redefine their mistakes as learning experiences that were actually fortuitous.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">These were the negative tells that I looked for in my assessment of George. I found some of them, but also found some positive tells, such as him admitting that he’d made a couple of mistakes.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It’s always nice when leaders admit their mistakes, but it’s nicer when they don’t make them.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">September 2018<br/>Ahead of the Action</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">One month in, I loved working on the drone team, and felt like a reliable teammate. For me, it was the perfect mix of aeronautical technology and manpower management—two of my comfort zones—and <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_132" title="132"/>most of the time I felt like I was in front of the action, moving at my own pace, a step or two ahead of real time. That’s a magical, almost metaphysical time frame—familiar territory for reliable people—that feels great and makes things happen.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In the luxurious flow of that feeling, created by confidence and determination, you feel like you can actually see the future, for a very prosaic reason: You’re helping to create it. Most of what you do feels familiar and predictable, and you can greet each new day like an old friend.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The power of feeling reliable—magnified exponentially by your own reliance on the people around you—comes naturally to the best managers, and they pass the feeling on to the team, creating a culture of excellence, and adherence to best practices.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It didn’t come naturally to me—I had to sweat for it, but that was fine, because when I feel like I’m creating my own future, work <i>replenishes</i> my energy, instead of draining it, and good ideas seem to arrive right on schedule as my vigor and sense of accomplishment builds.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I was in a good mood when I arrived at George’s office unannounced. I hardly ever make surprise appearances, but I was having a hard time connecting with him, and wanted to share an idea: a practical way to help control and predict the criminal use of drones domestically.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Thus far, the government had tried to limit access to drones at the sourcing stage—retail sales—similarly to guns and other munitions. But I’d been thinking about the 9/11 attack and how the terrorists had trained at U.S. aviation schools. Shortly before the attack, an FBI agent had alerted the Bureau about this training, and we were in the preliminary stages of identifying the eventual hijackers, and canvassing all American aviation schools about possible use of their training programs by terrorists. We were a step too late.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Why not do it right this time? Why not get in touch with all the drone training programs in the country now and ask them to report suspicious behavior? That strategy could give us a viable choke point at the intersection of terrorists and training.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_133" title="133"/>George liked the idea, even though it would, by definition, be a people-driven operation. He promised me he’d look into it after he caught up on some paperwork.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I didn’t see the need to wait, but he was the boss. So I dropped it and asked him about the expert I had previously recommended to him. I had been talking to the guy on a regular basis and knew that George hadn’t contacted him.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Don’t worry,” George said. “It’s in the works.” But I knew it wasn’t, and it made me uneasy. The expert had two PhDs from Johns Hopkins and was a lifetime military man. We <i>really</i> needed him—and his colleagues—because George was in charge of arranging for speakers, and he hadn’t mentioned finding any.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">George was getting fidgety, and let me know that I was intruding, with his body language: quick glances at the papers on his desk and the clock on the wall, with facial expressions of impatience. But he was too polite to say anything—unless passive-aggressive nonverbals say something: which they <i>do.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He changed the subject to some of his logistical concerns—funding, coordination, time lines. Good move: for getting me to <i>leave.</i> He knew those details didn’t involve me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was getting the feeling that he felt threatened by me, as a peer with equal authority. That was not good. The last person you ever want to pose a threat to is your team leader, for the obvious reason: You can get kicked off the team—or even fired from the organization—for a strictly personal reason that will never be admitted.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That happens about a million times a day in America, and I often see the behavior that’s revealed by the fear it causes.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">America is one of the safest countries in the world—but also one of the most fearful. Its wealth is mirrored by an equal fear of losing it. There is no apparent middle ground remaining in this country, and the center, as they say, will not hold.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I fear fear. It’s the <i>real</i> root of all evil, and it’s commonly embodied by the boring, trivial cuts and jabs of office politics and petty grievances that lie in the realm of aggression that philosopher Hannah <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_134" title="134"/>Arendt called “the banality of evil.” I’m not saying George was evil. I’m simply stating the obvious: What we do in our lives affects others, and if we don’t consciously try to affect people in a positive way, our actions can easily cause a spiral of hurt that gets worse every day.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">What George had done had already negated the three signs of trust he’d displayed when I first met him, indicating, once more, that when situations change, people change.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I still liked George, but was starting to think he was just clueless when it came to people, and was therefore not competent to fulfill his role. He could have compensated for his lack of competence by being very diligent—or just asking for help—but it wasn’t happening.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When I got up to leave, though, he was genuinely warm. That was the scary part. He meant well, but he was in the vast herd of humanity that means well but just can’t do what you need them to, and often aren’t even willing to try.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I didn’t know if I could trust him anymore—at least, not with this project, nor my own future.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN POSITIVE TELLS FOR RELIABILITY (COMPETENCE PLUS DILIGENCE)</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. Reliable people carry themselves with genuine confidence.</span> It’s fairly easy to fake confidence, so behavioral analysts have learned how to identify true, unforced confidence. People who have it don’t brag, don’t make excuses, take their jobs more seriously than themselves, and get things done.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They’re calm, polite, succinct, receptive, rational, and reliable. All of that shows in their nonverbals, the language they use, the relationships they have, their patterns of behavior, and their focus on long-term goals.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">One study of top-tier managers showed that a display of genuine confidence was a key element in their success. And it was present in them <i>before</i> their success. The study also confirmed the phenomenon called “erotic capital”—the confidence enjoyed by <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_135" title="135"/>people who are notably attractive and have translated that into worldly success.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><b>My takeaway:</b> Forget about fake-it-till-you-make-it. Instead, try <i>feel</i>-it-till-you-make-it, focusing on the <i>positive qualities you already have</i>.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">I call this self-selected, legitimate feeling “quantifiable confidence,” and I’ve found that most people have enough positive attributes to sincerely project confidence—if they try.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. Reliable people speak with specifics. </span>They bring communication instead of confusion to a conversation. They’re clear, concise, memorable, and motivational—in accord with Brockman’s dictum—and their words create charisma. The things they say in important professional conversations, and in presentations, stand out in a society of big talkers who try to prove their worth with circular talk, cutesy jargon, excessive modifiers, and political correctness so thick it’s mind-numbing.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The people you trust and admire the most don’t talk like that.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Most of us know instinctually not to trust big talkers, because we don’t know where they stand, or even understand what they’ve said. That makes them unpredictable, and therefore untrustworthy—and vice versa.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. Reliable people are transparent about their weaknesses and mistakes. </span>They don’t repackage their failures as successes. They don’t pretend that their weak points have become their strengths. They’re not defensive.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Most of us love working with people like this, because their strengths and weaknesses are obvious, which is pure gold for predictability.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Reliable people don’t leave you with that hollow feeling you get when an insecure person talks. Most of the time, you can tell that insecure people don’t even know that they’re being defensive. They think they’re just putting their best foot forward.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_136" title="136"/>The rest of us think they’re hiding something, and we often underestimate how egregious it actually is.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. Reliable people welcome tough jobs and hard deadlines. </span>Heavy lifting gives reliable people the welcome opportunity to show others what they can do. These people are self-aware enough to know they’re reliable, and they like to demonstrate it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They’re also realistic enough to know that they need to keep proving themselves.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Reliable people tend to get lost in the flow state that accompanies focus, selflessness, and intensity, and they’re aware of the fact that hard work can be a form of meditation.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Also, when reliable people are held accountable, it generally just reveals how well they’ve done—so why <i>shouldn’t</i> they like it?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. Reliable people hit the ground running and then speed up. </span>People who are competent and diligent are able to do their work quickly, because they know the processes, have the tools to get it done, and feel a personal sense of urgency.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">For the <i>most</i> diligent people, one of the rewards of working fast is finishing first, and then helping others. It makes them feel good—and look good.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Work needs to be done <i>well,</i> of course, but speed always counts. It helps organizations outrun their competitors, it attracts other reliable people, it keeps people sharp, it makes time move quickly, it sets a good example, it saves the company money, and it usually becomes the new normal.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><i>Fast is good.</i></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">A quick, high-quality turnaround on a project is one of the best objective measurements of reliability. The clock doesn’t lie.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. Reliable people are inquisitive. </span>Curiosity is the spark that lights the fire of learning, and is also the soul of creativity.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Reliable people have a multitude of questions about their own <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_137" title="137"/>job, other jobs, and the industry as a whole. The knowledge they accumulate empowers them to refine their work processes to a state of optimal efficiency.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">As a rule, people who don’t ask questions are either uninterested in their work, or are afraid that their questions will make them look unsure or incompetent.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When reliable people are being interviewed for a job, they often have as many questions as answers. They’re not desperate, and want to work in a place where they’ll be happy and satisfied. So they ask—politely and transparently—about scheduling, upward mobility, benefits, company culture, the physical work environment, and other issues that will contribute to their contentment and security.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They also want to know about you and your associates, because they’re seeking a situation of mutual vesting. So once again we see the interlocking nature of our six signs for predicting people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. Reliable people accept blame graciously. </span>They know that nobody’s perfect, including them. As a rule, the only people who pretend to be perfect are those who aren’t even very good at what they do, and try to hide their insecurity behind a facade of perfectionism.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But that just makes things worse, because being a perfectionist doesn’t make a person feel perfect—since nobody’s perfect. Instead, it makes them feel even more inadequate.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Reliable people rarely get ruffled when somebody calls them out, because they almost always have a deep reservoir of goodwill, and know they’ll live to fight another day. They approach it as just another learning experience.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. Reliable people don’t measure their contributions by their sacrifices, but by their productivity. </span>They don’t talk about how hard something was, unless there’s a practical reason to mention it. They don’t complain about giving up vacation time, or meeting <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_138" title="138"/>with people they’d rather avoid, or taking on projects that don’t interest them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">As far as they’re concerned, those are <i>their</i> problems, and they know you have your own.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Many reliable people believe in working smarter, not harder—but most of them believe in working smarter <i>and</i> harder.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They keep track of their achievements, but just for their own sense of satisfaction, and if they’ve gotten certificates or plaques, you’ve probably never seen them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. Reliable people’s nonverbals remain stable, and even positive, during times of stress and strain. </span>Hard times are the best measure of a person’s competence and diligence, and during hard times, nonverbal behaviors are even more revealing than what people say, write, and do, because nonverbals are harder to hide, and harder to fake.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In times of trouble, most of us can refrain from overt acts of bad behavior, such as bullying, snapping at people, or feuding with others. In most workplaces these days, that’s simply expected, and demanded.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But highly reliable people are also able to refrain from physical responses to stress that are <i>seemingly ingrained and uncontrollable</i>, such as sweating, muscle tension, hyperventilation, an upset stomach, pacing, or cold hands and feet. It’s not easy. Those responses are all governed by the fight-flight-freeze mechanism of the autonomic nervous system, and are therefore hard to control.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But reliable people can do that, because even in a crisis, they still feel largely in control of themselves and their responses. They’re calm, reasonable, physically comfortable, and capable of focus.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Their reaction is not based upon delusions of indestructability. They face the same consequences as everyone else, but they’re confident that they’ll weather the storm—and they usually do.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">One ironic reason they succeed is because they don’t feel as <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_139" title="139"/>if they <i>need</i> to. They don’t like losing, but they don’t measure themselves by wins and losses. They measure themselves by their competence and diligence: They do their best and let the chips fall.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. Reliable people don’t have enemies. </span>They don’t need to. People with enemies are usually their <i>own</i> best enemy—because of self-doubt—and their enemies are just surrogates for themselves. If you’re competent and diligent, it’s hard to blame yourself for losing a battle. You did the best you could. End of drama.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">When you’re no longer fixated on enemies, they’re gone, but the lesson of their incursion into your life remains. You can move on with your life.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">October 2018</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">The drone conference was only a month away, and George was losing what little faith in him that I still had. He didn’t have the skills, aptitude, training, or temperament to run a team that was only partly focused on technology.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was not his fault. He was just a fish out of water.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I had given him many opportunities to display some of the tells of reliability, but he didn’t come through on most of them. The tells of positive behavior that he <i>failed</i> to fulfill included: not speaking in specifics at meetings (Positive Tell #2), not being transparent about his weaknesses (Positive Tell #3), not asking many questions (Positive Tell #6), not making his deadlines (Positive Tell #5), and refusing to take responsibility when problems were pointed out (Positive Tell #7).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He’d also been weak in so many other tells—and the data points supporting them—that I’d shifted my perspective to one of distrust and disaffiliation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That wasn’t unusual. I’ve chosen <i>not</i> to ally myself with many people, many times, because even though I try to trust people, I often deal <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_140" title="140"/>with people who don’t live up to it. It’s hardly ever because they’re rotten people. It’s just that they display too many signs of alienation and unpredictability to make me feel comfortable.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">George had already displayed several objective, evidence-based tells of distrust. He’d driven me crazy with his tendency to be unavailable (Negative Tell #2), and even when I was able to pin him down on an appointment, he was chronically late (Negative Tell #3). At our meetings, he micromanaged, without providing any value-added services (Negative Tell #1).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d tried hard to be a resource for his success, and had not given up on him easily. But this had dragged on too long.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I wanted to resign from the task force, but I’d never quit a major assignment in my life. And if I did, and it triggered other resignations, it would be a significant black mark on George’s career. It could also stall America’s response to the drone threat.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So late in the summer I gave George a last chance to get his ass in gear and call the expert I’d recommended, and to find out if his colleagues would speak. George forgot, so I gave him <i>another</i> last chance—and that brought me from summer to autumn, and led to his <i>last</i> last chance. I was still optimistic—because I hang on to that perspective like a bulldog—but also realistic, because I value rationality as much as optimism.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Robin, my friend!” George said when I showed up at his office, again unexpectedly. “Good to see you!” He meant it. I’d told him the week before that if he didn’t reach out to the expert I’d found, I would take it into my own hands, and personally deliver the good news—if there was good news.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d gotten the expert’s consent, as well as that of his colleagues, and was ready to deliver the good news. But at this point I was worried that the whole project had drifted into chaos.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When I saw George’s desk, it reinforced my distrust of his organizational skills. It looked like somebody had just emptied a wastebasket on it. I saw one document in which a banana was apparently serving as a bookmark. It was hard not to stare at it (Negative Tell #5).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_141" title="141"/>I handed George some papers from the expert, including a rough outline of the keynote address, and a list of his colleagues who had also agreed to speak. I was hoping some of them could be used as consultants and become part of the permanent national security structure.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Others, I thought, might become confidential human sources in the project I had proposed: gathering information at drone flight schools.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Recognize any names?” I asked him. Some of the people were very prominent in the area of drone regulation. I’d met one of them at a training course, along with some producers from ABC News.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">George shook his head. It was fairly obvious that he hadn’t become conversant on the issue yet (Negative Tell #7), probably because he was still focused on logistics and finance. It didn’t surprise me. At this point, nothing could.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I pointed at the top four names on a list of potential speakers. “Want a quick rundown on these guys?” I asked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Absolutely.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I started, but quickly stopped. He wasn’t taking any notes (Negative Tell #8), and I wasn’t going to waste more of my time. I knew then that this relationship was over.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You don’t seem super interested in this part of the project,” I said, as gently as possible, because I don’t have enemies, and want to keep it that way.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’m interested,” he protested, “but I’ve got people who can take care of that kind of thing. Won’t be a problem!” (Negative Tell #9.) At this point, I just wanted out.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Robin,” he said, “you’re doing a terrific job. But I think you’re overestimating some problems and underestimating others.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I nodded in agreement—to whatever <i>that</i> meant—for the sake of gracious closure.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I probably should have mentioned this,” he said, “but I got some people of my own to speak.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was wrong. He <i>could</i> surprise me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“But maybe I could squeeze your guy into a fifteen-minute slot,” he said. “I wouldn’t want to insult him.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_142" title="142"/>No, not much!</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’ll ask him about that,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But that wasn’t going to happen. What did happen was that I called up the expert, apologized profusely, and told him the truth, the whole truth, and nothing but.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">And by the end of that call, I had another solid, trust-based relationship with one of the most stable and predictable people I’ve ever known.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I resigned so quietly from the task force that I hardly noticed it myself.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was not done with the issue. It needed work, and still does. But I knew that there was a better place for me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The hard part of that was not getting there. The hard part was knowing it had to be.</p>
			<aside>
				<h3 class="x10-Sidebar-Title">DEBRIEFING</h3>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-A">CHAPTER 5: <br/>“KNOW WHO TO GO TO”</p>
				<div class="border"/>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">SIGN #3: RELIABILITY</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Quote:</b> “You can love someone with all your heart and trust them with your deepest secrets, but there are countless areas in which relying upon that same person would be not just impossible, but insane.”</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Message:</b> Reliability is virtually synonymous with positive behavior, and requires a combination of competence and diligence.</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_143" title="143"/>THE TAKEAWAYS</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Competence: The skills, knowledge, and experience that a task requires.</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">Lack of competence—and the lack of ability to quickly become competent—is typically a dealbreaker for predicting success at a task.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Competence can generally be accurately measured.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">Most people are competent in a relatively small number of difficult professional tasks.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Diligence: The character qualities—such as drive, self-responsibility, and a strong work ethic—that turn competence into action and completion.</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">Virtually anyone, with enough effort, can be diligent. But diligence is harder to measure than competence, and is easier to fake.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Approximately 50 percent of all people lack diligence, according to behavioral science polls.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">Lack of diligence destroys reliability through absenteeism, poor productivity, apathy, lethargy, and negativity.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Ten Positive Tells for Reliability (Competence Plus Diligence)</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They are genuinely confident for the obvious reason: They know what they’re doing.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They speak with specifics: clear, concise, memorable, motivational (C<sup class="Sup">2</sup>M<sup class="Sup">2</sup>).</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re transparent about mistakes and weaknesses. No sad stories.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They welcome difficulties and deadlines. They know that hard can be fun.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They work fast, finish first, and know that time is money.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re inquisitive. That’s worth its weight in platinum. Questions prevent problems.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_144" title="144"/></b>They accept responsibility—never with excuses, but sometimes with reasons.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re productive. They don’t watch the clock—they watch their production.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They display comfortable nonverbals during hard tasks.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They don’t hold grudges and don’t seek revenge.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Ten Negative Tells for Reliability (Competence Plus Diligence)</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They micromanage, without really helping.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re hard to contact—on purpose.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re chronically late: a nasty combo of lack of diligence <i>and</i> competence.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They grab credit, often so surreptitiously that they get away with it.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They have a careless physical presentation: sloppy, dirty, messy.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They make others solve the hardest problems.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They learn slowly. They don’t care.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re careless with record keeping—a great way to hide things. Or they’re just lazy.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They like planning far more than action. It’s easier.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They don’t take their mistakes seriously. Sometimes they’re not even aware of them.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
			</aside>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 13: Chapter 6: Determine the Dealbreakers: Sign #4: Actions ===

<div>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Number" id="_idParaDest-23"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_145" title="145"/>6</h2>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Title" id="_idParaDest-24">DETERMINE THE DEALBREAKERS</h2>
			<div class="box">
				<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle" id="_idParaDest-25">Sign #4: ACTIONS</h2>
				<p class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle-Para">Does this person consistently demonstrate patterns of positive behavior?</p>
			</div>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A-CO">New York, 2003<br/>Rule Number One</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">After my supervisor rejected my most recent proposal for an operation, I asked Jesse, “Do you think there’s <i>anybody</i> around here who believes in me?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I didn’t know what to do, or even how to act.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I felt free to vent my disturbed state of mind around Jesse because I knew he wouldn’t get caught up in my emotion. He’d think it through and help me find a new supervisor, or a better approach to my current one. But I didn’t expect an instant answer, because he was too smart to offer any thoughts at all until he knew exactly what my situation was—stripped of its histrionics—and how I was <i>really</i> feeling, once I’d broken free of the debris from my pity party.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So Jesse started applying the behavioral science technique of asking nonjudgmental, rational questions, called “discovery questions,” which are intended to lead people—rather than push them—toward their own <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_146" title="146"/>inner wisdom and bedrock reality, beneath their layers of defensiveness, conjecture, and insecurity.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">All these layers are founded in fear, but have different names—including perfectionism, suspicion, or status seeking—and each offers a different form of fiction.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The only antidote for them is to return to realistic thought, but that’s not as hard as it sounds, because reality is rarely as threatening as the various scenarios we conjure in our brain’s fear center, during life’s darkest hours.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse, though, thought life could be great <i>if people would just see it as it really is</i>. So he never settled for just telling people what they wanted to hear, and he didn’t take the lazy route of telling them what <i>he</i> would do in their situation. People usually tune that out, because it’s not specifically about <i>them</i>, and can come off as condescending.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He just listened carefully enough to get into people’s heads, and see what was best for them from their <i>own</i> perspective. That alone was of supreme value, because people love to be understood, even more than they love someone agreeing with them. When people calm down, he found, they can usually be trusted to do what’s in their own best interests—as <i>they</i> perceive them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">By the time Jesse was done, people would usually know not only how they really felt, but also what they really <i>thought</i>.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Those two conditions should go together, but they often don’t. Feelings are almost always attached to thoughts, but the emotions they create can be so illusory and ephemeral that rational thoughts evaporate.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In contrast, the durable product of cognitive thought—far more than feelings—is <i>the primary predictor of what rational people will do.</i> Because, of course, people usually do what they think is in their own best interests.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Knowing what’s good for you may sound easy, but I’ve long believed that it’s the hardest element to isolate in the often awkward intersection of thought and emotion. Unfortunately, when human beings start thinking about what’s best for them, they kid themselves a lot. We’re <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_147" title="147"/>often too dreamy, and call it optimism, or too fearful, and call it prudence. Then we grab an option, tack it onto the problem, and abandon rational management of the situation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then, when Plan A doesn’t work, people often just sink deeper into distrust of not only those around them, but also themselves. That’s when we start screwing ourselves up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The danger of pervasive doubt and alienation may seem obvious to you, but an astonishing number of people just don’t get it—and possibly never will—because distrust, like other forms of self-imposed isolation, is a <i>self-perpetuating</i> character flaw.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When wary people fail to find an accurate way to predict the actions of those around them, they usually don’t feel safe enough to go ahead and do what’s best for themselves. Some of them are afraid they’ll be seen as selfish, controlling, or incompetent, and others are just tired of being burned by people they once thought were allies.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even more distressing, millions of people <i>do</i> know what’s in their own best interests—and by extension, the best interests of their families, friends, and colleagues—but <i>still</i> surrender their own precious peace of mind to unfounded fears, temptations, and pressures.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When that happens, they usually fail to act on the simple but sacred honor of doing what’s best for themselves and for those closest to them. Often as not, these people do it again and again, thinking that their sacrifices will save the world—or at least <i>them—</i>if only the world would come to its senses, and revolve around them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’m not judging this behavior, because most people make these sacrifices with a good heart. But when it doesn’t work, it can harden their hearts, kill their spirits, and exhaust their bodies and minds. At that point, they need someone to help them go back to square one and make sure they still want the goals that once seemed so important.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Deciding what you want most, though, is rarely as simple as doing whatever you feel like doing. Whatever you do usually affects the people around you, and if what you do hurts them, you’ll probably create an enemy, or at least get a negative reaction. Then things get really difficult. That’s why discovery questions can be so effective. These questions are <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_148" title="148"/>general and sincere, and are not the <i>rhetorical</i> questions that manipulative people ask. Their purpose is to point the way to rational action. Common discovery questions that fit most problems are: What’s the worst-case scenario? Who might not like it? What will they do? What have I tried so far? Did those actions lead to an ultimate goal, or just an immediate goal? Have other people tried to help? What actions might make things worse?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse asked me a discovery question embedded in a sincere compliment that was too flattering to reject: “Who around here could not trust <i>you,</i> Robin?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“My supervisor. And his supervisor.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Why do you think that?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“They keep rejecting my operations. Including the current one.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“What’s their reasoning?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Too risky.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">After ten or twenty simple, straightforward questions, he knew my full context—which, as I’ve mentioned, consists of a rational, informed understanding of the <i>person</i> plus the <i>situation.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You’ve already done the best possible thing,” Jesse said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I haven’t done <i>anything.</i>”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Right! That might feel passive to you, but it’s a real aggressive way to outsmart your emotions—especially when you’re pissed off or scared. That emotional nonsense is like the fog of war. You’ve gotta let it lift before you do something. <i>Then</i> you make your move. It takes discipline to wait. But Rule Number One is: Don’t f**k yourself up. Leave that to other people. They’re not as good at it as you are. You’re <i>your own best enemy</i>.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“It’s good to be the best at something,” I said.</p>
