			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He laughed. Humor comes easily when you can see the big picture.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He was lost in thought for a couple of minutes.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he said, “You need a new supervisor<i>.</i> And I already know the guy. You do, too. Jack! Jack Johnson. We can create a new operation and invite Jack <i>and</i> your current supervisor to be in it, and you’ll get the best of both worlds.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jack, as you may remember, was the supervisor who’d wrangled the <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_149" title="149"/>bureaucracy into approving my first operation with Annan, which Jesse sometimes referred to as “preventing World War III,” occasionally quite seriously.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Jack’s a good guy,” I said, “but he wouldn’t let me work the Annan operation unless I did it with you<i>.</i> Why do you think he’d have faith in me now?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“First of all, because he <i>did</i> give you the operation,” Jesse said. “Never overlook the obvious. And the only reason he had me tag along wasn’t because he didn’t trust you. It was because he didn’t trust his own supervisor. He was working for a low-risk/no-risk guy at HQ and knew he’d have to attach a senior agent to get the Yes. But he’s perfect for you, because his motto is <i>Feed the Beast:</i> Keep supplying headquarters with operations, even when they’re risky. He’s running his own ops now, and he’s still mission-first, not career-first. He rocks the boat and doesn’t look for scapegoats. But the supervisors trust him, because he sticks to his core values, and that makes him very predictable: a known commodity. Which makes <i>them</i> look brilliant.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“So he’ll be cool with this new thing?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He nodded. “Jack makes it look easy to feed the beast. He gets a read on people from what they say; then he cross-checks it with open sources, preferably with numbers. By then he’s a mind reader. He does it with his supervisors, and he’ll do it with you.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Okay,” I said, somewhat tentatively.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Oh, plus—he’s hit lotto eighteen times.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Sold!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Within a week, Jack had reengineered my operation, softening the risks that management had seen, but still keeping me as the lead agent—a brilliant bureaucratic feat that kept everyone happy, including me. He remembered me well from the prior operation, and knowing my past patterns of behavior played a critical role in his willingness to trust me in this new role.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i>Actions matter.</i> More than anything else. For good or ill, it’s your actions that define you, much more obviously and accurately than what you say or how you feel.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_150" title="150"/>Troublesome words and feelings come and go, but deals die when bad things actually occur, in the real world, and in real time<i>.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">More than any other form of self-expression, actions are viable <i>dealbreakers.</i> They’re not just a thought. They’re <i>what happens.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i>When</i> they happen can also make a big difference. Past actions don’t always affect the present, but current actions usually do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The operation I wanted to run would, I thought, result in major actions at the international level. Jesse liked it, I liked it, and Jack liked it. The prior rejections suddenly looked like the screwups of timid bureaucrats, so the other supervisor went along.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The mission was to recruit a Russian target who was a former Soviet general, with the help of an American CHS who’d been a prominent marine colonel. They were both public figures, and the patterns of their past and current behaviors were largely open-source information. I’d only talked to the colonel briefly, and had never spoken to the Russian, but I already felt as if I knew both of them well.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Not incidentally, the target was now a major player in Russian-American diplomacy. Recruiting him, at a time when Vladimir Putin was beginning to look like a modern-day czar, would be like hitting Powerball lotto.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Best of all, the general and the colonel were old friends. Now, you tell me: How could a setup like that <i>ever</i> get rejected?</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">The Power of Character</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">What people do—including what they’ve done consistently in the past—greatly heightens their predictability, because it reveals the rock-hard core of their character, consisting of their primary principles and values.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Because many different principles and values compose character, most people consider some elements of character to be more important than others, and they hold those qualities closest. For example, some <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_151" title="151"/>people might be more concerned about being honest than being humble, or more dedicated to being kind than being authentic.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I always try to get a read on the values that people hold dear to their hearts—their core values—because they’re a window into someone’s inner self, and are the traits most likely to influence people, and to remain steadfast even when circumstances change.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Quite often, a person’s character is forged by the philosophical and moral codes that they adopted many years earlier, often in adolescence or early adulthood, as they created their own unique codes of character.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In a sense, therefore, what people will do has already been determined by choices they made long ago. As the preeminent Stoic philosopher Epictetus said, “It is your own convictions that compel you: that is, choice compels choice.” That’s why it’s important to evaluate people by the entirety of their lives, in addition to how they’ve behaved in the more recent past. To a large extent, we become what we have long intended to become.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Placing importance on character is very consistent with the concept that emotionally healthy people usually do what they believe is in their own best interests, because someone’s best interests almost always include adhering to their own codes of conduct. When they violate them, they usually feel guilty and regretful, and fear that their violations will not only stunt their personal growth and self-esteem, but also their acceptance by others. This attachment to character makes people much easier to predict, and therefore easier to trust with your own fate.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Codes of morality also often come from various religions and philosophies, and most of them include some qualities of good character that are virtually universal. These include honesty, loyalty, kindness, humility, respect, and integrity.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The nobility of these qualities is practically inarguable. Therefore, they are the primary and sometimes sole factors that people consider when they assess trust. Some people simplify the process by trusting someone based only upon their particular religion—especially if they share it—but that’s risky, because merely belonging to a particular <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_152" title="152"/>faith does not automatically grant good character. Even so, looking for these bedrock qualities in other people is one of those fundamental, basic acts that allow us to accurately evaluate them, and assign a realistic reputation to them. Despite all its shortcomings, it’s one of the valid metrics for assessing trust and predicting what people will do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As always, though, in the soft science of behaviorism, this rule is rife with exceptions and contradictions. Human behavior always comes back to two hard truths: 1) we’re only human, and 2) nobody is perfect. Rules, therefore, are only as perfect as the people who apply them, and character can be surprisingly malleable, especially when it’s not in the category of a <i>core</i> character.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Behavioral studies indicate that people with impeccable behavior in the past often fall short of their prior good behavior. Even though people don’t change their core personality very often, they certainly behave differently from time to time, in ways that seem almost random, unless you pay close attention to them. If you do, you often find that they think their environment has changed and that they need to adjust. That can happen often, and quickly, because most environments are in a constant state of flux.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The changes that affect character most, as I’ve mentioned, are threats and temptations, which sometimes occur simultaneously.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Past patterns of honesty and fairness have also been shown to crumble when people believe that they can indulge in their actions anonymously, or remain free from consequences.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We see that happen repeatedly in two of the professions that are magnets for power: politics and business management. This common phenomenon is often called the “power paradox.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Conversely, people who know that they will be held responsible for their actions are those who are most likely to adhere to their past patterns of positive behavior. But if the situation changes, they might, too. That phenomenon seems to be somewhat common among the trusted people who become embezzlers, or among attorneys who know how to skirt the law.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Another frequently cited element of good character is loyalty, but <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_153" title="153"/>this is one more moral positive that may simply be a matter of expedience.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">A history of honesty is usually considered one of the most valuable indicators of strong character and trustworthiness, but honesty sounds far simpler than it is. Many professional people become adept at telling the truth, but rarely the whole truth.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Honesty can also be used as a weapon, especially when someone delivers an insult prefaced by the phrase, “I’m just being honest.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The same factors that complicate honesty also obscure integrity. Integrity is a fine quality, but it, too, can become twisted to the point that it becomes destructive. People often wield the tool of integrity to attack people they oppose.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Authenticity, too, is a precious quality, but negative aspects of one’s character can be quite authentic, and still be inappropriate or even indefensible.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Another gray area of authenticity occurs when people take out their anger on others by saying that they’re just “being themselves” or “calling it like they see it.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Despite all these incursions on character, though, most people try extremely hard to adhere to their core, central values.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>The takeaway:</b> Patterns of behavior, especially over a long period of time, define people’s character, and <i>character counts</i>—even as an imperfect measure. That’s why action is one of only six signs of sizing people up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So be generous to people who may have been coerced or otherwise dragged into questionable behavior. But be careful. And be just as careful of people who have sterling reputations.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i>Doveryai no Proveryai!</i></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN NEGATIVE TELLS FOR PATTERNS OF ACTIONS</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People are not fully transparent about their pasts. </span>Many people have a “secret spot”: an event or act that they try to hide, by <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_154" title="154"/>ignoring part of their work history, or by skating past certain questions in their initial job interviews, or in general conversations.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The degree of gaps in time and information generally indicates the importance of the secret spot, and people with significant secrets just aren’t predictable enough to be trustworthy.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If they’re open about it, though, that’s different. Transparency solves innumerable problems of prediction.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. People treat those with power better than those without it.</span></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">There is only one “real” side to this kind of person: the rude one. Bad behavior is almost always controllable, if the person <i>wants</i> to control it. So sucking up to the boss doesn’t count.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Be careful of these people, because sycophants are dangerous to everyone they work with. Their behavior is frequently used to become the boss’s pet, and they’ll throw you to the wolves, and never think twice about it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. People have gaps or exaggerations on their résumés. </span>It sounds like a dealbreaker, but sometimes it’s not.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Business culture, for good or ill, now often tolerates a modest degree of gold-plating, so some applicants feel that their mild misstatements amount to little more than putting their best foot forward.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Two things control the fib: the <i>degree</i> of misinformation, and how the person <i>responds</i> to being questioned about their misstatement. If they’re okay on one or both, give them a break. But keep your eye on them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People regard their competitors as enemies, and attack them whenever possible. </span>Dealbreaker. This pattern of behavior appeals to a specific type of gung-ho, take-no-prisoners operator. It can be seductive, because there is a hard edge to business that must be faced. But it’s juvenile behavior that they should have outgrown. Competition does not demand anger.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_155" title="155"/>The same people might soon be taking out their anger on you. For innately hostile people, it’s all about control.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Find people who have friends throughout the industry.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People have a history of irregular behavior patterns in their personal lives. </span>This could include multiple divorces, family feuds, minor legal or ethical matters, or numerous failed friendships. These are the kinds of nonwork issues, once hidden from employers, that are now on full display on social media.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Everybody has problems, but usually just troublemakers have lots of them. That’s often an indicator of wholesale unpredictability. Take a pass.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. You feel like you don’t know someone, even after close, prolonged contact. </span>It may not reflect their core personal qualities at all—but it still leaves you with a lack of information. And information—not intuition, and not affection—is the foundation of all predictability.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Ask them specific questions that are neither prying nor accusatory. If you can’t get straight answers, you’ll never really know what makes them tick.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It’s a dangerous situation—and could bring unwanted surprises.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. People are tight-lipped about past colleagues. </span>What are they hiding? Whatever it is, it’s keeping you from really knowing them—and that’s a dealbreaker.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Predicting people is all about <i>knowing them.</i></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If they don’t want you to know what other people think about them, you’ll never know what to expect.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Maybe they don’t want to talk about their former colleagues because they don’t trust them. But that’s <i>your</i> call, not theirs.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. People are critical about their colleagues’ personal lives. </span>It’s usually irrelevant. And it’s creepy. It’s especially creepy when they criticize people who’ve left the company.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_156" title="156"/>You can’t help but wonder what they’ll say about you someday.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">You need to align yourself with people who stick to <i>facts—</i>not feelings, not hearsay—and who know what matters and what doesn’t.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Even if what they’re saying is interesting, don’t feed into it. Anybody can tell a good story, but sometimes that’s all it is: a <i>story.</i> Don’t be a sucker for it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. A person’s behavior is often inconsistent. </span>Another dealbreaker. This is bad-news behavior, since consistency creates predictability, and predictability creates trust and effectiveness.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Inconsistency, and the drama it creates, sometimes has a certain romantic, devil-may-care panache to it, but it’s the worst possible trait at crunch time. In a crisis, it can be the kiss of death.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. It’s hard for someone to accept responsibility, especially for major mistakes. </span>This is so common—and so sad—because it almost <i>never</i> works. When was the last time you saw somebody actually beat the rap by stonewalling?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This behavior is founded in insecurity, though, so it can be addressed, and often reversed, by assuring people of their security and cultivating a blame-free culture around them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">But when people cling to their defensiveness after you do your best to make them feel safe and appreciated, be careful. They don’t trust you, so you can’t trust them.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">New York, 2003<br/>Feed the Beast</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">When Jack gave me the go-ahead on my diplomatic operation, I started surveillance of the Russian general, looking for patterns of behavior that would add to our understanding of his character and his past. It <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_157" title="157"/>would give us an update on his current priorities, reveal the optimal environment in which to approach him, and provide us with a good conversation starter.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was hoping that he realized that his own future, and that of his family, was now aligned more with the United States, and the West in general, than with Putin and his oligarchs. I thought he’d see it that way—but, as always, what I thought came from my <i>own</i> context, and his was quite different. We wouldn’t know how he felt until we launched the operation—or, as we put it, bought our lotto ticket. You can’t win if you don’t play.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In all honesty, the operation wasn’t likely to succeed. <i>Most</i> of our operations didn’t hit lotto. In that regard, we were similar to so many industries that revolve around ideas, research, intellectual assets, and other intangible products and services. So we had to perceive success as just putting together a great operation. If we thought we had to hit lotto every time, we’d never do anything.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The only option we ever have in counterintelligence is to buy the ticket, take the ride, roll the dice, and see what happens. That’s a reasonable stance, but only if we made rational decisions and took reasonable actions.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Despite that, this would probably be the last time, for the foreseeable future, that I’d have the opportunity to buy my lotto ticket.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If I failed to recruit the general, but he stayed quiet about it, I’d probably survive the blowback. But if the general carried our incursion to the top of Russia’s grievance list, my reputation—and Jack’s—would take a terrible and possibly terminal hit. In counterespionage, your reputation is your brand. The same is true in most professions.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was just hoping that the bond between the general and the American colonel superseded the demands of politics. But Russia had its own beast to feed—and, God knows, it loved trouble.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I enlisted the help of several sharp agents, and a CHS who had been working Russia with me for some time. They were all comfortable with my aggressive tempo, and we moved fast.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The more we learned about the general, the more optimistic I <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_158" title="158"/>became. He was definitely a member of the Russian military intelligence, the GRU, but he wasn’t a typical one, because he was also a gifted diplomat and general line officer.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Although movies don’t portray the bureaucratic aspect of international espionage, the standard GRU officer is very similar to any big-business careerist, wherever they may be. They tend to be very risk-averse, focusing on mere survival until they reach positions so lofty that they’re protected by scapegoats, money, and institutional rules. Then they become even more risk-averse, because they don’t want to lose the good thing they’ve got.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">They’re also generally micromanagers, because they don’t trust the people working under them to be as cautious as they are.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Another trait of me-first bureaucrats—in the government and out—is being clock-watchers who are more interested in getting through the day than in cranking out achievements.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Careerists are also savants at sucking up to people above them for protection and advancement, and being hard on people beneath them, so they can offer them up as pre-certified sacrificial lambs when the occasion demands.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">They’re also name-droppers, and are adept at scooping up credit and shifting blame.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">They’re even good at acting confident, especially when they feel insecure—which is most of the time—so they tend to adorn their offices with a Wall of Ego, comprised of photos of them with major players, and certificates of merit.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In meetings, the company guys usually remain quiet while others voice controversial ideas, but are gung-ho as soon as the boss gives the Go.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">All in all, they’re usually not the kind of people with whom you can develop predictable, positive relationships. These people climb with sickening regularity to the top of the pyramid, where they blight the lives of those beneath them with their insecurity, inaction, secrets, and infighting.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As our information about the general piled up, I wasn’t seeing <i>any</i> of these tells.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The traits were also absent from the character of our guy, the colonel.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_159" title="159"/>I could see how they might become friends.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The American colonel was a hard-core patriot, and from my multiple contacts and multiple meetings, I got the impression that he had a certain nostalgia for the bad old days of armed conflict and heroic action, and was itching to do something important—even if it carried the danger that always accompanies counterespionage activities against Russia.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The colonel’s military record showed that even though he was as mission-oriented as a marine rifleman, he was also schooled in the manners of diplomacy, having spent the last years of his service as a military diplomat at a European embassy. He was there at the same time that the Russian general was working at <i>his</i> country’s embassy. So they’d met at a number of international social events, and had become friendly rivals, each more vested in the achievement of personal honor and world stability than in political gain.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That quality of character is relatively common among career military people. Officers from nations all across the world gather together in these foreign posts and ride out invasions, trade wars, treaties, coalitions forming and fading, and presidents coming and going—but throughout the ebb and flow of history, they toast the same toasts, drink the same drinks, and talk the same talk, forming allegiances that often blur strict adherence to their own country’s policies.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If this particular American colonel couldn’t get me a meeting with the Russian general, nobody could.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">After about a month of surveillance, we found out that the general would soon be soon traveling to Las Vegas with his wife on a junket that was more play than work. I wanted Vegas to be our ground zero for the meet-up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Our narrative was that the colonel happened to be in Vegas at the same time as the Russian general: small world! But not so small that the general wouldn’t suspect something. That was fine. We wanted him to think about his options before he even knew he had them, and let his optimism inflate them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Near the end of their short mutual stay, the colonel would schedule a couple of events, like drinks in the late afternoon, then dinner with <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_160" title="160"/>the wives. After dinner, the colonel would say that he had a friend in special services who had great access to New York’s Thanksgiving Day parade. The friend—me, as you’ve probably guessed—had invited the colonel to come and told him to bring a couple of guests.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The general would be smart enough to decode the rest.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I took every step of the op to Jack, and to the whole team. The team was functioning flawlessly, and everybody was great. Smart questions, but not too many. Excitement. Eagerness. Total transparency about Jack’s approval process.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At this stage of his career, Jack’s approvals had to come from national headquarters, at the J. Edgar Hoover Building in Washington, D.C. That made things harder, because Washington has a way of turning character upside down, and making people greedy for the glamour and power of the place. But Jack never mentioned the pressure he was under, and he was oblivious to glamour.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">On an almost daily basis, Jack was giving me a tell of trust—similar to the following ones—and my morale was soaring. He could see my satisfaction, and he fed off it. So did our team. I was still working closely with Jesse, along with a crew of people who were so good that I called them the Magnificent Seven. With Jack leading this operation, it felt like I was finally where I belonged.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The patterns of behavior from all the people involved were coalescing into a finely crafted mosaic.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I could smell my first lotto.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN POSITIVE TELLS FOR PATTERNS OF ACTIONS</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People remain loyal to you when others are irrationally critical of you. </span>It’s easy to be loyal to people at the top of the food chain, but that’s not really loyalty—it’s just survival. When someone chooses to <i>suffer</i> from being on your side, you know that instinct comes from deep within, and is probably a core element of their character.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_161" title="161"/>One of the best things they can offer you is an objective appraisal of why you’re being unjustly criticized. A realistic fact of life is that when somebody has a problem with you, they usually have a fairly good reason. That’s a hard truth, but if you accept it, you’ll be astounded at how it can streamline your conflicts.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">There are people in the world who are simply and solely destructive, but you probably don’t work with one. Find the truth in a criticism, and you’ll grow in the eyes of everyone.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This doesn’t make you a pushover. It makes you somebody who’s wedded to rational thought: a hallmark trait of positive, effective people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. When you ask someone for details or documentation about a problem, they provide it immediately. </span>Many people stall, and try to present something that lets them off the hook. They comb their records for the documents that show them in a good light. Or they look for excuses. If there’s a delay, you’ll never really know.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">And when you don’t know, you can’t predict. When you can’t predict, you’ve got an even bigger problem.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. When someone repeats a story about themselves or one of their actions, it never varies. </span>But when somebody changes major components, they’re probably not very honest. Law enforcement and national security officials commonly depend on this phenomenon in determining the truth.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Consistency means predictability, and predictability lies at the heart of trust.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People opt out of conversations when they degenerate into gossip. </span>Helpful, dependable people don’t find amusement in other people’s problems.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_162" title="162"/>Insecure people, on the other hand, <i>need</i> that vicarious thrill, because of their own self-doubt. They have a deep-seated fear that they are not good enough, or don’t have enough, and actually enjoy hearing about the foibles of others.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The only thing that good allies want from those around them is healthy, happy relationships. It’s not very titillating—but it makes friends, and makes money.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People are never critical about their family members, even when it would be justified. </span>A common behavior I’ve never understood is how a husband (or wife—it goes either way) thinks he can build himself up by putting his wife down. All it makes me think is: <i>You’re</i> the one who married her, so what does that make <i>you</i>?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Being critical is one of the cruelest ways of being judgmental, and judgmentalism is poison for a positive situation. It makes almost everybody wonder what they’re being judged about, and scares them away.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If you’re willing to judge your own spouse, everybody must be fair game.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. People avoid hot-button issues, such as politics or religion, with people they don’t know well. </span>This is one manifestation of a tremendously important principle of positive behavior that I’ve mentioned: <i>seeking context</i>.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">We all have our own complex, multifaceted context, and when someone understands it, they usually understand us. When they don’t, understanding evaporates, judgment begins, and allegiance is lost.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">That’s why effective people tread lightly around sensitive issues when they’re with someone they don’t know well. Their goal is to have healthy relationships, and that starts with making people feel accepted and comfortable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_163" title="163"/>7. People display consistent personalities in their professional and personal lives.</span> Like other self-evident truths, this one is so obvious that it often gets overlooked. In matters of core values, there can be no dichotomies. Self is self.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Consistency = Predictability = Trust = Success.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. People sometimes cite moral or philosophical obligations, including honesty and fairness, in business situations. </span>You can usually trust people who aren’t afraid to admit that there are more important things than making money.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">These people tend to be successful, partly because people trust them. Some people think that’s ironic. I don’t.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. Someone’s social media postings reflect the same personality that you see in their professional life. </span>This is the e-version of personal and professional consistency. And thank God for the e-version: It’s accessible, it’s durable, and its ubiquitous.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Social media is the lie detector of the new millennium.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. After you hire someone, they actually have the skills they said they did during the hiring process. Or, after you get hired, your bosses do exactly what they said they would.</span></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Nothing is a better tell of trust than patterns of honest behavior.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">No boss likes to hear: “I guess I didn’t know as much about this as I thought—<i>but I’m a fast learne</i>r!”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">No new employee likes to hear: “We need to restructure your hours and responsibilities.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">These statements are especially frustrating when they come very shortly after hiring, because it implies that somebody was lying all along. By then, it’s usually too late to do anything.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">Nothing is more gratifying than seeing someone do exactly what they said they would. It gives you faith in them—and faith in yourself, too, for making them an ally.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_164" title="164"/>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>Now ask yourself:</b> Have you recognized any of the tells of trust-based behavior among the people you work for, and those who work for you? Does one person stand out?</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">April 2009<br/>Hillary’s Damn Button</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">In hindsight, the Vegas op was too good to be true—but only by the measure of its outcome. Outcome is huge, of course. But the more I learn about human behavior, the more I see that outcomes can be measured not just by money, or hitting a political target, but also by coalescing with the right people. Putting together my team ultimately brought me far more tangible gain than hitting lotto would have. I kept most of the core players with me, and we hit some major targets that I’ll tell you about later. Some of them even followed me to the Behavioral Analysis Program. There’s no way I would have traded that for hitting lotto.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Here’s how it went down, short version: The colonel made the parade pitch and the general said he would think about it overnight. The next day, he told the colonel that he had “seen enough parades in my life”—a response that had nothing to do with parades and everything to do with his loyalty to Russia. They shook hands and parted.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I waited several weeks for the storm to hit.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It never did. The general’s loyalty to his country was apparently tempered by his loyalty to his friend, whom he did not rat out (Positive Tell #1). So I won the consolation prize. We’d run a tight op with a solid fail-safe mechanism—and in the Bureau, that’s considered a successful mission.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I worked on many more operations with Jack over the next few years, then had an opportunity to move to the FBI’s Norfolk Field Office, where the Chesapeake Bay hits the Atlantic. For me, it was a <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_165" title="165"/>dream destination. My treacherous commute from the suburbs to Manhattan had been killing my family time, and I had two young children who loved living near the beach, with Dad home most of the time. I knew Jack would approve of my move, because he had kids of his own, and applied the same dedication and nurturing to them that he’d shown everyone at work (Positive Tell #7).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Despite his best efforts at home, though, Jack had pressures similar to mine, and two of his kids were acting out their frustration. One of them caused a serious problem, but Jack refused to judge his child. Instead, he got a transfer to FBI Headquarters in Washington, D.C., which was far more conducive to a good family life (Positive Tell #5).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">And a few years later, he found an opening for me on his new team. I couldn’t resist working at the epicenter of American law enforcement. We moved to D.C. That was when my troubles started.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I came up with a hot op. It had lotto written all over it. One of Russia’s richest oligarchs, who had a line straight to Putin, was having visa problems. I wanted to help him “solve” them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But Jack didn’t seem impressed. He was clearly different in D.C.—less opinionated, more conservative, less flexible. He was still pleasant with me, but distant. We shared some very enjoyable lunches, but not much real contact. Mostly, there was way less of it. He was cocooning socially, so at least he was consistent (Positive Tell #9).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was like night and day, and I didn’t know why.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then Secretary of State Hillary Clinton went to Russia with a gimmicky red “reset” button that symbolized State’s desire for a new, improved relationship between the two countries.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That same day, Jack sent me a detailed memo explaining why my operation was contrary to the red-button détente, which he’d known about for weeks (Positive Tell #2).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I appreciated the information. But it wasn’t great news for our Russia team.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I went to his office the next day, where he reiterated the whole story, exactly as he’d laid it out in the memo, clearly relieved that he no <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_166" title="166"/>longer had to keep the secret of the red button (Positive Tell #3). He said the worst part of his new job was having to keep things confidential from virtually everyone he worked with on a daily basis, which was different from keeping secrets <i>within</i> your own team. He equated it to lying (Positive Tell #8). There was truth in that, but I assured him that I understood the necessity for secrecy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He said that the only thing he liked about working at headquarters was that people were scrupulously careful about offending others—which some might call political correctness—and that, contrary to the movies, there was a dearth of gossip, at least in the Hoover building (Positive Tells #6 and #4).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I told him I was grateful for his honesty and that I’d been as uncomfortable with the deceitful element of the deep-dive subterfuge as he’d been (Positive Tell #10). That may sound like an odd conversation between two career spies, but in the final analysis, espionage and counterespionage in the twenty-first century is just another business. (Also there’s now almost as much spycraft in private industry as there is in government.)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I realized that Jack’s core values were intact, and that he hadn’t really changed. He was just dealing with a new context that consisted of a much broader picture, consisting of not just the FBI, but the State Department and the White House.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Now he was protecting the people under him by protecting himself. And he was also protecting us from <i>ourselves,</i> and our sometimes dangerous dreams of glory.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Once again, I’d learned the stark limitations of judging human behavior with such binary qualities as good and bad.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was clear, though, that after all my years of working Russia, my career had just hit a speed bump—about three feet tall. Jack mentioned that there was a major opening in the Behavioral Analysis Program.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was for the position of director.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Think you might be interested in that?” he asked.</p>
			<aside>
				<h3 class="x10-Sidebar-Title"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_167" title="167"/>DEBRIEFING</h3>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-A">CHAPTER 6: <br/>“DETERMINE THE DEALBREAKERS”</p>
				<div class="border"/>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">SIGN #4: ACTIONS</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Quote:</b> “For good or ill, it’s your actions that define you, much more obviously and accurately than what you say or how you feel.”</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Message:</b> To predict what someone will do, actions—past and present—must be analyzed objectively, systematically, and rationally, because people frequently change, and situations always change.</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">THE TAKEAWAYS</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First"><b>Patterns:</b> Past patterns of honesty and fairness can sometimes crumble when people are no longer held accountable, due to power, wealth, or anonymity.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Character:</b> Actions are the most tangible, observable, and measurable indicator of a person’s character. That’s primarily why action is one of the six signs for behavior prediction.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last"><b>Morals:</b> A strong moral code heightens predictability, but can change over time.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-Head">Ten Positive Tells for Patterns of Actions</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They remain loyal while others are irrationally critical.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They fulfill documentation requests immediately.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_168" title="168"/>Their versions of stories never change.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t participate in gossip.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t complain about their families.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They avoid divisive topics.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Their work personality is essentially the same as their home personality.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They sometimes cite moral obligations in business situations.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Their social media is consistent with their professional life.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They live up to what they said during the hiring process.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Bulleted-Head">Ten Negative Tells for Patterns of Actions</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They are not fully transparent about their past.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They treat powerful people better than others.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They have gaps or exaggerations on their résumés.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They regard their competitors as enemies.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They have a history of irregular behavior in their personal life.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">You feel like you don’t know them, even after close, prolonged contact.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They are tight-lipped about past colleagues.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They are critical about their colleagues’ personal lives.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Their behavior is often inconsistent.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">It’s hard for them to accept responsibility.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
			</aside>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 14: Chapter 7: Listen for the Reveal: Sign #5: Language ===

<div>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Number" id="_idParaDest-26"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_169" title="169"/>7</h2>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Title" id="_idParaDest-27">LISTEN FOR THE REVEAL</h2>
			<div class="box">
				<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle" id="_idParaDest-28">Sign #5: LANGUAGE</h2>
				<p class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle-Para">Does this person know how to communicate in a positive way? Or do they talk trash?</p>
			</div>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A-CO">September 17, 2018, 4:00 p.m.<br/>Corporate Spooks</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Our plane banked low over the San Francisco Bay, and it was so late in the day that only the triangular tip of the glassy Transamerica Pyramid was still glinting like gold in the sun. That was appropriate: I was here for money.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">More specifically, I was here to find another job, because I was set to retire from the FBI in a matter of weeks. I was looking for ways to apply my expertise in intelligence to the private sector, and a few weeks earlier I’d gotten an email from a division head of a prominent corporate security firm based in S.F. He invited me to consult for a couple of days, in what appeared to be a tryout for full-time employment.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The pay would be great, but that was not what I was excited about. I’d been extremely impressed by the division head’s email, because it was incredibly grounded in the principles that had guided my years of <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_170" title="170"/>work in the Behavioral Analysis Program, and had blossomed into my system for sizing people up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In less than 150 words, he’d touched on some of the most basic pillars of positive, sane, trustworthy behavior.</p>
			<div role="list">
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered-First" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">He sought my thoughts and opinions.</b> That was the whole point of his email. In short notes, people tend to start most of their sentences with “I.” His was all “you.”</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">He validated my context.</b> He indicated that he understood my style of operating in an industry similar to his. Understanding is a powerful quality, and is not mere flattery. Flattery tends to be broad, and is often done to manipulate, but validation is much more specific and is done to find common ground.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">He spoke in terms of my priorities.</b> He wanted to know what my top interests were, and how I might be able to benefit from his firm.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered2" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">He empowered me with choice.</b> He left the timing, the agenda, and the future of our relationship up to me.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered-Indent" role="listitem">The content was excellent, because the absolute essence of great communication is to focus on the other person, instead of yourself.</p>
				<p class="x07-List-Unnumbered" role="listitem"><b class="BOLD-Sans">He was rational and reasonable.</b> Those two qualities should always be synonymous—but they’re not: Somebody can very rationally suggest something that’s unreasonable. And that can sometimes result in the negative definition of “rationalizing”—as in “manipulating facts to support an idea.” He did neither. He was straight-up facts.</p>
			</div>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Also, his <i>presentation of those concepts</i> was perfectly consistent with their content. That combination comprises what I call the language of trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Whenever I hear it, I feel a kindred spirit calling out to me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">This guy sounded like a dream boss.</p>
			<h4 class="x05-Head-B"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_171" title="171"/>Trusting Your Boss</h4>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Having a boss you can trust with your ideas, desires, and financial future can make almost any job great. But enduring a tyrant you don’t trust and respect—and who doesn’t trust and respect you—can be slow professional suicide.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For that matter, it’s hard to robustly trust <i>anyone</i> who has direct power over you, because too much is at stake to be complacent about it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We all know that supervisors have more to worry about than just <i>our</i> welfare and that their major concerns revolve around their own well-being. Besides, it’s equally hard for <i>them</i> to trust <i>you—</i>a situation that’s a setup for reciprocal suspicion.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If you don’t trust your supervisors, your doubt can become a self-fulfilling prophecy, even if you try to hide it. Most bosses take lack of trust as an insult, and often conclude that if you’re worried about them, you must have a good reason to be. Then you’ve got a real problem.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But when you apply a rational system to your assessment of their behaviors, you can suspend the emotionalism that clouds your perceptions—and you’ll often find that you really <i>do</i> trust them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Once you find a way to trust your managers, it puts you in a firm position to build healthy, mutually beneficial relationships with them. When that happens, you can begin to transform them from being supervisors to mentors, and eventually even peers.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As you rise, with their blessing, it’s easier for you to see the broad, big-picture perspective that bosses are generally required to have. With that perspective, you won’t become emotionally hijacked if your boss needs you to make a sacrifice for the benefit of your company.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Of course, in the real world, there are plenty of lousy bosses. Some of them try to limit your success on purpose, either to elevate themselves, to take credit for something you did, or to use you as a scapegoat. The typical reaction to that almost impossible situation is to rebel against it, try to escape, or just cower and do nothing. It’s pure essence of fight-flight-freeze—but only if you <i>allow</i> it to be.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If, on the other hand, you can stay rational and recognize the bosses’ <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_172" title="172"/>limitations as trustworthy, generous people, you can predict these kinds of problems and not be caught off guard. You’ll probably be rewarded for not overreacting, and your mature response might turn a negative into a positive.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">September 17, 2018, 5:00 p.m.<br/>Silicon Valley</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I’d started the day on the East Coast by giving a presentation shortly after dawn, and I looked forward to cruising an Uber to the luxury hotel that the company had booked for me, eating a pricey (nongovernmental) room service dinner, and crashing for the night.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But when I got out of my gate, I saw a guy holding a placard with my name, and he told me the division head was waiting in a car at the curb. Felt good. The guy cared enough to drop me at the hotel personally.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He hopped out of his car to greet me, flashing a bright, dentally perfected smile, and gave me a hundred-dollar handshake: perfect firmness and timing, with the hint of a two-handed grasp. It’s a minor but pleasing nonverbal, because it says somebody is at least trying. And he had a good car: a rugged, six-figure Mercedes AMG that screamed money and muscle.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He was well barbered and wearing a cashmere Armani sport coat, with jeans that probably cost about the same. In corporate intelligence, all the players wear money and drive money, because money attracts money, within the company and outside it. In the top private security corporations, you always see glitzy watches and flashy suits—it’s all about the bling. But these days, you also see no tie, no jacket, six-figure sneakers, and a designer shirt with the tail out, intended to say Rich but Young.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As I settled in for the ride south to Silicon Valley, he invited me to call him not just by his first name, but by his nickname.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But for the sake of privacy protection, let’s call him Mr. X—a name that reveals nothing, similar to the standard FBI code for an unnamed country, Erehwon, which is “nowhere” spelled backward.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_173" title="173"/>“You haven’t told me what department you manage,” I said. I hadn’t seen anything about him online.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He smiled warmly, seeming to luxuriate in the safety of secrecy, and then said, “Murders and Assassinations.” He meant mergers and acquisitions, and narrowed his function to chief of security.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That was why he’d been so cryptic. A blockbuster global merger was unfolding, and he had either wanted me to know he was involved, or <i>didn’t</i> want me to know. The companies in play were not household names, but they were both bigger than either of the companies in a recent mega-merger: Amazon and Whole Foods.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Every Fortune 500 corporation has its own team of merger analysts, who pore over the spreadsheets and databases, but even hard numbers are tough to trust at the monolithic level, due to the wizardry of corporate accountants. So the multinationals hire corporate security specialists for their M&amp;A division, to look for the proverbial dirt under the fingernails of the company they’re targeting for a takeover.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">His department’s job would be to pick up raw data and scraps of facts that revealed hidden weaknesses or strengths that the other company had “overlooked.” But since the fine points were hidden, his tradecraft would involve cold-calling dozens of executives in a process called “elicitation”: gathering and garnering information without the other person realizing that they’re giving it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The info stretched from intellectual property to less obvious elements of proprietary information, such as wages, health benefits, 401(k) funding, parking spaces—anything that contributed to the bottom line of worth and indebtedness, or that showed discrepancies in a company’s bookkeeping. Very prominent people often lied. It’s another example of the “power paradox” of unethical, dishonest behavior among people with limited accountability. And sometimes the companies were just running fishing operations. Chinese companies loved to feign interest in American companies, to figure out how they could undercut them. They made lowball offers and grabbed as much data as they could.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Forty of the largest countries in the world have a combined total of <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_174" title="174"/>corporate security divisions that is larger than their nation’s number of police officers. In the United States, there are about half again as many corporate security agents as police officers and sheriffs.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Worldwide, there are about twenty million corporate security officers, all governed more by the rules and cultures of their companies than by those of their countries, with incomes of about $200 billion.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So it was easy enough to understand where the nice car came from. And it was easy enough to dream about buying one myself.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I really wanted to like this guy. <i>Whoops!</i> Correction: I really wanted to <i>trust</i> this guy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d learned enough about Mr. X’s company to know that their division managers made all hiring decisions autonomously, that they cherry-picked the best of the best, and that their workforce tended to stay, even though they could bolt to an even bigger and richer company—like Booz Allen Hamilton, which has about twenty thousand employees, or Black Cube, a flashy international firm with roots in Israel’s intelligence agency, Mossad.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Of all the nests I could possibly land in, this was probably the most well feathered. But like almost any other job, it all started with <i>trusting the boss</i>—and that was what I would investigate over the next few days. Spending just three days inside the head of a corporate intelligence officer might sound impossible, but that’s the kind of thing I can do now, with my trust-evaluation system.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I didn’t need to know all his secrets. I just needed to know that <i>what he told me was true</i>, especially if it had anything to do with me or my possible job. I didn’t even know if Mr. X was genuinely interested in me, or was just trying to elicit information, but for the purposes of investigation, it didn’t make much difference. He had his agenda and I had mine, and the goal now was to see if he and I were headed in the same direction. If we were, we’d probably want to vest in each other’s success. If not, so be it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I settled into the soft leather seat, and the ride was so smooth it felt like floating. It lulled me into the same torpor I’d felt about eighteen hours earlier, when I’d awakened before dawn on the East Coast.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_175" title="175"/>I slipped into a luxurious reverie of hope and peace. For almost thirty years, I’d given everything I had to the country, and to the study of trust, and it was time to give something to myself and my family. I really didn’t know what was coming. All I knew was that, one way or another, I’d learn something. I hoped it wasn’t the hard way. But at this moment, life had never felt so easy.</p>
			<h4 class="x05-Head-B">Unleashing the Power of Language</h4>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Because the whole aim and end of language is to <i>tell</i> someone something, language itself is <i>one long tell</i>—and thereby the most transparent and easily accessible of the six signs.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even a message that’s cloaked in vagaries, corporate jargon, hidden agendas, and political correctness can be extremely revealing, if you know how to listen systematically—as do almost all investigators, including FBI agents. The system I’ve been showing you is as good as any that are used throughout the intelligence community.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The single most common way, and the easiest way, to sift trust from trickery is to analyze a person’s spoken and written words—for vocabulary, phrasing, and delivery, as well as content.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The content of any message is the most important element, but its delivery system—its vocabulary, tone, mood, modifiers, and body language—can also be eminently telling, especially when those sometimes involuntary choices unmask feelings that aren’t consistent with the content.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Nonverbal communication is a particularly important element in assessing language, mostly because of the obvious: It’s another form of language. When nonverbals are interpreted accurately, they can be one of the best tells for exposing contradictions, seeing through false promises, and accurately measuring sincerity.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Assessing language accurately is generally more difficult at work than anywhere else. When you’re at home or with friends, the atmosphere is much more likely to be relaxed, which makes it easier to have transparent, meaningful exchanges.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Communicating at work is complicated by the fact that almost <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_176" title="176"/>everyone has the same direct, or indirect, goal: Making money. It’s especially difficult to communicate outside your own company. In this often overheated, fast-forward arena, one of the most difficult professional tasks of all is having to cold-call people. In an era of multitasking and mega-networking, the chore of cold calling isn’t just for salespeople, but for practically everyone who needs to contact strangers from other companies.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So cold calling is a great test of someone’s ability to identify trustworthy people and to inspire those people to trust <i>them.</i> Those two actions often unfold simultaneously and synergistically, as relationships are made and deepened.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Often, though, the cold call lasts about a minute and creates nothing except an even greater fear of rejection.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">This kills deals by the dozen and costs everybody not just lost money, but lost time and lost sleep.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Also, many people just aren’t very good at communicating simply because they haven’t studied it with the same intensity that they’ve applied to their core skills. This tends to occur less commonly among those who work at people-oriented jobs—everything from being a social worker to a salesperson—but is relatively more pronounced among people who work in science and technology.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So as we move toward an increasingly technology-dominated economy, it’s important to remember that millions of people just haven’t been trained in communication.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The CEO of LinkedIn, Jeff Weiner, recently noted on CBS that the greatest skill gap in American business is the seemingly simple task of communicating. That’s partly because countless people are blind to the effects of their words, don’t know how to listen, and preen and preach as if the world revolves around them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That perception is often understandable, because their <i>own</i> world <i>does</i> revolve around them. Just not yours. That’s the part they don’t get.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">People like that, though, aren’t the scary ones, because their communicative behavior is so bad that it’s easy to see. The scary ones are the master manipulators who talk the talk but stab the back.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_177" title="177"/>September 17, 2018, 6:45 p.m.<br/>The Silicon Weeds</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">“Robin!” said Mr. X.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Half asleep from a long day of travel and the luxury of floating silently down the freeway, I was startled but tried not to show it. I had a strong feeling that Mr. X wasn’t very fatigue-friendly. He’d been rattling off a series of business theories he’d read about that were hard to follow, and I was impressed. I’d never met someone with quite his mastery of books and business before. But he was going too fast for me to feel informed, and I thought that might have been his intention. You never know until somebody starts talking. Speaking is much more revealing than emails, because emails are naturally truncated, and can also be edited. That’s partly why they’re so popular.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It’s also why text messages, even more than emails, can be so dangerous. Contrary to conventional culture, I think some things are <i>too</i> quick and convenient.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You know Joe Navarro and Chris Voss, right?” he said, and I nodded. I’d worked with both of them in the Bureau, and now they had successful careers as consultants and authors. “They came out here,” he said. But that was all. I assumed he was either trying to make me feel comfortable by mentioning mutual friends—or <i>not</i> comfortable, by mentioning possible competitors for the job he had in mind for me: whatever <i>that</i> was. All I knew was that I’d been offered a two-day consultancy, and even that was mostly a mystery.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I always try to keep an open mind about what people say, and I listen hard. Often as not, I can tell if I can trust them in a matter of minutes. But Mr. X was relatively hard to read, a common quality in Fortune 500 management—and a virtual epidemic in the F500 <i>intelligence</i> sector.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he mentioned another acquaintance and said, “Good guy, but slow on the uptake.” I agreed with the opinion, but responded neutrally. The guy he mentioned had a problem, but it was none of my business.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_178" title="178"/>“You don’t agree?” he prodded. It really wasn’t a question, so I just smiled. Smiling solves a shocking number of problems. “Got it,” he said, and I smiled again.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Do me a favor,” he said, “and see how our stock did today.” I checked it with my phone and told him his company was up six points. “Awesome!” he said. “That’s two months’ pay!” And that was the end of that. Either he was trying to show me how much money he was making, or he was just happy. It was another one of those bifurcated paths of communication that only get sorted out as the words pile up and the data builds.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Do you know why you’re here?” he asked abruptly. I just shrugged. “To teach my people about trust. That’s your genius.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he was off to the races again about business, probing my background and knowledge on everything from elicitation, to rapport building, to trust, to influence: the various areas in which I’ve worked most often. He was seeking my ideas and opinions, as he had in that first introductory email, but this time it was all about what <i>I</i> could do for <i>him.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But that’s common, so I didn’t let it bother me. Studies conducted as early as a hundred years ago had already started showing that about 40 to 50 percent of everything we say is about ourselves. For some reason, it seemed to me as if people would have been less self-absorbed in that simpler era, but I guess it’s just endemic to human behavior.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In one of the more recent behavioral studies I read, researchers saw that when people talked about themselves to somebody, the pleasure centers in their brains lit up, powered by happy hormones like dopamine, serotonin, and even the post-orgasm and bonding hormone oxytocin. Most of the people did it even after researchers offered to pay them to talk about the other person. When they did let the other person talk, that person’s brain went straight to its happy place, too.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’ve also learned that men are even bigger conversation hogs than women, and focus mostly on self-aggrandizement. Women talk more about bonding and networking, but still mostly in relation to themselves.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_179" title="179"/>It’s been shown that the people in a group who speak the most concisely tend to be the ones who are considered the most trustworthy and wise. It’s because they look as if they don’t feel the need to <i>sell</i> their ideas, which makes the listeners <i>more</i> likely to buy in.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The concept of <i>evoking</i> a feeling in someone, rather than just <i>showing</i> it to them, is invoked in the acting profession as: “Let the audience do the acting.” Corollaries include: “Let the buyer do the selling,” and “Let the reader do the writing.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I got the feeling that Mr. X didn’t even feel very comfortable with what <i>he</i> was saying, because I was getting too many nonverbals that were inconsistent with the points he was making. I especially noticed a strange nervous tic that made him shake his head—in <i>dissent</i> with his words—when he should have been nodding.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He kept asking questions—which typically indicates somebody’s interest in the other person’s opinion. But when I told him how I felt about things, he’d usually contradict me. It seemed reflexive, as if he would have disagreed with <i>whatever</i> I would’ve said. Maybe it was his way of trying to control.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We pulled up in front of a twenty-story building near Palo Alto. “Let’s go see the Eskimos!” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Eskimos?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“My guys. I call ’em Eskimos because all they do is make cold calls. To find stuff out about the companies we’re researching. Your job is to teach them how to be trusted in fifteen minutes!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Why fifteen?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Because if you cold-call executives who are smart enough to know what we need to know, all they’ll <i>give</i> you is fifteen minutes. On a <i>good</i> day.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Apparently we weren’t at the hotel. It was his company’s building, and it looked as if my workday was just starting. I’d only billed him for two days, since it’s customary to have travel-days off, but now it looked as if he wanted an extra half-day. <i>Damn.</i> I was tired. But at least I’d learned that he liked to get more than he’d bargained for. That was good to know. I felt like yawning, but didn’t let myself.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_180" title="180"/>TEN POSITIVE TELLS FOR THE LANGUAGE OF TRUST</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People don’t jump to conclusions.</span> They jump to <i>facts,</i> asking enough questions to fully understand you before they make any comments of their own. Jumping to conclusions is a terribly common trait that tends to cause more conflicts than the issues that are supposedly the <i>sources</i> of conflict.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The trait comes straight from fear, and usually leads to even more fear—and fewer friendly contacts. It also creates emotional hijacking.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">There’s a reason why one of the most common synonyms for a fight, an argument, or another from of discord is called a “misunderstanding.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. People make you feel good when they talk. </span>That’s because they know the single most potent key to being liked: It’s not how you make people feel about you. It’s how you make them feel about <i>themselves</i>.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When somebody makes you feel intelligent, understood, important, or powerful, in a sincere way, how could you possibly not like them?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When somebody tells you that <i>they</i> are intelligent, understanding, important, and powerful, how could you possibly stand one more minute of their company?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This is one of the rare ideas that can change your life, or at least the way you talk to people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. People talk more about your similarities than your differences. </span>They understand that most people, day to day, generally agree about most things. How could we not? We all have the same basic needs and the same basic rights. We all want to be perceived as good people and to behave as good people. We all suffer, we all feel joy, we all win, and we all lose.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_181" title="181"/>When you’re <i>looking</i> for differences, you will find them. The easiest way is to presume that someone disagrees with you. Another way is to <i>ask</i> them if they disagree with you. Why bother?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Every time you do that, you take a significant risk of making your negative assumptions come true.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Start with your agreements, and meander to the <i>disagreements</i>, if you must. Unity and agreement lie in the hearts of all humans. Discord lies at the margins, and every time you fixate on it, you marginalize yourself.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People look at you closely when you talk.</span> They don’t stare off into space, glance at their phone, peek at the TV, or look straight ahead.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Why? They’re looking for how you <i>feel</i> about what you’re saying. Your words alone rarely tell the whole story, and even if they do, people still feel ignored when you don’t look at them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The people you like to talk to keep their eyes as open as their ears.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People don’t speak like the talking heads on TV. </span>In this age of splintered media, TV newspeople tell the audience what they know they want to hear and—guess what?—the audience thinks they’re brilliant.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They don’t present facts. Just arguments. They don’t talk. They accuse and pose.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It’s considered “lively.” It’s deadly.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In entertainment, drama is an art. In communication, it’s a vice.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. They almost never win arguments. </span>(Have you already stopped reading? Don’t worry—there’s a happy ending.) By the time a discussion has degenerated from talking to arguing, and then reaches the stage of either winning or losing, you’ve both lost.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_182" title="182"/>Have you ever “won” an argument in which you were positive that the “loser” wasn’t resentful, vengeful, offended, or otherwise alienated?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People never really win until they stop fighting. That’s not a Zen philosophy. That’s the way life goes.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Any way you look at it, there’s more to winning than just getting your way on a particular day.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Here’s the happy ending: By the time you’ve lost enough to never want to fight again—but just talk—you can’t do anything <i>but</i> win. It happens all the time, and makes people wonder why they ever wasted so much time trying to make someone else lose.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. People are easy to understand. </span>That doesn’t mean it’s easy for them. Being clear is hard work.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The hard part is having good ideas, because clarity lays bare the ideas it conveys. Complexity conceals and simplicity reveals.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Shoot for deep and simple, not shallow and complicated.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. The moral content of their speech goes beyond political correctness. They’re far more concerned about being morally correct.</span></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When you have a big heart and a clear conscience, you don’t feel obligated to advertise how magnanimous and hip you are.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">With the decline of organized religion, political correctness took its place. But it’s mostly composed of good manners, rather than sacrifice from helping the less fortunate.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">I love good manners, but they don’t constitute a complete and robust moral code. PC is not a religion. It’s just being polite.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. People talk about you more than themselves. </span>That’s somewhat similar to Positive Tell #2 (the importance of making people feel good about themselves), but it has a different goal: inspiring you to vest in someone else’s success, and vice versa.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_183" title="183"/>If someone encourages you to be the center of attention, it’s often because they’re trying to see if you have similar goals. If you do, they’ll probably want to do things with you. Because the majority of people talk mostly about themselves. So this is a relatively rare trait, but one of the best possible tells of exemplary behavior.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. People talk the same to rich and poor alike. </span>To them, all people deserve equal respect. Almost all people believe that they are fair and kind to almost everybody, but it’s often a delusion. It’s extremely difficult to treat all people the same, and most of us sometimes stray from that ideal.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">You can’t trust people who are egotistical, condescending, and discriminatory—even if they don’t treat <i>you</i> that way.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">September 17, 2018, 7:00 p.m.<br/>Life Among the Eskimos</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">After Mr. X and I arrived at his company, I shook off my fatigue, grabbed some coffee in the building’s lobby, and followed him to his division’s floor. I was expecting a crowd, but discovered that his whole division was fewer than ten people. Security teams like privacy, but even by that profession’s standards, having a whole floor for a handful of people was an impressive indication of this team’s value to its company.</p>
