			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Mr. X brought his entire team to the center of the floor, which felt as big as a parking lot, because some of the inner walls had been reduced to just load-bearing posts, creating a very rare 360-degree panorama. Every window held a view of what is arguably the world’s most powerful small town.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Most of the staff was clustered near the middle, in individualized, multimodal workstations. It was a very nouveau, Google-like formation that was apparently intended to reflect democracy. (As long as everybody knows who the <i>boss</i> is—that kind of democracy . . .)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_184" title="184"/>With his endemic sense of showmanship, Mr. X pushed a button that revealed a virtually endless bank of cabinets holding vast rows of thin bound booklets, all in the red-brown color of dried blood. Even though I lived in the epicenter of the world’s largest bureaucracy, I’d never seen so much . . . <i>paper.</i> I felt like I was looking at an endangered species.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“What is <i>this</i>?” I asked, genuinely shocked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Our <i>scripts</i>,” Mr. X said proudly. I didn’t know what he meant. “For the <i>calls</i>! The <i>cold</i> calls!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He handed me one, and I got as far as:</p>
			<blockquote>
				<p class="x06-Extract-1P">GOOD <span class="UNDERLINE">_______</span> (MORNING, AFTERNOON), <span class="UNDERLINE">___________</span> (MR./MS.) <span class="UNDERLINE">____________</span>! I’M <span class="UNDERLINE">______</span>, AND I REALLY APPRECIATE YOUR TIME. HOW ARE <i>YOU</i> DOING TODAY?</p>
			</blockquote>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Um, professionals don’t <i>script</i>,” I blurted. I had visions of somebody trying to sell me a time-share over the phone. “<i>Amateurs</i> script.” I wished almost immediately that I hadn’t said that. It wasn’t remotely diplomatic. But it was true.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As the people closest to me studied the floor, Mr. X said, “Tell that to <i>Wired!</i>”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Okay.” I shrugged. I had no idea what he was referencing, but I’d dug myself in deep enough.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some of the staffers glanced up with what seemed to be carefully guarded expressions of appreciation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“How many of these are there?” I asked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Three thousand,” Mr. X said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he asked somebody to get take-out dinners at a nearby upscale restaurant. Nobody seemed surprised when he did that, so there was apparently no such thing as overtime here: just nonstop work, until the boss got tired.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He grabbed an armload of booklets, summoned me to a workstation, and handed me a red pencil. I hadn’t seen a red pencil in years. “Go nuts!” he said. “And bring these up to <i>your</i> standards.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_185" title="185"/>Sounded good. No matter how manipulative these crafted dialogues were, I thought I could fix them. The basic function was just to reverse the perspective and make them about the opinions, ideas, and goals of the <i>person being called,</i> instead of the caller.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You’ve probably already seen enough examples in this book to do the job yourself.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Fueled by caffeine from their ultragourmet coffee and snack station, I was able to plow through ten of them before dinner arrived, because the changes were relatively uniform.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">To me, that’s the most beautiful aspect of building trust-based relationships, even during a quick phone call. You don’t need to reinvent communication; you just need to put the other person in the driver’s seat.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When people first hear that, they usually think they’ll lose control. But control is an illusion. <i>Nobody</i> wants to be controlled, and the moment you start trying to do it, people start resisting. Most people are even uneasy with trying to be influenced.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Genuine, lasting power—which in the final analysis is <i>leadership—</i>resides in those who become resources for the success and inspiration of others. If you can figure out where someone wants to go, and point them in that general direction, your influence can be not only profound, but appreciated. And the appreciation quickly becomes the primary source of your power.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’ll say this about Mr. X’s operation, though: Dinner was amazing. Five stars. In my own plush workstation, I felt like the president of the United States enjoying the creative genius of the White House chef as I toiled into the evening.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">People were really nice to me, and that renewed my confidence in Mr. X, because you can often assess the quality of executives by the caliber of the people they place around themselves.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I started giving people the finished edits of the booklets, and the delivery accelerated as I figured out the most consistent elements that needed to be fixed, and built a system to fix them. A lot of it was just changing “I” to “you” and putting a question mark at the end. It really is that simple.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_186" title="186"/>As occasionally happens when exhaustion and output simultaneously peak, I reached a state of endorphin euphoria that made me think I could revolutionize corporate America’s communication style. A few minutes later, I came to my senses and hit a wall. I dragged my ass into Mr. X’s workspace, which was shaped like a cockpit.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Dude,” I said, “I’m fried.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He reacted as if I’d said that I’d had a minor stroke, and in a matter of minutes he’d arranged a high-speed retreat to my hotel suite, where he assured me my luggage was waiting, along with a nice dessert, a nightcap, and a wake-up call courteously structured to my own time zone. He also called me the “Cold-Call King”—CCK—and the staff picked up on it, as they gathered to say good-bye. Great people.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was flattering. I liked Mr. X. He was a good guy—and at this late hour, liking was enough.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">On my way out, he assured me that even just the changes I’d engineered already would enable his people to “win trust” in the requisite fifteen minutes. I didn’t mention it, but “win trust” is a phrase I never use, because trust isn’t a game, and “winning” trust makes no more sense than “winning” love. In either case, it’s given freely, or it doesn’t exist. Over the years, I’d tried to win both, and it had given me nothing but heartache.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN NEGATIVE TELLS FOR THE LANGUAGE OF TRUST</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People think they can brag artfully. </span>Some people wait for the right moment in a conversation to casually toss in their moment of self-promotion as mere information, or as a pertinent example, or a flash of amusing recollection.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When you give them the kudos they’re looking for, they brush it off.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They also sometimes reassure you that you will be able to do something even better than they did—in the guise of encouragement—when their primary goal is to help you remember how great they are, as you struggle to do it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_187" title="187"/>If they name-drop, they mention the Big Name in a cluster of unknowns, as if they’re not even aware of their status seeking.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. People try to please you by judging people you both know.</span> They imply that you are better than those people, or they wouldn’t be confiding their disapproval.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They give you opportunities to jump in with your own disapproval of those people, as if it’s a healthy form of bonding.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Meanwhile, all you’re thinking is: <i>What do they say about</i> me, <i>when I’m not present?</i></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. People are defensive. </span>Dangerous trait! And one of the most common.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Many people feel that if they deny something, it ceases to exist.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They turn criticisms of themselves into a joke or into an offensive statement that makes no sense.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They pout. They act passive-aggressive.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They change the subject. They distort the “accusation.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Or they just withdraw.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">These are the ways they put up their shields. Shields up, information out. Shields down, information in.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People debate. </span>I’ve mentioned this previously, because it’s a major problem. I’m not talking about an exchange of rational ideas. I’m talking about the hyperemotional dogfights that now dominate opposing discussions everywhere from <i>The Real Housewives of New Jersey</i> to political debates.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Debating tactics are just a string of tricks that can be shockingly effective in manipulating people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Some of the worst include: attacking people instead of their ideas, using insinuation and innuendo, playing on fears, being sarcastic and dismissive, scapegoating, changing the subject, <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_188" title="188"/>exaggerating or diminishing realities, putting words into people’s mouths, and labeling people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Once upon a time, you couldn’t get a passing grade in English if you communicated like that. Now you can run for high public office.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People often speak in absolutes, such as “always” or “never.”</span> This is another form of miscommunication that causes many different problems. Absolutes are meant to support a point of view, but they’re rarely true and can easily incite denial and opposition.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When somebody says, “You never compliment me,” they’re just begging you to say: “That’s ridiculous! I remember giving you compliments!”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Even when you know that somebody is just exaggerating, it can be hard to tell if <i>they</i> know it. When absolutes go unchallenged, they have a perverse tendency to be reborn as the truth.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When you’re looking for someone to trust, listen for their use of words that soften absolutes, such as “usually,” “often,” “probably,” “practically,” “sometimes,” “frequently,” or “generally.” You may have noticed that this book is full of them. They have an almost magical ability to prevent petty bickering.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. People tell you not to mind before they kick your teeth in.</span> As in: “Don’t take this personally, but you suck.” Or: “I don’t mean to be critical—<i>at all</i>—but, as I previously mentioned, you <i>suck</i>.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">I could go on, but you’ve heard it all before.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In behavioral analysis, this is called “negating the frame,” and it’s one of those ugly tricks of language that do, on occasion, seem to work, by softening a blow. More often, they merely announce it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. People talk too much and say too little. </span>It’s usually because they’re trying to hide something, or just don’t have anything to say. So they try to substitute quantity for quality, especially by dropping business buzzwords, like “negative growth,” “thought leader,” <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_189" title="189"/>or a currently ubiquitous cliché du jour: “strategic planning”—as if a regular plan is just a wish list.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In contrast, Winston Churchill—a gifted speaker, inspiring wartime prime minister, and Nobel laureate in literature—once wrote in <i>The Economist,</i> “Short words are best, and old words, when short, are best of all.” A similar sentiment was echoed by business communications specialist L. J. Brockman, creator of the C2M2 formula, which designates the four primary characteristics of successful communications: clear, concise, memorable, and motivational.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Despite the accessibility of straight talk, brevity, and good manners, many people are afraid that if they don’t dominate the conversation or shoot down the ideas of others, they’ll lose control of the situation.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. A person’s body betrays them. </span>Nonverbal communication is often a dead giveaway, and I believe that a person’s face tells the most. Here are some signs of stress displayed by people who are uncomfortable with what they’re saying. When you see these signs, it’s wise—and often kind—to give people special attention about <i>why</i> they feel uncomfortable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Their smile: Unlike in a genuine smile, the corners of their mouth don’t go up, but are pulled straight back. The smile doesn’t include their whole face. Their brows are often furrowed.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Their head angle: Their head is tilted slightly backward, rather than off to either side, and they literally look down their nose at you.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Their eyes: Unlike the wide-open eyes of someone you can trust, their eyes are somewhat lidded, and tend to be locked on you, without much movement.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">These expressions are most revealing if they happen relatively often, but not all the time. The times when they’re not happening provide you with a baseline for evaluation. Then, when you see these signs, you have good reason to analyze them for other tells that show how they really feel.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_190" title="190"/>9. People don’t know how to apologize.</span> Apologizing is pretty easy. You say, “I’m sorry,” and that’s it. But that doesn’t happen as often as it should, does it?</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Far too commonly, people say, “I’m sorry. <i>But</i> . . .” Then comes the about-face, usually fueled by an accusation. <i>“But</i> I only did it because <i>you</i> did, blah, blah, blah.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">This happens out of fear—particularly in fear’s common disguises of arrogance, perfectionism, or some other form of superiority.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The person’s central, self-dooming premise is: <i>It’s all about me—and if I just plead not guilty to every charge, it’ll stay that way.</i></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Quit while you’re ahead.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. People assign psychological diagnoses to themselves, as if that justifies their troubled behavior.</span> The most common traits that are cited are depression, anxiety, and post-traumatic stress syndrome, all of which are perfectly valid for millions of people. But even though they may explain unpleasant behavior, they don’t justify it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">If an overly self-conscious or unhappy person can’t accept the idea that many other people suffer similarly—without inflicting their suffering on others—it will be even harder for them to get the help they desire.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">September 20, 2018<br/>Mr. X Misdirects Me</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">On my last day in Palo Alto—another travel day that had been shoehorned into a day’s work—I talked to as many of the Eskimos as possible, getting a feel for their internal levels of comfort and satisfaction. They were all loyal to the boss, but I thought that might have reflected <i>their</i> high level of character and ethics more than his.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">One of the most forthright people I met wasn’t one of the Eskimos, but a man who worked in the Spies and Lies section. It was a microniche <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_191" title="191"/>that apparently had something to do with a huge bank of databases and the creation of “pretext”—the spook-speak word for misrepresenting the actual purpose of a phone call—or some other form of penetrating an organization.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He said he’d heard that I was the Fifteen-Minute Man—the guy who could create trust in a fifteen-minute call—but that Mr. X might be misdirecting me about the bottom-line specifics of what he wanted from me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“He tends to prevent people from seeing the big picture,” the Spies and Lies guy said. “I think he’s most comfortable when he’s the only person who knows what’s going on. Makes him feel safer, I guess.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Safer from what, I never found out, although I suspected he meant safer from an outraged multinational company, or safer from being replaced. In any case, the subject wasn’t much of a conversation starter. Mystery rarely is. I didn’t probe any further, since I was trying to keep a low profile at this point. But I certainly took note.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was beginning to enjoy the exotic atmosphere, and there was a lot about Mr. X to like and admire—which created at least the verisimilitude of trust, comfort, and admiration, if not the real thing. He’d dropped all the buzzwords and business lingo (Positive Tell #7), was careful to talk about our similarities (Positive Tell #3), and paid close attention when I was talking (Positive Tell #4). He’d backed off on being argumentative (Positive Tells #6 and #1), and talked more about me than himself (Positive Tell #9). That’s a lot. In any number of situations, it would be more than enough.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But I wasn’t in love with the scripting process, and hadn’t made a dent in his infatuation with it. He tried to flatter me by saying his Eskimos just weren’t at my level—so they needed the scripts—but I was afraid he was just deceiving himself. He was good at the creation of compelling narratives, even if they were deceptive—and I don’t mean that as a gratuitous criticism, because counterespionage is all about narratives and deception.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even so, he was one of the very best wordsmiths I’ve ever met, but he tended to let his beautifully expressed words determine his thoughts, instead of letting his thoughts determine his words.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_192" title="192"/>That trait is not uncommon among top salespeople, politicians, writers, inspirational speakers, and other professionals who are simply more fluent than thoughtful.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">From my perspective, though, he seemed to be scripting manipulation. I asked him that—politely but point-blank—but he was emotionally defensive about it (Negative Tell #3).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Instead of defending his position with logic, he used another of his debating tactics, as he had when I’d first mentioned that scripting was amateurish. At that time, he’d created a false choice: If I didn’t agree with him, I was disagreeing with the editors at <i>Wired</i> (Negative Tell #4).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As I was gathering my stuff to head to the airport, I remembered other red flags I’d noticed in his language. Right off the bat, he’d bragged about his stock holdings in a very indirect way, by equating them to his income (Negative Tell #1). He’d also criticized a guy we both knew, for no reason other than to intimate that he and I were better than the guy (Negative Tell #2). Also on that first day, before we even got out of the car, I’d seen him contradict himself nonverbally, by shaking his head when he should have been nodding (Negative Tell #8). That was creepy, because it seemed to reflect a deep inner conflict—a behavior problem particularly well reflected by nonverbals.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He’d also used far too many absolutes (Negative Tell #5), which can be harmless, but can also indicate an attachment to polar opposites that can metastasize into oppositional behavior: disagreeing for the sake of disagreement. Like so many other people who are argumentative, I thought that he liked to argue because he liked to win, and had no sense of my belief that by the time you’re arguing, <i>nobody</i> wins.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was also concerned about a time when he’d contradicted me. I’m fine with differing opinions, but he’d said, “I’m not implying that you know <i>nothing</i> about surveillance, but . . .” (Negative Tells #5 and #6).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He was kind enough to drive me back to the airport, and was in good spirits.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“We got a <i>ton</i> done,” he said, “and I’m gonna have you <i>back</i>!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he asked me if I’d like to be put on retainer. I didn’t know what <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_193" title="193"/>kind of money he had in mind, and I didn’t really want to know. It would have made it harder for me to do what I’d already decided to do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“One thing, though,” he said. “We’re going to take this one step further. Really <i>streamline</i> it. I’m talking about”—he paused for effect—“<i>thirty seconds</i>!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So that was probably the misdirection that the guy from Spies and Lies had alluded to.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Mr. X, staring straight ahead—as if he was so sure about my reaction that he didn’t need to look at me—said, “I’ll be honest. That’s what I always wanted. But I didn’t know if you could wrap your head around it.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That was it. I was done. Too many surprises. Not enough predictability.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I told him so, and he practically began to cry. It was very strange. I could <i>never</i> have predicted that. But it reassured me that I’d made the right decision.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The rest of the ride was awkward. For him. I felt fine.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was going home.</p>
			<aside>
				<h3 class="x10-Sidebar-Title">DEBRIEFING</h3>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-A">CHAPTER 7: <br/>“LISTEN FOR THE REVEAL”</p>
				<div class="border"/>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">SIGN #5: LANGUAGE</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Quote:</b> “Because the whole aim and end of language is to <i>tell</i> someone something, language itself is <i>one long tell</i>—and thereby the most transparent and easily accessible of the six signs.”</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_194" title="194"/>Key Message:</b> The basic content of the language of trust consists of verbal and nonverbal statements that reflect nonjudgmental, validating thoughts intended to empower others with choice. It reflects a willingness to be a resource for the benefit of others, and the willingness to vest in their success.</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">THE TAKEAWAYS</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First"><b>Listen carefully:</b> The language of trust focuses more on the listener than the speaker, and reflects a continuous effort to understand what people want, and how to be a resource for those desires.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Pay attention to nonverbal communication:</b> Nonverbal communication is more important and revealing for the language of trust than it is for any of the other signs, simply because it is another form of language.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last"><b>Avoid misunderstandings:</b> Lack of understanding causes so many conflicts that one of the most common synonyms for a disagreement is a “misunderstanding.” Get the facts. Then talk.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Ten Positive Tells for the Language of Trust</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They don’t jump to conclusions.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They make you feel good when they talk.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They talk more about your similarities than your differences.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They look at you closely when you talk.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t speak like the talking heads on TV.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They almost never “win” arguments.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re easy to understand.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">The moral content of their speech goes beyond political correctness.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They talk more about you than about themselves.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They talk the same to rich and poor.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_195" title="195"/>Ten Negative Tells for the Language of Trust</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They use subtle forms of self-aggrandizement.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They try to please you by judging people you both know.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re defensive.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They debate.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They often speak in absolutes, such as “always” or “never.”</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They tell you not to mind before they kick your teeth in.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They talk too much and say too little.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">Their bodies betray their words.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They don’t know how to apologize.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They use psychological labels to avoid <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_196" title="196"/>accountability.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
			</aside>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 15: Chapter 8: See Inside People: Sign #6: Stability ===

<div>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Number" id="_idParaDest-29"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_197" title="197"/>8</h2>
			<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Title" id="_idParaDest-30">SEE INSIDE PEOPLE</h2>
			<div class="box">
				<h2 class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle" id="_idParaDest-31">Sign #6: STABILITY</h2>
				<p class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle-Para">Does this person consistently demonstrate emotional maturity, self-awareness, and social skills?</p>
			</div>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A-CO">Quantico, 2019<br/>Building Your Own World</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">We all live in a world that is disproportionately dominated by those who are the most domineering. Our acts are often compelled by the most compulsive, and controlled by the most controlling.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even our day-to-day moods are routinely usurped by the moody people around us, and many of our anxieties are created by the overly anxious.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">These intrusive, emotionally unstable people often gain great power—not just in companies, cities, or social groups, but even in entire nations, where they callously degrade and destroy the lives of millions of people they’ve never met. Consider the criminal insanity of Adolf Hitler. Or Joseph Stalin. Osama bin Laden. Saddam Hussein. Pol Pot. Bashar al-Assad. These are only a few of recent history’s major-league villains, and there are also millions of minor-league monsters in the world, as well as <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_198" title="198"/>a virtually infinite number of ordinary idiots who’ve never learned how to act like decent human beings.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That’s not how most of us would like life to be, but it’s the way it’s always been. The tyranny of the troubled is a sad fact of human nature that’s almost as immutable as the laws of physics.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even so, the power of the imbalanced remains reasonably bearable—unless we’re called to war, taken to prison, or similarly harmed—because it’s the only real price we have to pay for enjoying <i>our own unsung luxury of emotional stabilit</i>y<i>,</i> while the fretful crazies stew about the problems that they’ve created for themselves.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The price we pay is usually worth it. To be in the mind of any of these people would be like living in hell.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Many of the most distressed and demented people—even those whose power is tiny and mundane—see themselves as superheroes, power players, or victims who now deserve to be victimizers. But that’s not how other people see them. With certain exceptions, we see emotionally unstable people—no matter their place in the human hierarchy—as shaky, sly, and at least slightly deranged. Even if they’re wealthy and powerful, they look like imposters to us and are impossible to respect, predict, or ally with.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You probably realized long ago, after meeting hundreds of ten-cent villains, that they acted that way because, at some point, they were so miserable that they traded their own sense of self for a reward they may already have lost, or to escape a punishment they may have deserved. But whether they won or lost the tangible prizes of life, they almost certainly lost control of their <i>own</i> lives, and that’s a loss that few souls survive.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Countless troubled but courageous people—thank God—have clawed their way back from the abyss of emotional instability, but some people just can’t. They lose control of their behavior and environments and become the architects of their own doom. As their desperation for control deepens, their sense of worth disintegrates, and they begin an eternal search outside themselves for the peace of mind that’s only available within.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i>But you do not have to live in their world.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_199" title="199"/>With work, luck, help—and a free country—you can create a world of your own. Not everyone will choose to live in your world, of course. That would be too much to hope for (and too much trouble!). But you can <i>exclude</i> virtually anyone you want from your world, if you’re willing to move forward without them. And from that point on, no one can tell you what to do, without your consent.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That might sound risky or overly ambitious, but it’s the deal that’s now on the table in front of you. It’s been there most of your life and it will never go away—even if you don’t take advantage of it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It’s a good deal! It’s the same one I made with myself years ago. And now I wake up every morning in what feels very much like my own world, tailored to fit my own desires, abilities, obligations, and goals. It’s not a perfect world. (How <i>could</i> it be? <i>I’m</i> in it.) But it makes life inordinately richer in one central way: It allows me to <i>be myself.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">That’s huge, because if you’re <i>not</i> yourself, it’s almost as if you don’t exist. I’m sure you’ve met many people who have surrendered their sense of self—and that you’ve seen the blank look in their eyes, and heard the hollow sound of their voice.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Life can be a tough challenge for anybody, and it’s easy to lose your sense of self. It happens—to at least a moderate degree, and for a finite period of time—to most people. It happened to me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In my younger years, I was a typical type-A, all-American dumb-ass who wasn’t remotely as confident as I acted, so I set out to reinvent myself, sort of like The Great Gatsby did—but I was more like The Pretty Good Gatsby.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was luckier than most. I failed at an early age. I had the misfortune to succeed as a student and athlete in high school, and that success, and the rewards it left, revealed my emptiness. I was considered cool—but only by people <i>other</i> than myself.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Like so many people, I was my own best enemy, because I always knew which of my buttons to push, which of my fears to exaggerate, and how to piss people off if they slighted me. And the more I criticized myself, the more I rebelled against it, and hid behind my pride. With my insecurity churning, I skewed more toward arrogance than <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_200" title="200"/>humility, believing that the only reason most people were humble was because they had a lot to be humble about. But even then, I knew my facade was paper thin.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As an antidote, and a matter of patriotism, I entered the U.S. Marine Corps with the lofty goal of shedding that callow feeling of vulnerability, longing, and unworthiness that’s often called innocence. Similarly to many young people, I was filled with worry about my failings thus far, and what they might mean to my future. Those feelings emerged mostly as disappointment in myself, and as shame: just garden-variety guilt.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">No matter how hard I tried, I couldn’t stop feeling guilty about what I’d already done wrong, and I didn’t even know what it <i>was.</i> That’s youth, right?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But as I kept studying human behavior, I found that the most accurate and granular definition of guilt is: <i>fear of not being loved.</i> We’re afraid that when people—or deities—discover the bad things we’ve done, their love for us will fade. That simple but profound threat is usually the root cause of the empty, sick feeling that always goes with guilt.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We’re less likely, though, to feel remorse or shame when we think that we’ll get away with something. That phenomenon is a form of the previously mentioned power paradox: Powerful people are more likely than vulnerable people to break rules, betray trust, and not worry too much about it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But even if you’re one of the rich, famous (and unindicted) people, there’s still no easy way out of this dilemma, because if you hide your transgressions, you’ll always know that the people who love you don’t love the <i>real</i> you.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So innocence and guilt—generally seen as opposites—share some of the same shaky ground in the eternal search for love. And both can hurt.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The pain comes from facing your hunger for love, and accepting the changes in yourself that love requires, without losing your sacred sense of self. It’s a balancing act, and when you attempt it, you will at some point fall.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><i>We will all fall.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_201" title="201"/>We all know that the concept of a perfect world—even one you craft around yourself—is an illusion. But it’s a hard illusion to resist, and impossible to leave behind without some regret.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We all want to strip away life’s illusions, but—as I learned on 9/11—who among us wants to be disillusioned? I’d lost enough of my own illusions—on that grave day—to clearly see my vast number of humbling limitations. And it actually made me feel better! That may sound paradoxical, but it’s not. Most people who don’t see their limitations are also largely unaware of their powers, because the nature of insight is to see everything . . . or nothing. You can’t cherry-pick self-awareness.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">People who can’t handle the challenge of seeing inside themselves tend to preen, pose, dominate, and manipulate, never knowing that these sad acts carry them further and further from the primal desire they wanted in the first place: self-acceptance. It’s the foundation of all emotional stability.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some people who aren’t emotionally stable try to compensate by being perfect—but perfectionism is generally just another face of fear. Perfectionists always want more-more-more, but people who are humble usually believe they’ve got nothing to prove. They think that they’ve already done enough to earn the right to live in their own world, without sacrificing their individuality, strengths, and desires.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So humility offers an enormous head start on the road to emotional stability. Even so, humility is one of those funny things that we all want, but don’t always like. When you first sample it, you feel let down, as if you’re just like everybody else. You forget that <i>that’s</i> <i>what you wanted all along:</i> a sense of belonging, with the warmth of family, friends, and professional allies—even if every one of them has some flaws.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But if you’re lucky, you meet somebody else who’s humble. You accept and respect them—but not because you think they’re better than you. To you, they’re just another member of the tribe.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then you see them do something incredible, and the single best and hardest lesson of emotional stability starts to sink in: <i>You don’t have to be better than other people to be amazing.</i></p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_202" title="202"/>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>Here’s a fact of life:</b> No matter how hard you try, you’ll never be majestically, inordinately better than you’ve always been—but that’s good <i>enough.</i> You won’t be loved by everyone, but you’ll be loved <i>truly</i> by the people who are capable of true love. They’ll be the only ones whose love really matters to you, because they’ll love you for being you, and won’t need you to fulfill the fantasies that are so important to people who are incapable of true love.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I first found that lofty level of love and acceptance in my wife and kids. Family is where most people find stability. And then it filters out to some close friends. Sadly, that’s often where it ends. When many people head for work, it’s often gone, because they believe in (and thereby help to create) a work-world dominated by attitudes of dog-eat-dog, killer-instincts, take-no-prisoners, nose-to-the-grindstone, trust-no-one—and all the other clichés that <i>just aren’t necessary</i> in an emotionally stable, self-created world. Even the rough-and-tumble world of the Marine Corps, I found, was governed far more by camaraderie and shared mission than by aggression and selfish ambition.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">After the Corps had kicked some sense into me, I gravitated naturally to the FBI, as do many people from the military who are looking for another role that will require great discipline, a way to bring their patriotism home, and the chance to serve greater gods than money and power.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">By that time, I was a fairly humble, stable guy (by the crazy standards of young American males), and I believed I could be a great FBI agent during the day—and a human being at night! (My wife more or less insisted on it.)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So even as a young agent, I was still a little rough around the edges. That’s me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But it was all working!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Until September 11, 2001. All hell broke loose—even in <i>my world—</i>and peace in America never really returned.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At that time, as I’ve mentioned, I was working Russia, mostly in New York. I spent my days helling around Manhattan like a batshit-crazy <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_203" title="203"/>bloodhound, sniffing out spies, and finding spies of my own. But when the Bureau was blindsided by 9/11—as was practically every three-letter agency—it gave the Russians a window of opportunity to grab back some of their empire. They invaded Chechnya and killed about fifty thousand people, mostly civilians. They unleashed a vicious storm of terror, kidnapping, hostage taking, torture of entire families, beheading, degradation, and rape, with a special focus on sex crimes, which horrified the conservative Chechen culture.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">America, though, was understandably obsessed with the Middle East, and was vulnerable to Russia’s propaganda programs. Their line was: “Chechnya is full of Muslim terrorists, and we’re doing your dirty work <i>for</i> you—so, <i>you’re welcome</i>!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">America’s perception of the Russian narratives tilted toward acceptance. It was a crazy perception, and allowed the rebirth of Russia as an unstable, loose cannon nation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The shift in attitude was alarming. I’d already learned that most aberrant behavior starts with misperception—rather than evil, or outright insanity—and that America was <i>choosing</i> to perceive Russia as benign.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I was bewildered by it—until I joined the Behavioral Analysis Program—because the BAP specialized in <i>why</i> things were happening, rather than the issues of who, what, when, or where. When the BAP taught me not to look at things from strictly my <i>own</i> context, I realized that American citizens were tolerating Russia’s cruelty and greed simply because we could not, at that time, tolerate having another enemy. From the American public’s context of fighting virtually permanent wars in Afghanistan and Iraq, <i>any</i> war seemed destined for disaster.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In the Behavioral Analysis Program, my understanding of human nature grew exponentially, partly because I had to parse my <i>own</i> perceptions practically every day, in order to understand those of others. It opened my eyes, and revealed a more rational, warmhearted world. When I stopped seeing my life as a never-ending melodrama, <i>it no longer was.</i> When I started looking for people to trust, I found them. When I stopped allying myself with emotionally unstable people, my world got healthier.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_204" title="204"/>Over several years, with the help of my Jedi Master Jesse and my family, I <i>drove myself sane</i>, and—guess what?—it felt like the world was getting sane right along with me! That perspective was mostly just my own perception, but the immediate environment in <i>my own world</i> did become more sane, and I did everything I could to help that condition spread.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In the BAP, I got an incredible crash course in the predictable cause-and-effect mechanics of human nature, as my life improved immeasurably—even among my family and friends. The approach hinged on one powerful principle: If you do A with someone, you’ll usually get B—whether you want to or not, work hard or not, or apply resources or not. People are people. Therefore: People are predictable.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I rode that principle all the way to the top of the program, and never looked back. In 2011, I was appointed head of the BAP. I was only its fourth leader in history, so I took the job very seriously. I felt obligated to leverage the most potent power I’d ever had into making America feel safe again.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I loved my new job. Time glided by with the peace of mind that comes naturally when you accept the fact that almost everything that people do—<i>from their perspective—</i>makes sense! (It may <i>suck</i>, but it makes sense.)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">And if it makes sense, <i>you can predict it.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">And if you can predict it, <i>you can</i> <i>act accordingly.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">And then, far more often than not, the things people do won’t even suck—especially in <i>your own</i> world—because you’ll see them coming and can deal with them appropriately.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When people master that simple set of rules—as <i>you</i> probably have by now—they can easily become a resource for the success of others, which elevates their own success, as they form more and more alliances.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At that point, virtually all good things seem within reach.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_205" title="205"/>December 2011<br/>Saddam Hussein’s Rec Room</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">“Do me a favor?” It was Jesse calling, shortly after I’d become head of the Behavioral Analysis Program.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Name it.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He said he was calling on behalf of a young New York agent who felt like she was in over her head. She’d approached him because he was a legend at simplifying complex challenges.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse was retired, but still helped the agency as a contract specialist, and was embedded with her squad. She was working Russia, which was raising all kinds of hell again.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">This time the issue was: Would Russia become a genuine democracy, or regress once more into the kind of criminal dictatorship that had haunted the nation for three hundred years? (Spoiler alert: Don’t get your hopes up.)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Vladimir Putin had just pulled off a rigged election that had triggered rioting in the streets and mass incarcerations, including the arrests of about a thousand political prisoners.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even the concept of Russian political prisoners created terrible images in my mind, and I longed to help.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Russia has never abandoned the use of torture and terror, and there was no doubt that they were used in this situation. Some people lived to talk about it, and some didn’t. The details are too disturbing to recount here.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The endless spiral of arrests, interrogations, imprisonment, abuse, and merciless intimidation of victims and their families that began then <i>is still happening</i>.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The agent Jesse called about was a young New York woman named Linda, who had recently earned a PhD in psychology. She’d just been briefed by another three-letter agency in NYC, and he’d pointed her toward a target who was suspected of running the current media whitewash of Russia’s sadistic brutality. He supposedly worked at the <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_206" title="206"/>Russian consulate and the United Nations, but he had a background in media, and seemed to know the soft spots in the American psyche. The program Russia was using at this time was almost exactly the same strategy it had employed during the Chechen War: Blame Middle Eastern terrorists.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But to get to the target, Linda needed to take a circuitous path, through an Iraqi defector who’d reportedly worked directly with Saddam in his Baghdad palace. He wasn’t a CHS, but we had reason to believe he might help us. He was attending a series of brown-bag lectures at Fordham University on Russian history—and so was the target.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda was freaked out, Jesse said, by the life-or-death elements of the case, even though the deaths were occurring thousands of miles away. The mission was a far cry from the cases of depression and anxiety she’d treated as a clinical psychologist before signing on with the FBI in order to, as she later put it, “make a difference.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I told her I could teach her by example,” Jesse said, “but that <i>you </i>could show her a system that she can keep using forever. She needs something like that.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Then he said softly, “She’s not”—he hesitated—“functioning at her best.” That was as close as he ever got to criticizing people. He generally expressed negativity by what he <i>didn’t</i> say, which packed more punch than the explicit attacks of most people.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse said that Linda had the potential to be a great agent, but that she was very sensitive, and her emotions seemed to be taking over. Her feelings were crowding out her rationality, and making her hesitant about what to do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Tell her I’d be happy to help,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">My plan was to do a standard BAP consultation: invite her—and her team, if possible—to Quantico, and help her create a strategy based on behavioral analysis. We’d try to discern the predictable behavior of the people involved, and offer suggestions, and points to consider. The execution of it, as always, would be up to the agent in charge: her, in this case.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For starters, I was concerned about the Iraqi that she needed to interview. He sounded like a spooky guy with a cloudy past.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_207" title="207"/>There was some vague mention of him being the only man in Iraq that Saddam had feared—even though Saddam had his own torture chamber in the basement of the palace where he lived with his wife and kids. Saddam wanted it there because he <i>enjoyed</i> his own version of a rec room, just as his sons enjoyed their own rape rooms. He also had one in Manhattan, across the street from Michael Bloomberg’s home, deep in the basement of the Mission of Iraq, where he authorized the kidnapping and abuse of Iraqis living in the U.S., using them for leverage against family members who were still in the Middle East. The torture chamber remained until federal investigators raided the building following Saddam’s fall in 2003.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">There were similar rooms in Iraqi embassies all around the world.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I could see how this level of horror might make Linda feel off-balance. Russia was successfully executing the most repulsive shift toward tyranny in recent history, and when you’re that close to depravity, paranoia can chill your blood and make every possible fear seem dreadfully real.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda’s reaction wasn’t even unusual. Many people quickly become unstable in dangerous and hostile environments. And most people act irrationally when they’re desperate. You never <i>really know</i> somebody until you see them try to function under fire.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But threatening events aren’t even the most common cause of erratic, unpredictable behavior. Even people in typical, unremarkable environments can be pushed toward irrationality by many different factors: childhood abuse (which often prompts rebound abuse), adult PTSD, serious biochemical imbalances, substance abuse, head injuries, and neurological illnesses.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even more people exist in a gray area of emotional instability that is not obvious, constant, nor even severe. These people are often expected to function as well as people with robust mental health, but they just can’t.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Fortunately, though, other powerful factors can help people who “go crazy” to “go sane” again. These healing forces include medication, healthier lifestyles, counseling, the help of friends and family, meditation, support groups, spiritual fulfillment, and substance abuse treatment.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_208" title="208"/>Those forces—of personal redemption and generous support—are among the most highly evolved functions of humanity. They allow us to remain stable in an unstable world, and to help others <i>regain</i> their stability.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even so, emotional instability—even if temporary—is a hell of a monkey wrench to throw into the pursuit of predictable, laudable behavior.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The lack of emotional stability is arguably the most important deficit in all of the six signs—because it makes unstable people so hard to predict. Like everybody else, unstable people do what they <i>think</i> <i>is in their own best interests</i>, even if it isn’t, which can set them off on a cycle of reckless or irrational behavior—which cannot be predicted.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even more problematic, anyone with significant emotional instability usually destroys their ability to engage effectively in any of the other signs for predicting people.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So without the foundation of emotional stability, it can be very hard for people to properly exercise the other five signs, and they become virtually unknowable.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The best way to navigate that ocean of the unknown is to learn to spot emotional instability, and stay as far from it as possible, unless you feel morally obligated to help.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If I saw significant instability in Linda, I’d probably have to help her explore a role that was more suited to her. That’s not quite like, “You’re fired,” but that’s almost certainly how she’d see it.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">January 2012<br/>Second Impressions</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">In early January, Linda walked uncertainly into my office, exuding an attitude of: <i>I do not belong here.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">My first thought was: <i>Damn! This woman looks about as old as Dora the Explorer! Who’s going to take</i> her <i>seriously? Russian spies?</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But I’ve learned that second impressions are almost always more <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_209" title="209"/>accurate than the overrated first impression, because you’ve had time to <i>think.</i> Going with your gut is popular, but I guarantee you that your brain is smarter than your gut.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Linda! Great to see you!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I shook her hand, and it was cold. Nonverbal explanation: fear. Everybody knows that “cold feet” refers to fear—not just figuratively, but also literally, since fear constricts blood vessels, and impedes circulation to the extremities. But many people don’t know that cold <i>hands</i> are just as indicative of fear, and are much harder for someone to conceal in a social situation. (So if you’re going into a scary meeting, put your hands under warm running water, which masks your nervousness to anyone whose hand you might shake, as a <i>literal</i> expression of the fight-flight-freeze response. It sends your brain a feed-forward message of reassurance, the same way that taking a deep breath does.)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Thanks for seeing me,” Linda said, in sort of a little-girl voice that fit her appearance, but was almost certainly a career handicap.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">She was breathing hard and said something apologetic about traffic.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I smiled, waved it off, and said, “Take a breath!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I took one myself, because I was, to some extent, absorbing some of her fear, and coming up with stuff of my own to worry about. It’s a natural instinct. Fear is the most contagious of all feelings—much more than love—because it’s stored not just in the thought-dominated, rational forebrain, but in the even deeper regions of the primitive, mammalian brain, and even the reptilian brain.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But I was here for Linda, so I put my thoughts on rewind, and looked at her again. The single best way for me to achieve emotional stability in a relationship—and also to recognize stability in others—is to <i>see people as they are</i>, without emotional bias. Something trivial—such as simply having a youthful face or a high voice—may make someone different from me, but it was nothing more than a challenge to overcome. Unfortunately, though, difference is the underlying trigger of <i>most phobias</i>, and creates the multifaceted, broad-spectrum fear known as xenophobia. So it’s not always easy to feel comfortable with people who are clearly different from you.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_210" title="210"/>Even so, most of us don’t need to be victims of <i>any</i> phobias, if we simply allow rationalism to lead the way.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Besides, I didn’t want to let my own concerns make Linda feel even worse. How we feel, even when we think it’s not obvious, has a powerful effect on how others feel—especially people who are already unstable. Their tipping point is too easy to reach.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I took another breath, and looked at her again, trying to see her for who she was, without imposing my own prejudices.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">She looked better! Older! Wiser! (Kids grow so fast!)</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I asked her how I could be a resource for her, because vesting in someone’s success is a phenomenally fast and effective way to gain an ally, and prompt people to open up.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">She didn’t really respond, though. Maybe she just felt helpless, and needed me to reach out even further. The best thing you can do with most people is to just keep trying.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Jesse thinks you’ve got huge potential,” I said. She seemed very flattered and relieved. I don’t use fake flattery—people smell it a mile away—but I rarely see people who think they don’t deserve a sincere compliment, even if it’s a little exaggerated (because it rarely seems like an exaggeration to <i>them</i>).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I told her that I’d worked Russia for about twenty years, knew the culture, and that she could count on my help for as long as she needed it. As always, I sought her thoughts and opinions, validated her context, spoke in terms of her priorities, and empowered her with choice.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I trust you know the power of that approach by now.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">She warmed up, but she was still scared. She had to go meet the Iraqi contact the following day. “I’ll go with you tomorrow,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Her nonverbals told me that much of her fear had just evaporated.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’ll frame it to my supervisor as an urgent request for an informal consultation. Know why?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">She shook her head.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Less paperwork.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For the first time, she smiled.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_211" title="211"/>Long Island, New York<br/>The Only Man Saddam Hussein Feared</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">The next day, as we drove out to Long Island to see the Iraqi, she was tense again. I knew that the stress, from a medical perspective, was impairing her cognitive function, including her memory and rationality. It’s arguably the best single reason to control your emotions.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The man’s house was nice—red brick with forest green shutters, and kids playing in the street—and it calmed my nerves a little. His wife answered the door. “Yeah?” she said. It reminded me of how Leo’s grandson had answered the phone every time his caller ID showed it was me—at least in the early days.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I told her who we were and that we had an appointment with her husband.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“We’ll see,” she said, and closed the door.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We could hear her raise her voice. After some time, the man came out and started sizing us up. I put on my happy face, Linda was charming, and we managed to get in the door. I felt, as I often did, like a salesman—which was basically what I was. One way or another, we’re all in sales, and like all salespeople, what we need to sell first is ourselves.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I brought up the class he was taking at Fordham University, because that was his link to our target. At the dining room table, I edged into understanding him better, so I could strategize a dialogue. But his wife suddenly appeared and shouted, “Oh my God—you’re invading our privacy!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda looked stunned. I gave her a look that said, <i>Don’t buy into the drama</i>.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’m sorry, ma’am,” I said, “but your husband invited us here. We’ll leave if you want.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You have no right!” She cited a law, and said she worked with the American Civil Liberties Union. I figured it had probably been a big stretch for her to go from Saddam’s palace to the ACLU, so I shrugged it off.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The man stood, took her by the arm, and asked her to let Linda and me finish, as he guided her toward the door—to the best of his ability.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_212" title="212"/>She barged in two more times, pissed and weird, and after the second time he escorted her out, we gave him a high-speed rundown on gathering some information about the presumed Russian spy. We could hear her just outside the door.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I regret I cannot help you,” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda’s expression went sour. I shook my head almost imperceptibly—meaning, <i>Don’t let him emotionally hijack you</i>. Then I said to him, “Why? Are you concerned it’s dangerous?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yes,” he said, motioning with his thumb toward the kitchen door. “But take this.” He wrote down a phone number and the name Susan, and handed it to me. It seemed as if that had been his plan all along. “She, too, takes the class. She is very intelligent. Perhaps she would find this work . . . easier to schedule.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I gave the number to Linda, to help underscore her power for the Iraqi (and herself). I’ve found that if you hand off power often enough, the person you give it to will begin to feel and act empowered—not necessarily because you changed their degree of power, but because you acknowledged it. We’re all pretty powerful, unless someone is trying to control us—and even then, most people rise to the occasion and make themselves heard.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We heard a pan hit the floor, probably to remind us that his wife was still home.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At that point, I had nothing to lose, so I said, “I heard something about you being the only man in the world that Saddam Hussein feared.” I was just curious. I thought maybe he was some kind of secret police hitman—the kind of thug who emerges from nowhere during a coup, then disappears forever.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He smiled. “Yes. He did fear me. I’m surprised you know that.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Why?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I braced myself for a potentially chilling answer. Out of the corner of my eye, I saw Linda do the same.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I was his dentist,” he said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We heard, “Shit!” from the kitchen. And another pan.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I, too, fear only one person,” he said. He used his thumb again, to finish the thought.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_213" title="213"/>The Quiet Mind vs. the Noisy Mind</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I subscribe to the psychological theory that there are only two basic emotions that are hardwired into the brain, and are the ultimate drivers of human thought and behavior: the opposing forces of love and fear.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">All other emotions, in the end, are derived from them.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even the deeply held emotion of anger, for example, comes from fear, because anger—in all its ferocity, and with all its comorbid conditions, such as hate or panic—commonly subsides and can even disappear when there is <i>nothing left to fear from whatever caused the anger</i>. Similarly, sadness—which most often comes from loss—is, at rock bottom, the fear of living forever without whatever is gone and will never return. The same basic principle of feeling relief when a threat is gone also applies to the other forms of fear, such as disgust, cynicism, distress, contempt, or insecurity.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Love, the other primary emotion that we’re born with, emerges in its own forms of expression, including romance, security, protectiveness, appreciation, loyalty, enchantment, devotion, worship, and allegiance. And sometimes love is necessary to survive, and fear is necessary to thrive. They run that deep.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Fear is necessary to survive, and love is necessary to thrive. The same can’t be said of any other single emotion.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The most beautiful thing about love—first widely proposed by prominent Duke University psychologist and author Dan Baker, PhD—is that it is mentally and emotionally impossible to be in a state of fear and a state of love at the same time. Love, therefore, is life’s only reliable, eternal, and universal antidote to fear.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Love, in the broadest sense of the word, must also be present for trust. As I’ve delineated several times, there’s a huge difference between liking or even <i>loving</i> someone, and trusting them. Trusting and liking are not at all synonymous. But they are in almost all cases simultaneously present, simply because trusting someone is impossible without a certain feeling of allegiance or identification, even if it comes only <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_214" title="214"/>from having corresponding goals. It’s also hard to see someone engage in the first sign of trust—vesting in your success—without feeling at least a little gratitude, which is essentially the same as appreciation, the all-giving, highest form of love.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Because of this, the conflicting emotions of love and fear are at the root of almost all the tells of emotional stability and instability.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I think of these two polarized conditions—love and emotional stability vs. fear and emotional instability—as the “quiet mind” vs. the “noisy mind.” The quiet mind speaks with a single, consolidated voice, calmed by reason, while the noisy mind is often a cacophony of contradictory feelings, ungoverned by rationality, and muddled with emotion.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Love is the pure essence of the quiet mind, and fear is the constant cold shriek of the noisy mind.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The problem that blocks many people from achieving a quiet mind begins with the unavoidable fact that we are all descended from ancestors who learned to be survivors. Every single person who is now alive comes from a genetic lineage in which every ancestor from time immemorial not only survived, but gave birth to new life: humans who became stronger and smarter for generation after generation, ending with <i>you.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">How? With survival skills embedded deeply in the most primitive part of the brain. We’re <i>built</i> for it—hardwired for hard times. We’re endowed with genetic coding that urges us to put our own needs first, to seize power in each moment, and to remember insults and threats. That’s the function of the noisy mind.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But over the millennia, we learned to override this genetic coding and organize our actions around the needs of <i>others—</i>an act that, paradoxically, provides the ultimate protection. That sense of social responsibility is a function of the quiet mind, which resides in the brain’s most advanced area—the forebrain, near the forehead—and governs all rational thought.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The more primitive noisy mind is dominated by the very back of the brain, behind the forebrain and the mammalian brain, in the area known as the reptilian brain. It sits just above the spinal column, and mostly just handles automatic action and fear. It does very little <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_215" title="215"/>thinking, but lots of neural housekeeping, like breathing and heartbeat. But those can be handy, too.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Virtually every element of trust is guided by the quiet mind, because the act of trusting people is—<i>or should be—</i>a thoughtful process. People who feel safe enough to assign trust rationally—and therefore effectively—have the ability to see others the way they are, rather than the way they <i>want</i> them to be. When you see people the way they are, you can see <i>yourself</i> the way <i>they</i> see you. When that happens, your assessment of their behavior becomes flawless—perfectly predictable—and together you can achieve great things.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The opposite outlook, driven by the fear found in the noisy mind, is animated by instinct and impulse, and is responsible for greed, compulsion, envy, and insecurity.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The survivor-based mindset is especially hard for type-A people to avoid, and they tend to become immune to the havoc they create, and the harm they do to themselves in the name of advancement. They sometimes dominate entire societies, but still do harm.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Ironically, one manifestation of the noisy mind—a fear-based feeling of unchecked egotism, and the choices made in its wake—is often a direct result of success. This subversive trait can be very hard to avoid. It’s common for people to be blind to their own arrogance. The first sign they see often comes when others pull away. But by then it’s often too late to repair the relationships.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Another unfortunate product of the noisy mind that’s hard to avoid is the destructive habit of assigning negative labels to the person you’re trying to size up. To predict people accurately, you can’t even <i>think</i> in terms of someone being a liar, a manipulator, a cheat, or a betrayer. Most people have an uncanny ability to know when they’re being judged, even when no judgments are voiced. These judgments, besides alienating people, can actually spur people to <i>adopt</i> the negative behaviors that they are suspected of having. When derogatory categories are assigned to people, I can’t always tell what they will do, but I can predict a negative response with almost 100 percent accuracy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Another failure of the noisy mind is to trust too much or too little. <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_216" title="216"/>Both behaviors stem from the single most common destructive force in business and in life: fear. People trust too much because they’re apprehensive about facing challenges alone, and people are too wary of trust because they’re worried that others will take advantage.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Most incorrect choices in predicting trust in someone are due to their <i>fear—</i>of not having enough or not being good enough—even if only in fear’s familiar disguises of greed, vanity, envy, authoritarianism, anger, insecurity, and perfectionism. One of the most common ways fear asserts itself is in anxiety about rejection from one’s own coworkers, supervisors, and even family and friends—in effect, one’s “tribe.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Despite the power of the noisy mind, it can generally be overridden by the quiet mind, which has a multitude of fear-fighting factors. To state the obvious, the quiet mind is smarter than the noisy mind.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The outlook of the quiet mind is characterized by calmness and confidence, with the ability to see things realistically, and the discipline to avoid self-deceit, with its many traps, including unrealistic optimism.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The quieter your brain becomes, the more easily you’ll recognize opportunities to move your goals forward in a seamless and easy manner.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Achieving this mindset is one of the great challenges of predicting behavior, and requires people to lead themselves to it. That’s why another phrase I use to describe the actions of the quiet mind is “self-leadership.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Like other primary aspects of personality, the quiet mind is individualized and self-sponsored, and can’t be created by other people unless you offer them your consent and active participation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When you are assessing people for their most deep-seated behaviors, it’s very important to look for the people who have the qualities of the quiet mind.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But nobody is perfect, and people should not be distrusted or disliked just because they’re naturally pessimistic, insecure, or depressed—especially if they make an honest effort to refrain from acting out their troubled feelings on <i>you.</i> When they do have moderate emotional flaws—as <i>so many</i> of us do—you can still trust them, by establishing a baseline of their behavior, and being alert for deviations from that norm. For example, if you propose an idea to someone who is typically pessimistic, it’s <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_217" title="217"/>not necessarily a problem if they’re pessimistic about your idea. It’s just how they are, and they may have some interesting things to say about the idea, even if it’s limited to potential problems.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Emotional stability exists on a continuum, and most people are adequately stable, while others have abundant emotional stability, characterized by self-control, consistency, communication skills, empathy, and the golden, self-sustaining quality of empathy that’s combined with stoical acceptance, which I call stempathy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Paradoxically, however, many people in our current compartmentalized, mechanized culture—particularly in the technological sectors—are skewed toward <i>overreliance</i> on rationality and emotional control, and have difficulty operating outside their narrow emotional bandwidth.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But who knows which of them will become the next dot-com billionaire? And whoever that may be will probably find that people are quite willing to talk to them, about whatever they want.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">TEN NEGATIVE TELLS FOR EMOTIONAL STABILITY</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People learn to be helpless. </span>We’re all born helpless, but some people seem to get <i>better</i> at it as they age. At first, it’s not because they want to, but because they get stuck in insoluble situations that they seemingly have no control over. Most people keep trying—but not everybody.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Some people respond to this as if it’s a valid life lesson: You’re helpless—in <i>every</i> situation!</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">That conclusion may seem ridiculous, but it’s very common, and results in a trait called “learned helplessness.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The desire among people with learned helplessness to give up in difficult situations can become a deeply held trait. It’s so instinctual that it even occurs in animals.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. People surrender their right to positive perceptions. </span>People can be born with mood chemistry that’s so skewed that it darkens their perceptions.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_218" title="218"/>Trauma can also create mood disorders, including depression and anxiety.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But in many cases among people who are consistently negative, there is no organic cause of a mood disorder, nor evidence of significant trauma.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It appears to simply be a choice.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It’s a bad choice—and often a lazy one—and with persistent freezing behavior—also called “dissociation”—the choice can become as immovable as an iceberg.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When someone perceives life as inherently negative, they are hard to predict, because they’ve usually lost touch with their goals. They’re like the <i>Titanic</i> (a metaphor I keep coming back to): big ships with tiny rudders. It’s particularly dangerous in iceberg country.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When they don’t know what they want, you don’t know, either, and then it becomes very hard to trust them, vest in them, or perceive a long relationship.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. People catastrophize. </span>Some people have an extremely low tolerance for problems, and have a hard time sorting big problems from little ones. To them, almost every problem looks like a fearful catastrophe.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Their expectations have a way of fulfilling themselves, especially when other people are involved, since fear is one of the most contagious of all emotions. It is very difficult to trust people who think every little thing is a disaster. They’re out of control, and totally unpredictable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Even so, you can establish their negative attitude as their baseline behavior, and then interpret their reactions accordingly. When you accept the stoic reality that most things simply <i>are—</i>without judging them as good or bad—you can often deal successfully with damaged people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People show signs of the “3-P” personality.</span> It harbors three toxic traits: permanence, pervasiveness, and personalization.</p>
