			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><b><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_219" title="219"/>Permanence</b> is thinking that today’s problem will be there forever. These people deserve kindness, but kindness need not translate into trust and partnership. So be kind, but beware.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><b>Pervasiveness</b> is thinking that a problem in one part of your life is sure to infect other parts. But that usually doesn’t happen, so if you can’t talk somebody out of it, don’t trust them with something important—or you may become their <i>next</i> problem.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><b>Personalization</b> is assuming that every problem is your own fault—even the weather, as in: “I should have known it would rain. When will I ever learn?” Don’t get sucked into this vortex of negative self-absorption. Try to steer clear of anybody who has a full-blown 3-P personality.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But you can help people, as I mentioned, to go sane. That’s achieved through kindness, patience, practical help, insight, and the tough-love quality of empathy plus stoicism that I call stempathy. However, if somebody has multiple, connected, self-destructive instabilities, the smart thing to do is to limit your interaction with them in serious matters.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People perceive themselves as victims. </span>I’m not one of those people who deny the existence of victimization. It’s all around us.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Even so, countless people habitually feel sorry for themselves, despite only <i>mild</i> suffering. It’s based in fear, and can limit the lives of not only the self-professed victims, but those who try to help them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">That’s not hard-hearted. It’s rational—and it’s the best way to have the energy to help the people who really need it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. People have a sense of entitlement. </span>Keep your eyes open, especially at work, for people who seem <i>too</i> carefree, have time to waste, and don’t get much done. They are the entitled.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They may be amusing and charming, because those qualities come easily to the well rested, but every day that you get stronger, they get weaker.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_220" title="220"/>Affiliation, respect, and trust belong to those who have something to offer, or it’s not win-win—and if it’s not win-win, it won’t last.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. People wait to be rescued. </span>This is another toxic behavior of the modern era. Self-reliance is out of style.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Overdependence upon others is contrary to <i>human</i> nature, and the very laws of nature itself.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">All creatures are provided with almost every resource they need, but the resources that nature offers must be gleaned, developed, divided, and stored. There’s no way around it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The demand for emotional rescue is even more common than the demand for physical rescue, and is just as insidious.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The hidden tragedy is that the rescuer is almost always overly idealized, and is expected to keep it up. If you don’t, you’ll be the new villain!</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. People think blame is constructive, because it exposes the weak links, and keeps people on their toes.</span></p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But it’s really the ugly flip side of rescue. It’s a way of finding another person to take responsibility for what happens to you.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Start with the fact that whoever screwed up knows it, and feels worse than anyone.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Most people realize that scapegoating is wrong, but fail to see that finding the “right” person to punish is quite similar. Efficient functioning depends solely upon <i>getting the job done,</i> not singling out the person who failed. It’s only good for venting, feeling superior, creating grudges, generating fear, and encouraging people to lie.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Don’t ally yourself with somebody who thinks blame is an efficient mechanism for optimal function. You could be next.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. People are volatile. </span>But they’re not stupid, so sometimes they’ll leaven their angry volatility with something positive, but equally <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_221" title="221"/>inappropriate. They’ll celebrate a moderate victory as if it’s world-changing, or they’ll promote somebody in a fit of grandiose generosity. From their perspective, they’re exuberant, ultratransparent, and spontaneous.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They’re actually just emotionally unstable, and get off on inflicting their mood swings and psychological imbalance on the people around them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It enables them to retain their narcissistic position as the center of attention.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But if you like walking on eggs, it’s fine.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. People are manipulators. </span>If they’re good manipulators, you won’t even notice until it’s too late. They’ll treat you great, then grab something you deserve, leave you out of an alliance, or just make you look bad.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">If they’re really good, you won’t have a single opportunity to correct the damage they’ve done to you.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Manipulation is the antithesis of productive, positive behavior. In a C-suite setting, it’s the pure essence of the power paradox.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Their ultimate goal may be to provoke you, but there is one thing they can’t control: your reaction.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">You can learn about the dark side of human behavior from them, but stay as far away as possible.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">August 2012<br/>Linda: Trusted Associate</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">“Two weeks left and I’m outta here!” Linda said. “That’s the bad news—for you. <i>I’m</i> okay with it!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda was funny. And a great FBI agent. But now I was going to lose her, because she’d landed a teaching job at a prestigious California university. Made me sad. She was supplying good information on her Russian target, and we’d both learned from each other.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_222" title="222"/>She took me deeper into behavioral psychology than any of my course instructors had, and it gave me a theoretical foundation for the techniques I now apply in the real world. I’d taught her to distrust the noisy-mind messages that she’d once considered a key element of critical thinking.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For about a year, we’d gotten together or talked on the phone every couple of weeks, and she’d given me everything she had on a small coterie of Russians, through her CHS named Susan, the woman who knew the Iraqi dentist. Susan saw most of them almost every week, usually at the ongoing lectures at Fordham.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It looked to me as if Linda’s original target, as I’d suspected, was spreading disinformation about the protests in Russia, and the victims of Putin’s police state. I was noticing a lot of articles, news conferences, think tank reports, news clips, and print ads about the Russian campaign against “domestic terrorists.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The name of the diplomat—Adrik Petrov, let’s say—was never attached to any of the releases about Chechnya, but it all originated in the New York Russian consulate, where he worked.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We had determined that Adrik had filled the slot of a diplomat who had clandestinely worked in intelligence, and Linda had discovered that he’d also replaced a spy in London several years prior. Adrik didn’t tell her the guy was a spy, but he’d mentioned the name and we’d confirmed his role, so I wanted to get as much as I could.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But now she was leaving.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Gonna miss you,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Me, too. I owe you one. I was seriously down on myself when we met. So many pressures to act a certain way, and say the right things. It carried over into my whole life. But you showed me I could act like myself, and if somebody had a problem, it was <i>their</i> problem.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Thanks,” I said. “But I just saw what was there already. It’s <i>you</i>. <i>You </i>did it.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Still owe you one. And here it is. I’ve got an idea,” Linda said. “I’ll have Susan launch it. She’s leaving town, too—for real. She’ll tell Adrik that she’s met somebody—a guy who works for investment bankers in <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_223" title="223"/>Eastern Europe—who’s looking for ground truth for investors, and that she’s been writing research reports for them. The ‘investor’ will be one of my people—or <i>you</i>, if you’d prefer.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Don’t say ‘ground truth,’” I said. “It’s spy-talk.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Okay, ‘inside information.’ Susan will tell Adrik she’s leaving town and needs to have somebody cover for her during the next few months. She’ll ask Adrik if he’ll do it, as a favor to her. They’re pretty tight. Susan likes him. Doesn’t trust him, but likes him. She’ll tell him that it’s a really good gig, with big money floating around. He’ll see it as a chance to grab more information. Susan told me he’ll probably do it anyway, because he’s a nice guy.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda said other complimentary things about him, and it worried me a little, because her descriptions of him didn’t fit the profile of a spy. For one thing, Russians like their spies to be extreme risk takers, because they want them to <i>take extreme risks</i>. The FBI has the opposite perspective. They don’t like risk takers, since they’re too likely to flame out. Adrik was more like an FBI guy. Linda thought he was just conflict-avoidant.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">She also said the only thing he didn’t like about being a diplomat was the “importance” of the job, which she interpreted as “power.” She said he worried about not giving his aides and office staff enough of what they needed, and that once he’d had to fire a guy and had almost quit himself.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I thought about Linda’s idea for minute. “You’re good,” I told her.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I know.” She was matter-of-fact, not boastful.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Three weeks later, I was in an overpriced steak house on the East Side, not too far from the United Nations, with Adrik Petrov.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Adrik was about my age, was thin and fit, and he seemed to be observant, glancing around the restaurant in the seemingly distracted but very focused manner of a Secret Service agent, or personal bodyguard. He was wearing a black suit and white shirt, and I was impressed by its simplicity. We were in a style-heavy neighborhood that was often <i>too</i> heavy. I was dressed better than usual, because I was supposed to be one of the rich guys who wanted inside information.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_224" title="224"/>After we ordered, I tried to steer the conversation to the new wave of protests in Russia, but he was clearly uncomfortable. “I saw too much of conflict. In the streets,” he said. “When I was young. Those were bad days. It was after I got home from the Afghan War and the Soviet Union was dissolving. Life should have been good.” He was lost in thought, and suddenly seemed more interested in his steak. We stopped talking, and he seemed comfortable with silence.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He wasn’t as I’d imagined, and I could see why Susan had connected with him. Sometimes you just can’t get a fix on somebody until they’re sitting right in front of you, and then you can read them like a map. A word of advice: Don’t live in the e-world 24/7. Go face-to-face at every reasonable opportunity.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Susan said you have business interests in Eastern Europe,” Adrik said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I do. I need to be cautious about revealing what they don’t wish to be public, but they are substantial people with many interests.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Instead of following up on that, Adrik went back to the subject of Susan. That was interesting. Most people, when given the chance to talk about money or a mutual acquaintance, will talk about the money.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I realized he had no interest in doing business, but was just doing a favor for Susan.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I asked Adrik about his family, and he brightened. His two children, he said, were coming to New York for the remainder of his tour here. He didn’t mention anything about a wife.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I started talking about my own kids, and he seemed genuinely interested, but I wanted the focus to return to him.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It never did. That was all I got. Happens all the time.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We met a few more times, and he gave me some written information that I requested. It was very precise, and quickly worked its way up the Bureau hierarchy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It got me noticed, and put the BAP in a favorable light.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Not every relationship leads to glory, but in the end, those that do and those that don’t can be hard to tell apart.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_225" title="225"/>TEN POSITIVE TELLS FOR EMOTIONAL STABILITY</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">1. People show an abundance of appreciation. </span>If the two most primal emotions are love and fear—with love leading the way to emotional stability—appreciation is pure elixir of emotional stability, because it is the finest form of love. It is the outward-flowing, self-perpetuating aspect of love that gives everything, and asks for nothing.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">As such, it is the single most potent force against fear—and fear is the worst enemy of emotional stability.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When someone is in a state of appreciation, they are literally incapable of feeling fear. The two states are mutually exclusive.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Appreciation makes not only the giver, but also the recipient, far more capable of attaining the courage that creates bold visions and life-changing relationships.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">2. People are hard to scare. </span>Fearlessness is the single most natural companion of appreciation, and is indispensable for people who are trying to inspire your trust.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Fearlessness is even greater than the very similar quality of courage, because courage is the ability to <i>overcome</i> fear, while fearlessness indicates an <i>absence</i> of fear.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The condition of fearlessness cannot be constant, because fear is not only endemic to the human race, but also indispensable.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">But a little fear goes a long way.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Fearless people are usually fun and effective, and can be genuinely inspiring.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">In an age of constantly churning crises, fearless people rise above the fray and are virtually immune to one of the great saboteurs of trust: desperation.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">3. People are impeccably rational.</span> Rationality is in shockingly short supply in an era dominated by emotion. Our society is now <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_226" title="226"/>almost bereft of the once common checks and balances of an unbiased media, and a dedication to civil discourse, and that has taken a terrible toll on rationality.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Those who treasure it, though, have a big head start on being trustworthy, because it’s easy to predict what they’ll do in almost any rational scenario. Rationality is governed by <i>rules</i>: very old rules, which have the same basic application now as they did eons ago.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Rationality, like love, is also one of our strongest forces against fear, since fear is an emotion that arises from the most primitive part of the brain.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">The most incredible “wiring” in the human brain dictates that 95 percent of all incoming information goes straight to the brain’s most advanced portion—the forebrain—and only then is shipped to the fear-driven part of the brain.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Many people don’t take advantage of that gorgeous work of wiring. And then they wonder why they’re regarded as a loose cannon, with very few allies.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">4. People adhere to the Code of Trust. </span>Even if they’ve never heard of it, they have intuitively adopted its five principles as a practical and ethical part of their behavioral code. When you find somebody who does that, you’re good to go: They’re among the easiest people in the world to trust.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They’ve put a lid on their ego. They validate people by taking the time to understand them, by finding some common ground. They don’t judge people, so they get to hear almost everything. They’re reasonable and rational, which makes them predictable, even in the worst crisis. And they’re generous enough to always go win-win.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Use that decoding method, and you’ll never fail to meet the right people.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">5. People offer you choices. </span>What’s not to trust? It’s your choice, so it’s hard to go wrong.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_227" title="227"/>Having choice is what freedom is all about. It feels good, and it works even better.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Having limited choices feels like being in jail, and can come from something as common as just being micromanaged.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Self-autonomy is the path to self-esteem.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">When people offer that to you, you feel like you live in <i>your</i> world—the one you’ve always wanted, where you’re in charge—and you’re in the company of the people who helped make it happen.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">6. People are happy with themselves.</span> That allows them to be happy with you. Nothing is worse than a relationship with people who don’t like themselves. Sooner or later, they’ll decide they don’t like you, either.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It’s not so easy to be happy with yourself. There are too many people who enjoy being critical of you. And there are also innumerable people in business, government, and culture who want you to feel inadequate, and in need of what they provide.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People who aren’t satisfied with themselves are hard to satisfy. They undermine your efforts, even at the expense of their own. That creates classic cases of people who don’t work in their own best interests. It makes them impossible to predict, and therefore impossible to trust as an ally.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People who aren’t happy with themselves tend to be unemotional, negative, and critical, and they even direct a lot of their own sour attitude toward themselves.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">People who like themselves tend to be pleasant, accepting, funny, and healthy. You know what they like and where they’re headed, so it’s easy to trust them, and build great alliances.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">7. People have power, but don’t love it.</span> It’s easy for trustworthy, centered people to rise to positions of power, because they’re magnets for other positive people, and work with diligence and self-responsibility.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_228" title="228"/>But they take little pleasure, if any, in the power they have over other people. Because of their suspension of ego, they don’t pat themselves on the back for being able to tell other people what to do. For truly sane people, power is nothing more than a lot of work.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Why? Because they care about people, try to be fair, and like to serve as a resource for the success of others.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">They may have a corner office, but many of them speak nostalgically of the days when they had fewer management responsibilities, and more time in the field.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">These people are extraordinarily valuable as a force against the power paradox. For the power-paradox people, just <i>trying</i> to reach for power can doom their dream, because we all have a natural tendency to distrust someone who loves power.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Those who are truly worthy of power don’t pursue it. It pursues them.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">8. People are flexible. </span>Life changes, and if you don’t change with it, you get left behind. You’ll pay for your rigidity with anxiety, dissociation, depression, and various practical penalties, such as loss of income, or outdated skills. All of this can quickly add up to desperation.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Because most desperate people will do almost anything to escape their peril, they’re hard to predict.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Flexible people feel as if life has innumerable opportunities, so they’re eager to accept new people, ideas, and places into their lives. They’re open and reasonable with those who reach out to them, and see change as an opportunity, instead of a threat.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">9. People are calm. </span>This trait reflects fairness, friendliness, optimism, and generosity. As a rule, people like this are successful, because they’re great in a crisis—and even better at not creating crises.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">An old cliché is, “The bigger they are, the nicer they are.” But <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_229" title="229"/>behavioral studies show that this variation of that theme is also true: “The nicer they are, the bigger they are.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">One study showed that “givers” are more likely to succeed than “takers.”</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Calm people function more effectively than hyperactive people, and are much easier to work with.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Part of their relaxed attitude comes from having no enemies, because they’re nice to people, and attract trust.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">It’s good that we’re motivated to trust nice people. It makes life feel far more sensible.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-FL"><span class="Callout">10. People don’t look for problems. </span>They don’t need to. They don’t feed on the drama that manipulators like, or play games of power.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">Emotionally stable people look for the best in others, so they’re usually the first to find it.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text">A businessperson I know who depends upon internet research in his job always starts his search by looking at sites that tell him what he wants to hear. That’s not where the research ends, of course, but by starting with a search for good news, he remains optimistic, as he finds supporting information. Only then does he look for problems. If he started from the negative end, he says, he’d probably give up before he got to the bottom of the situation.</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-Last">One interesting study about optimism in the workplace showed that optimistic people process information more effectively than people who feel negative or neutral, theoretically because a troubled mind is a greater distraction than a positive one.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">Welcome to the Bureau!</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Okay, I know you’ve figured out the anecdote in this chapter.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You know who I trusted, who I didn’t, and why. You’ve learned to think like an agent.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_230" title="230"/>You ignored the distractions caused by emotion.</p>
			<ul>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted-First">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted-First">You were brutally honest.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted">You didn’t trust people just because you liked them.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted">You resisted manipulation.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted">You forgot about your own political leanings.</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x07-List-Bulleted-Last">
					<p class="x07-List-Bulleted-Last">You read between the lines.</p>
				</li>
			</ul>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">But let’s honor the process and break it down.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Adrik: What a nice guy! He turned out to be a straight-up diplomat, with nothing on his record but honor. You trusted him, Susan <i>almost</i> trusted him, and Linda kept an open mind, which was one of her greatest assets.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I finally did, too. We all came to that conclusion <i>in</i> <i>spite</i> <i>of</i> the fact that he was a nice guy.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We had to be tough, right? What kind of people would we be if we trusted someone just because he was nice, and allowed him to continue to be complicit in a propaganda program that tried to legitimize false imprisonment, torture, and occasional murder? The circumstantial case against Adrik—that he replaced spies in two different embassies, and was peripherally associated with a propaganda program—made him a person of interest, but that’s all. He was a regular diplomat with nothing on his record but honor.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Let’s look at his numbers. He was obviously a hard guy to scare (Positive Tell #2), because he’d fought in the plains of Afghanistan and served in a government that too often turned on its own.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">He’d also seen enough of war to make him <i>rational</i> about it in the way that only those who have served can understand (Positive Tell #3), while millions of others who’ve never been near war see it almost as a spectacle, or even a sport.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even before I met Adrik, I’d learned from Linda that he accepted power only as a burden (Positive Tell #7), that he was conflict-avoidant (Positive Tell #10), and that he was generous enough to work with her until she’d make the move to California (Positive Tell #4).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_231" title="231"/>From the first time I met him, I could see that he had a very calm, nonvolatile personality (Positive Tell #9). I also noticed that he had no problem with what many people perceive as awkward silences during our conversations. It’s been my observation that people who are like that feel very comfortable with themselves, and people who like themselves <i>the way they are</i> tend to stay that way, and are easy to predict (Positive Tell #6).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">From the several other times I saw him, I never perceived even <i>one</i> of the negative tells of trust.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">And you trusted Susan, didn’t you? Hell yeah! You didn’t get many details about her, but what you saw was a true patriot who operated efficiently, with nothing to gain for herself.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Linda: She’s a little more complex. At first, when she seemed to be having an emotional crisis, we saw signs of learned helplessness (Negative Tell #1), the desire to be rescued (Negative Tell #7), and a propensity for catastrophizing (Negative Tell #3). I didn’t trust her early on. If I had, I wouldn’t have gotten as involved as I did. But when I offered help, stempathy, and patience, she practically became a different person. <i>Sometimes</i><i> that’s all it takes!</i> So remember that when you run into people you don’t initially trust. Over time, as I soon predicted, she became calm (Positive Tell #9), happy with herself (Positive Tell #6), and rational (Positive Tell #3).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You probably trusted the dentist, too—despite his connection with Saddam Hussein—because you didn’t have enough hard data to indicate that he was a willing accomplice to crime. All you knew was that he’d escaped from that situation at the first opportunity. So that act alone—a reflection of Sign #4: Actions—was sufficiently redemptive to trigger a tentative level of trust, especially since it was reinforced by positive, transparent communication (Sign #5: Language).</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Trust the dentist’s wife? Not so much. You undoubtedly observed her almost constant state of desperation—the “kryptonite” of trust—which was not consistent with <i>any</i> of her stressors of the moment. She seemed like the kind of person who would find a way to screw herself up. But you had plenty of data indicating that she’d been through <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_232" title="232"/>quite an ordeal, and maybe was still healing. So you probably thought her PTSD was understandable, and predictable. She would have been scarier if she’d acted like Mrs. Perfect.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL"><b>Now: Can you imagine trying to size these people up without my behavior assessment system?</b></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I can’t. I often wonder how I ever evaluated anybody with just the tools of conventional decision making: intuition, open-source research, legwork, libraries, and the opinions of various people, along with other surface-level information—some of which is often falsified, as so much is these days.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Around the time I met these people, I was applying my system on a virtually daily basis, and it became vastly easier to size people up and predict what they’d do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As more operatives were neutralized, Americans gradually stopped hearing—and believing—that Russia’s barbarism was a heroic battle against terrorists.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So hopefully the system helped to indirectly save some lives, at least, or stop some atrocities. But we’ll never know. Fine with me. All that matters is that it stopped.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For the next six years, I became increasingly involved with the science and application of understanding people, and accurately predicting what they would do.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It gave meaning to my life in the FBI, and to the new life I began after I retired from the Bureau in 2018.</p>
			<hr class="transition"/>
			<div aria-hidden="true" class="x04-Space-Break-Orn">•   •   •</div>
			<p class="x04-Space-Break-FL">If the American public becomes interested in this rational way of evaluating other people, I think it will change the country. Or certain segments. Who knows?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If it ever happens, maybe you’ll be part of it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">In this newly blossoming millennium, invigorated and informed by the new wave of people who feel most at home in history’s newest era, <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_233" title="233"/>you will search for the allies you need—protected by this system—as you offer prosperity and power to all who join you.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The search will enable you to <i>build your own life—</i>free from devastation, if you fall—and then <i>build it again</i>, as the positive people around you grow wiser, happier, more abundant, and closer, in a journey of humankind that will, with great good fortune, never end.</p>
			<aside>
				<h3 class="x10-Sidebar-Title">DEBRIEFING</h3>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-A">CHAPTER 8:<br/>“SEE INSIDE PEOPLE”</p>
				<div class="border"/>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">SIGN #6: STABILITY</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Quote:</b> “Most incorrect choices in predicting trust in someone are due to their <i>fear—</i>of not having enough or not being good enough—even if only in fear’s familiar disguises of greed, vanity, envy, authoritarianism, anger, insecurity, and perfectionism.”</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Body-Text-FL"><b>Key Message:</b> A lack of emotional stability is arguably the most devastating deficit in all of the six signs—because if somebody strays into emotional self-destruction just long enough to <i>stop pursuing their own best interests,</i> they’re suddenly a loose cannon, and will be far harder to predict.</p>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-Head-B">THE TAKEAWAYS</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First"><b>Unmanageable problems:</b> Parts of our lives are often temporarily dominated by people who have problems they can’t handle. These people act out their own issues in the public arena, and make <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_234" title="234"/>life more challenging for everyone. But they are very much in the minority, and their troubling actions are usually transparent.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><b>Emotional instability:</b> The two most common sources of emotional instability are biochemical imbalances and prior trauma. Both can be overcome, often with the help of other people. The help transforms what once was pain into a new level of love, both for the person who was helped and for the helper.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last"><b>Embracing imperfections:</b> When people realize that they’re good enough as they are, they usually see that they’re even better than they need to be. One last, ironic note to perfectionists: Don’t worry about being perfect. Worry about being <i>good enough.</i> That does not mean being <i>just okay.</i> It means reaching a <i>high level of conduct and insight.</i> And there’s even a good chance you’ve already done it.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Ten Positive Tells for Emotional Stability</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They’re very appreciative. Appreciation is the purest form of love, because it gives everything and asks nothing.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re hard to scare. They have almost no free-floating anxiety that needs to be attached to some event, so they’re on an even keel, even when things get hairy.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re impeccably rational. Rationality is arguably the most underrated of all virtues.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They follow the Code of Trust. They’re humble, nonjudgmental, rational, understanding, and generous, and these traits usually grow more vibrant as they grow older.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They offer you choices. Nobody likes to be ordered around, and people who know that get far <i>more</i> help and <i>more</i> kindness from others than those who insist on creating the illusion of control.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re happy with themselves. So they’re happy with you. When that happens, you can be yourself. When you’re happy with yourself, you’re happy with them, and the circle of contentment can begin again.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_235" title="235"/>They don’t relish the power they have. It’s a burden to them, because they want to use it fairly and wisely. That’s why people <i>want</i> them to have it.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re flexible. Life is one long process of letting go. Some people see that as loss. Others see it as a constant source of rebirth and re-creation.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They’re calm. They make sure that the quiet mind prevails over the noisy mind.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They don’t <i>look</i> for problems, or act them out with others.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
				<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Head">Ten Negative Tells for Emotional Stability</p>
				<ol>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-First">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-First">They learn to be helpless, and use it to manipulate.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They surrender their right to positive perceptions, without experiencing significant precursors, including biochemical imbalances or trauma.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They catastrophize, by exaggerating their problems and getting caught in the dissociative “freeze” condition of the fight-flight-freeze response.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They have a 3-P personality and believe that 1) every problem will last forever (permanence), 2) individual problems will spread to other parts of their lives (pervasiveness), and 3) they’re personally at fault for every problem they have (personalization).</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They fall prey to victimization, automatically labeling themselves as victims for most of the things in their lives that don’t please them, no matter how minor.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They have a sense of entitlement. They think they’re better than other people, and have a right to seize a disproportionate share of resources.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They wait to be rescued. It’s easy, and it makes them feel good. If you offer to be their white knight, get used to it.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered">They blame others. It’s another one of those things that makes insecure people feel better, so they tell themselves that it’s for the good of everybody, including the person they blame.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_236" title="236"/>They’re volatile. One minute they’re over-the-top angry and the next they’re ridiculously positive. Either way, they’re the center of attention.</p>
					</li>
					<li class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered2-Last">
						<p class="x10-Sidebar-List-Numbered-Last">They’re manipulators. Instead of being forthright about what they want, they play mind games, and try to stay under the radar until all of the traps they set spring shut.</p>
					</li>
				</ol>
			</aside>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 16: Afterword ===

<div>
			<p class="x03-Chapter-Title-BRK" id="_idParaDest-32"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_237" title="237"/>AFTERWORD</p>
			<p class="x03-Chapter-Subtitle-2">The Golden Moments</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A-CO">Choice and Change</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">Life rarely travels in a straight, unbroken line. As a rule, life’s most important moments don’t even unfold in gradual twists and turns, but at abrupt right angles, created by the choices we make: to trust or doubt, to accept or reject, to go or stay, to love or fear. These are the moments that define us.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Each binary choice occurs at a ninety-degree intersection, or at least a fork in the road, and every one of them creates not only the utterly unique “you” in your life, but also the equally singular world in which you live.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Making a healthy, rational choice forges a golden moment in your life, and a long series of them—each building upon the others—can reward you with a veritable dream life: one of happiness, prosperity, love, health, and gratitude.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even so, for most people, this unrelenting procession of choices can be nerve-racking, overwhelming, and even paralyzing.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But for <i>you—</i>at this pivotal point in your exploration of ideas—it <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_238" title="238"/>probably isn’t. You’ve learned how to <i>predict the outcome</i> of your choices—rationally and dispassionately—with a simple, six-point system for sizing people up and enlisting them in your own self-selected future.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When your future is foreseeable, it’s easy to make these choices. You know what you’re doing and see where you’re going. Your life proceeds gracefully, with minimal melodrama and very little waste of time, money, worry, or regret.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Why do you have this special advantage? <i>Because you’ve learned to think like an FBI behavioral analyst.</i> You know who to trust, and why. It’s as if you can see inside people, knowing that what you see is what you get.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">At this point, you’re in the rare company of those who compose America’s largest and—in my opinion—finest domestic investigative agency. With logic, discipline, and information, you can spot flakes and phonies, defy the forces of manipulation, get to the bottom of complex questions, and find the kind of allies that will supercharge your life.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was a hard system to create, but as you’ve seen, it’s easy to operate, if you can just remember six words:</p>
			<p class="x09-Box-Text-List-Para-Head">THE SYSTEM FOR SIZING PEOPLE UP</p>
			<ol>
				<li class="x09-Box-List-Numbered-First">
					<p class="x09-Box-List-Numbered-First">Vesting</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">
					<p class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">Longevity</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">
					<p class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">Reliability</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">
					<p class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">Actions</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">
					<p class="x09-Box-List-Numbered">Language</p>
				</li>
				<li class="x09-Box-List-Numbered-Last">
					<p class="x09-Box-List-Numbered-Last">Stability</p>
				</li>
			</ol>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_239" title="239"/>This method of behavioral analysis—guided at each juncture by deductive reasoning—has freed you from the mind-numbing forces of emotion, distraction, trickery, coercion, false fears, false hopes, and desperation.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It’s enabled you to clearly see the world as it <i>is.</i> From this fully informed starting place, you’re in the perfect position to create not only your own life, but your <i>own world,</i> populated by your supporters, and customized to fit your own special abilities and desires.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You can’t always change the world, but you usually can change <i>your</i> world.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The people who share your worldview will be happy to help you succeed, because it’s in their best interests. They’ll stick with you for the long haul and will help you do the work that keeps your alliance strong. They’ll be competent and diligent, and will almost always know what to say—and what <i>not</i> to say. And they’ll have enough emotional stability to let you sleep well at night and awake to a morning of promise and pleasure.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As you’ve discovered, most of your allies won’t excel at all six of these behavioral cornerstones, but they don’t need to. Nobody’s perfect, but anybody who’s proficient in at least one or two of these psychosocial graces can learn the others, since they’re all so closely linked and synergistic.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">When some of your allies can’t be a resource for everything you need, other people will fill in for them. Platinum-level success doesn’t always take a village, but it certainly takes a team.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The main reason you’ll need a team is because <i>change</i> is a virtual <i>definition</i> of life (just as death is defined by immobility), and hitting a moving target can’t be done by the proverbial Army of One.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Sadly, though, the people who are trapped in their own <i>living</i> immobility often perceive change as nothing but <i>loss—</i>almost as if it’s a form of death. That’s not much of a leap, because the specter of death is the strongest and longest-held fear of most people, and it’s easy for these people to piggyback their living fears upon it.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">This unhealthy linkage is hard to break, because change and death share a formidable link: fear of the unknown.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_240" title="240"/>Tragically, when people make the transition from lifelong fear of change to the hush of the grave, it’s as if they had never lived on this earth at all.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Can there be a <i>better</i> reason to learn predictability and embrace change?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">There is—but only one: to luxuriate throughout life in an undulation of constantly cresting moments of gold.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But rising to that level and staying there isn’t easy, because—again—nobody’s perfect. Including me, of course.</p>
			<h3 class="x05-Head-A">January 7, 2019<br/>Quantico, Virginia<br/>Free at Last</h3>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text-FL">I didn’t feel like myself. Something was off, and I had a pretty good idea of what it was.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">After thirty consecutive years of serving America—in the Marine Corps and FBI—I was free. That was the problem. Or so I thought.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">This day—a Monday that was devoid of work, or even a bittersweet nostalgia for the weekend—should have felt like one of the best ever.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But I was sitting in a restaurant feeling gloomy, and waiting for my life to begin.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some people in that situation—newly retired, and on the cusp of creating another career—would refer to their future as their “new” life, but to me it felt so foreign that it was like my <i>next</i> life: as if the old one had been in another universe, long ago.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">So I was a little uneasy. The only certainty I had was that, from this point on, everything would look different. But, as you now know, I’m no longer afraid of change.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’m not even particularly afraid of death anymore, or the inevitable changes that precede it. To me, all of that is just a challenge. An adventure!</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But this would be my biggest change yet. And the finality of no <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_241" title="241"/>longer being a marine or an FBI agent carried consequences that were hard to predict. It was, to some extent, an exploratory voyage into the void.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Some people believe in a very literal afterlife of heavenly bliss, and some think they’ll rejoin the universe as something along the lines of a white light, or as another creature. Some believe in nothingness. What just about everybody agrees on, though, is that it’s going to be <i>way differ</i><i>ent.</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even so, while we’re still alive, we all experience versions of our existence that for all practical purposes are completely new incarnations.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The starkest rendition of this is the passage from infancy to childhood, when our first memories of life are essentially erased into oblivion, an occurrence that neurologists call infantile amnesia. For some people, everything before about age ten fades to nothingness.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">As life proceeds, this incremental loss continues, often quite consciously, as we abandon what we have to create a better version of what will be. With that loss—and the freedom that so often accompanies major change—old opinions, strategies, and alliances wither, just as surely as memories fade.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">For all intents and purposes, therefore, all of us have already died—and not entirely metaphorically. With each passing day, part of us is gone, while the rest—and hopefully the best—moves on.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d spent years developing that philosophy of choice and change, and I thought it would make retirement a cakewalk.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Okay, fine . . . So <i>why</i>, then, was I so uneasy?</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Was it just plain old lack of income? After all, money is the root of all freedom.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">If that sounds questionable, or even cynical, ask any retiree who’s sitting on a nice nest egg—or ask one who <i>isn’t.</i> Ask anybody who works two jobs just to survive. Don’t even bother asking the wealthiest one percent.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Money can’t buy happiness or love—but it can buy time. And free time, governed solely by oneself, offers the richness of choice that ultimately constitutes freedom.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_242" title="242"/>But not for me. I’m motivated far more by passion and patriotism than by money.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Maybe I missed the excitement of work. Sometimes my job was dicey and disturbing, but it never got boring.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I also might have been missing the loss of a palpable, immediate purpose. I was no longer a role, but just a man staring—without really seeing—into the void.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I did feel tired, not physically, but with a bone-deep psychic exhaustion from the endless effort of keeping other countries from ripping America apart—without us even knowing it—as we blamed each other for ugly things that hadn’t even happened.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Even so, here I was, suddenly free from the routines that had limited my life for as long as I could remember—feeling strangely stifled and restrained, and missing something I couldn’t name. <i>Why?</i></p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Robin!” It was Jesse Thorne.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Obi-Wan!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">After Jesse retired from his post in New York, he’d moved down here, close to Quantico, Virginia. Lately he’d become fascinated by my system for sizing people up. He’d inspired at least half of it himself, but had always been a seat-of-the-pants, intuitive agent who didn’t need a system. There are people like that, but I’ve only known a few.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I’d promised to lay the whole thing out for him when we both had time, and <i>now</i> that time was here.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The waiter brought out two beers: Goodwood Bourbon Barrel Stout, seasoned in whiskey barrels.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“So,” Jesse said. “Sizing people up: What’s the secret sauce?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You’ll think it’s too simple,” I said. “It’s just six things, and you know all of them.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Humor me.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Okay. Six signs.” I took my first sip of stout, with a sense of wonder and guilt about drinking before dark. “If any one sign obviously applies to somebody, you can usually trust them. But more is better. To spot the sign, you just look at people objectively and rationally—the same way agents do—looking for tells.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_243" title="243"/>“Tells like nonverbals? Intentions? Actions? Track record?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yep. The basic investigative elements. The most important is when somebody vests their interests in your success. I learned that from Leo.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Hell of a guy. On Putin’s hit list!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Leo lived and died by his relationships,” I said. “They were his money in the bank. He taught me that we’re all salesmen, and that the only thing we have to sell is ourselves. If somebody buys you, they’ll eventually buy what you’ve got. Leo was the one who introduced me to Sergei,” I said. “Remember him?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yeah, Russian diplomat. What made you trust Sergei?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Solid nonverbals, high emotional intelligence, good listener, looked for ways to expand the relationship, candid, transparent. Asked about my priorities. He didn’t promise me anything, but when you trust somebody, you don’t need promises. You can just let them be themselves—”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse finished my sentence: “And they’ll let you be you.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Old mentors never die; they just stay a step ahead.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“And Leo introduced me to Annan,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Annan! You served with him in World War III! Right? Except it was just the two of you.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“That’s my kind of war.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“The best thing in counterintelligence is what <i>doesn’t</i> happen.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Annan taught me the second sign of trust: thinking that a relationship will have longevity,” I said. “Right off the bat, he realized he and I could be a big help to each other on a lot of things over the years. So it was easy to trust him.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse took a long pull on his Bourbon Barrel Stout and said, “What’s the third sign?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Reliability—based on competence and diligence. I learned this one from what <i>not</i> to do, when I got hooked up with a drone team and our first job was organizing a conference.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I told him about George. “He’s a great guy,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“He is.” Jesse knew him from Quantico.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“But that was the wrong job for him. He’s good with tech, not people.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text"><span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_244" title="244"/>I felt an odd nostalgia for George and made a mental note to give him a call. I still had my own drone. Loved it. We could have fun.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“It’s odd,” Jesse said, “that the most important thing you did with Annan—defusing that nuclear standoff—was the <i>first</i> thing.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“On one level, it’s super weird,” I said. “But it didn’t feel like a new relationship. Annan ritualized relationships and made people feel like he’d been waiting his whole life to meet them.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“He’s still overseas?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yeah, he said he’d come back often. But he hasn’t.” Remembering that made the butterflies in my stomach come back.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You and Annan owe Jack Johnson on that one,” Jesse said. “Cowboy Jack! Never met a mission he didn’t love.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“We need more guys like him now,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Yeah, but maybe dialed <i>down</i> a little? You called in <i>your own lead </i>on that!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Okay, but I did it right in front of Jack. You said he had a clear pattern of that kind of thing. That’s the fourth sign: actions, or patterns of positive behavior.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Did you hear that Jack is sick?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I hadn’t. Jesse told me what it was, and it was bad.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I’ll go see him,” I said. I knew that was why he’d told me.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Sign five,” I said. “I call it the language of trust, because it’s derived from the Code of Trust, which you helped me create back in New York. It’s listening to how somebody talks, and looking for nonjudgmental, validating statements that empower people with options. If somebody does that naturally, you can count on them.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I love the Code,” he said. “I learned a lot from it.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“<i>You</i> learned from <i>me</i>?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“It happens. It’s like the Infinite Monkey Theorem: Give a million monkeys a million typewriters for a million years, and they’ll eventually write a book.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Or in my case, three, Obi-Wan.”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">I told Jesse that the fifth sign was the language of trust, but I didn’t mention that I’d recognized its importance from what Mr. X—the <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_245" title="245"/>Silicon Valley executive—<i>didn’t </i>say. Not out of animosity. As Mr. X revealed more and more of himself with his language, he failed to make much of an impression on me, as a person. People you don’t trust leave your life without a trace. And that’s a good thing.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Last sign,” I said, “and last call.” I signaled the waiter for another round of stout.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Sign six is emotional stability. I learned that one from a woman named Susan, who helped me with a Chechen War operation. I was still trying to save the whole world all at once, and she showed me how much can be done with a quiet mind. She’s in Asia now, working intelligence. Just had a baby.” That seemed important to mention. I was looking forward to some pictures.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Jesse was gazing at the ceiling, lost in thought. Then he looked at me and said, “I can predict this one. Your idea of emotional stability is somebody who hates drama, doesn’t enjoy power, listens well, is humble, and stays rational when everybody else is freaking out. Close enough?”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“You forgot Rule Number One,” I said.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“I <i>do</i> remember! I taught you that. Rule Number One: Don’t f**k yourself up!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">“Right! You said, ‘Leave that to other people! They’re not as good at it as you are, because everybody is their own best enemy!”</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">We clinked our glasses together for the last time. Then he left. I watched him walk to his car. When I was alone, I was expecting the sick, sad feeling to return. But it didn’t. I’d thought that the feeling had come from leaving my job. But it wasn’t about the work. Or the money. Or the excitement. Or the sense of purpose.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">My dread was letting go of the people—Leo, Annan, Jesse, Sergei, Jack, George, Susan, and so many others: the living and the lost.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">But just realizing why I felt empty was enough to make it go away. That’s the power of <i>reality</i>. It’s usually so much kinder than the imagination. Real life, even in dark days, is never as unbearable as it is in our nightmares and daydreams.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Reality can always be altered. The vast majority of problems—stripped <span epub:type="pagebreak" id="page_246" title="246"/>of the dread of worst-case scenarios—yield to teamwork, time, and logic. And memories alone can summon the lost.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">You may remember from chapter 2 that in the dark days after 9/11, a girl named Emily handed me a Gatorade and a note that said, “Thank you for saving America.” Back then, I’d felt nothing but shame, because I knew I couldn’t live up to that sentiment. But I’d promised myself I’d save one person. That was Leo. It wasn’t <i>enough</i> for me. Then. Looking back, I am in awe of all that I did not know.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Everyone who leaves a job loses people to time, distance, and eventually death. But my loss, I think, was even more poignant than the norm, because I came to my people with the mission of learning what lay within their souls. Once you look inside someone so deeply that you choose to trust them with your dreams, and accept their dreams as your own, a golden moment is born that outlives you both.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Trust lives inside that moment. When you offer to share your fate with someone through the linkage of trust—which can be even harder to offer than love—you’re sealed in a bond that enriches your life as long as it lasts, even after they’re gone.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">They don’t age, they don’t change, they don’t leave your heart, and you’re never the same, because part of them became you. And just as surely, part of you is inside them, and may someday inhabit the lives of their children and many others—scattered in time and place, but enduring still as fragments of your life begin again.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">It was still the same idle Monday afternoon, but I no longer felt empty. Immense changes lay ahead, but my fear of <i>loss</i> was gone.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">The past, the present, the success, the failure, the thrill, the work, the goals—all of that fades, like old pictures. One day they grow dark, and then brittle, and soon are gone.</p>
			<p class="x04-Body-Text">Everything fades but the people, and the relationships, as bonds grow golden, and life goes on.</p>
		</div>

=== CHAPTER 19: About the Authors ===

<h1 class="x13-BM-About-Au-Head">ABOUT THE AUTHORS</h1>
		<p class="x13-BM-About-Au">Robin Dreeke entered federal law enforcement in 1997, after graduating from the US Naval Academy and serving in the US Marine Corps. He received advanced training and operational experience in social psychology and in the practical application of the science of relationship development. Eventually he rose to direct the behavioral analysis program in a federal law enforcement agency. Dreeke recently retired as an agent of the FBI. He is the author of<i> It's Not All About "Me.</i>"</p>
		<p class="x13-BM-About-Au">Cameron Stauth is the author or co-author of more than twenty books, including several national and international bestsellers.</p>
